=====================================================================
MISSION ORDER FOR GEMINI: FLY8 STUDY, NP1-NP5 REVIEW-NOTE EXPANSION
(Philippine nursing licensure exam / PNLE review content) - PACK NP5
=====================================================================

READ THIS WHOLE ORDER FIRST. DO NOT START WORKING YET.
When you have read everything and opened the files in PART 2, reply ONLY with:
  1. the word READY,
  2. a 10-line-max summary of what you understood,
  3. your proposed batch plan (which file and which notes come first),
  4. any question you have.
Then STOP and WAIT. Do nothing else until I (the owner) type the single word: GO
After GO, do exactly ONE batch per reply (see PART 6), then stop and wait for me to type NEXT.
If at any point I type STOP, stop immediately.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 1. WHO YOU ARE AND WHAT THE PROJECT IS
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You are a research-and-writing agent working for the owner of "FLY8 Study",
a personal Android study app for the Philippine Nurse Licensure Examination
(PNLE). The owner is a nursing reviewee, not a programmer. Another AI (Claude)
builds and maintains the app; it will check and merge what you produce. You do
NOT edit the GitHub repository directly. You produce exact, ready-to-merge
JSON patches in your replies (format in PART 5). The owner pastes them back to
Claude.

The app holds about 974 short review notes. Each note is a JSON object inside
a "pack" file. Your job: EXPAND and STRENGTHEN the NP1 to NP5 notes so every
disease / condition / problem note follows one clinical structure, is
Philippine-based, is fact-checked, and carries real references.

The five PNLE areas used in this app (current understanding, verify if you can):
  NP1  Community Health Nursing (+ communicable diseases, PH health programs)
  NP2  Care of Healthy/At-Risk Mother and Child (OB, newborn, pediatrics, growth and development)
  NP3  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part A (cardio, respiratory, blood/hema, oncology,
       perioperative, fluids-electrolytes and renal, burns)
  NP4  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part B (GI, endocrine, neuro, eye and ear, ortho)
  NP5  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part C (psychiatric nursing, emergency, disaster, critical care)
  (Fundamentals, pharmacology, ethics/laws run through all five. They are NOT in scope for this mission.)


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 2. THE FILES (ALL INCLUDED BELOW IN THIS SAME DOCUMENT)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You do NOT need GitHub or any link. Everything you need is pasted at the end of this
document, between lines that start with "===== FILE:" and "===== END FILE".
This pack covers NP5 ONLY. Work only on the files in this pack, in this order:
  1. data/presets/np5-a.json
  2. data/presets/np5-b.json
  3. data/presets/np5-psych-c.json
  4. data/presets/np5-psych-d.json

Also included for context: data/presets/bundles.json (which subject each note belongs to).
Use the file path shown after "===== FILE:" as the "target" in your JSON patches.
Do not touch anything that is not in this pack. Other NP areas come in separate packs.
If a file below looks cut off or will not parse, say which one and STOP.

---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 3. WHAT A NOTE LOOKS LIKE TODAY (keep these fields)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Each note is a JSON object like this (shortened):
{
  "id": "np1-cd-dengue",
  "area": "NP1",
  "title": "Dengue Fever",
  "body": "## What it is\n...markdown...\n\n## Related topics\n[[Malaria]], [[Filariasis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n...",
  "tags": ["communicable disease", "..."],
  "priority": "high",
  "outlineRefs": ["..."],
  "aiWritten": true,
  "nurseReviewed": false
}
Rules for fields:
- NEVER change "id", "area", "outlineRefs". Keep "tags" (you may ADD tags, never remove).
- "priority" stays as is unless clearly wrong (then explain why in your batch notes).
- "aiWritten" must be true. "nurseReviewed" must stay false. You are not a nurse reviewer.
- "body" is markdown with only: "## " headings, paragraphs, "- " bullets, "1. " numbered lists,
  **bold**, *italic*, and [[Wikilinks]] to other note titles. No tables, no HTML, no images, no code fences.
- Keep [[Wikilinks]] that exist. Only add a [[Link]] if a note with that exact title exists in the packs.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 4. THE NEW STRUCTURE FOR EVERY DISEASE / CONDITION NOTE
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Rewrite the "body" of each disease, disorder or clinical-problem note into EXACTLY these sections,
in this order. Use plain, exam-ready wording. Short bullets. Philippine context everywhere it applies.

## What it is
  1-3 sentences: definition, who gets it, why it matters on the PNLE.

## Causative agent
  The organism (genus/species, type: virus/bacteria/parasite/fungus), or the cause/risk factors
  if not infectious (e.g. autoimmune, genetic, trauma). Include mode of transmission, incubation
  period and period of communicability for communicable diseases. Include the vector or reservoir
  common in the Philippines (e.g. Aedes aegypti, Anopheles flavirostris, Oncomelania snail).
  If there is no causative agent (e.g. a nursing concept), write "Not applicable" and the main risk factors.

## Main problem
  The core pathophysiology in 1-3 sentences: what goes wrong in the body (the "why" behind the signs).

## Initial manifestations
  The EARLY / first signs and symptoms (what the nurse sees first). Mark pathognomonic signs with
  "(pathognomonic)" only if a reference supports it.

## Other manifestations
  Later, severe or complicating signs; warning signs; danger signs that need referral.

## Laboratory and diagnostic data
  Key tests and the expected abnormal result (with normal ranges ONLY when a reference gives them,
  in SI or conventional units as used in the Philippines). Confirmatory test vs screening test.

## Nursing diagnoses
  2-5 priority nursing diagnoses in NANDA-I style (problem related to cause as evidenced by signs),
  ordered by priority (ABC, Maslow, safety). Mark the top one "(priority)".

## Nursing interventions
  Numbered, in priority order. Each line: the action, then " - " and a short rationale.
  Include: assessment first, safety, infection control/isolation type, positioning, fluids/nutrition,
  medications by CLASS (drug names only if the reference names them; DOSES ONLY if a cited
  reference states the dose, otherwise write "dose per physician order / current guideline"),
  health teaching, referral, and prevention.

## Philippine context
  DOH program or strategy that covers it (e.g. EPI, National TB Program DOTS, Dengue Prevention and
  Control Program, Rabies Prevention and Control Program, Integrated Management of Childhood Illness),
  relevant Republic Act or DOH Administrative Order (number + name, only if verified),
  local terms (Filipino names, e.g. "bulutong", "tigdas"), common local exam traps.

## Exam traps
  3-5 bullets: classic PNLE-style confusions and the correct thinking (e.g. "fever falls in the
  critical phase of dengue = danger, not improvement").

## Related topics
  Keep the existing [[Wikilinks]]; add only links to notes that exist.

## References
  2-5 numbered references you ACTUALLY opened and read. Format:
  1. Organization or author. Title. Year (or "accessed YYYY-MM-DD"). URL
  Each key fact you added must be supported by at least one reference in this list.

## Check against your textbook
  Keep the existing text, then add any fact you could NOT verify, written as
  "UNVERIFIED: ..." so the owner can check it in their textbook.

For NON-disease notes (programs, laws, concepts, procedures, growth and development stages,
therapeutic communication, etc.): do NOT force the disease sections. Use only the sections that
make sense (always keep "What it is", add "Philippine context", "Exam traps", "References",
"Check against your textbook"). Say in your batch notes which notes you treated as non-disease.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 5. VERIFY AND REFERENCE (SEARCH FOR THESE SOURCES)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You MUST search the web and verify facts. Prefer, in this order:
  1. Philippine Department of Health: https://doh.gov.ph (programs, Administrative Orders,
     clinical practice guidelines, disease fact sheets), DOH Epidemiology Bureau.
  2. Official Philippine law and issuances: https://www.officialgazette.gov.ph , https://lawphil.net
     (Republic Acts, e.g. RA 9173 Philippine Nursing Act of 2002; verify every RA number).
  3. Professional Regulation Commission (PRC): https://www.prc.gov.ph (PNLE scope / table of
     specifications if available).
  4. WHO and WHO Western Pacific Region: https://www.who.int , https://www.who.int/westernpacific
  5. Philippine medical societies' public guidelines (e.g. Philippine Society for Microbiology and
     Infectious Diseases, Philippine Pediatric Society, Philippine Heart Association), PhilHealth.
  6. US CDC / NIH MedlinePlus / StatPearls (NCBI Bookshelf) for pathophysiology and lab data.
  7. NANDA-I terminology for nursing diagnoses (name the diagnosis; do not paste copyrighted definitions).

Search queries you should use (adapt per topic):
  "<disease> DOH Philippines", "<disease> clinical practice guideline Philippines",
  "<disease> administrative order DOH", "<disease> WHO fact sheet", "<disease> StatPearls",
  "<program name> DOH manual of operations", "Republic Act <number> <title> lawphil".

Verification rules:
- Open every source you cite and confirm the fact is on that page. Never cite a page you did not open.
- Never invent a URL, a law number, a guideline year, a page number, an author, or a drug dose.
- If two good sources disagree, state both and which is current (newest DOH guidance wins for PH practice).
- If the existing note contains a fact that your sources contradict, FIX it and list the fix in your
  batch notes as "CORRECTED: old -> new (source #)".
- If you cannot verify something, keep it out of the main sections and put it under
  "Check against your textbook" as UNVERIFIED.
- Write in your own words. Do not copy paragraphs from sources or textbooks. Short facts and
  official names/terms are fine.
- NEVER use, copy or reconstruct real PNLE board questions or "recalled questions" sites.
  They are confidential/copyrighted. Do not write any test questions in this mission.
- No patient-specific medical advice. This is exam review content.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 6. HOW TO DELIVER (EXACT OUTPUT FORMAT, ONE BATCH PER REPLY)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Batch size: 5 notes per reply (fewer if the notes are long). Work file by file in the PART 2 order,
NP1 first. Inside a file, do disease/condition notes before non-disease notes.

Each reply after GO / NEXT must contain exactly these 3 parts:

(1) A heading line:  BATCH <n> - <file path> - notes <i>-<j> of <total in file>

(2) ONE fenced JSON code block (this is the patch; it must be valid JSON, nothing else inside it):
```json
{
  "target": "data/presets/np1-comm.json",
  "batch": 1,
  "ops": [
    {
      "op": "update",
      "id": "np1-cd-dengue",
      "note": { ...the FULL updated note object with all fields... }
    }
  ]
}
```
  - "op": "update" replaces the note with that id in the target file (send the FULL note, not a diff).
  - "op": "add" adds a NEW note (only if an important PNLE disease/condition is clearly missing from the
    NP area). New ids: "<area lowercase>-x-<short-slug>", e.g. "np1-x-leptospirosis". Check every pack
    first so you do not duplicate an existing note under another title. Put a new note in the pack file
    of the same subject. Mark it "priority": "medium" unless it is a core PNLE topic.
  - Escape newlines inside "body" as \n and quotes as \". The block must parse as JSON.

(3) "BATCH NOTES" in plain text, short:
  - which notes were treated as disease vs non-disease,
  - every CORRECTED item,
  - every UNVERIFIED item,
  - sources you could not open,
  - suggested new notes for later (title + why), without writing them yet.

Then stop and wait for NEXT. Do not start the next batch on your own.
If your reply would be too long, split the batch smaller. Never cut a JSON block in half.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 7. QUALITY BAR (CHECK BEFORE SENDING EACH BATCH)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] Every disease note has all sections of PART 4 in order (non-disease notes: the sensible subset).
[ ] Causative agent, main problem, initial and other manifestations, lab data, nursing diagnoses,
    interventions are present and specific (no filler like "monitor the patient" without what/why).
[ ] Philippine context is real (program names, RA/AO numbers verified, local terms).
[ ] Every reference was opened; URLs are real; each added fact traces to a reference.
[ ] No invented doses, laws, years, page numbers or URLs. Unverified items are labelled.
[ ] id/area/outlineRefs unchanged; aiWritten true; nurseReviewed false.
[ ] Body uses only allowed markdown (no tables/HTML/images/code).
[ ] The JSON block parses (no trailing commas, quotes escaped).
[ ] No PNLE board questions or recalled-question content anywhere.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 8. COMMANDS I (THE OWNER) WILL USE
---------------------------------------------------------------------
  GO          start BATCH 1
  NEXT        do the next batch
  REDO <n>    redo batch n with my comments
  SKIP        skip the current note and continue
  JUMP <file> move to another file in PART 2
  STATUS      tell me which file/notes are done and what is left (no new work)
  STOP        stop now

Remember: right now, only reply READY + summary + batch plan + questions, then WAIT for GO.

"title": "Psychiatric Nursing Overview",
"body": "## What it is\nA map of the psychiatric branch of NP5 (maladaptive patterns of behavior). Use it to find the right note and to see how the pieces fit.\nThe same map also tells you where the drug notes, substance notes and special therapies live, so you can jump straight to what a question is really testing: a communication choice, a safety decision, a symptom term or a drug point.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Part 1 is the foundation: how the nurse talks, builds a relationship, handles crisis, understands defenses, names symptoms and keeps everyone safe. See [[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Nurse-Patient Relationship Phases]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]], [[Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]].\n- Part 2 covers anxiety, trauma, eating, personality and substance disorders.\n- Part 3 covers the big mood and thought disorders and the delirium versus dementia contrast.\n- Psych Extra revisits personality clusters, drug classes, developmental disorders, Alzheimer's disease and substances.\n- Foundation topics beyond Part 1: [[Mental Health Concepts and History of Psychiatric Care]], [[Therapeutic Use of Self and Johari's Window]], [[Group, Milieu and Family Therapy]], [[Expressive and Insight Therapies]], [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]], [[Electroconvulsive Therapy]].\n- Communication detail: [[Therapeutic Communication Techniques with Examples]] and [[Non-Therapeutic Communication Blocks with Examples]].\n- Drug detail: [[Psychotropic Medication Classes: Which Drug for Which Problem]], [[Antipsychotic Anticholinergic Effects, Clozapine Alert and Client Teaching]], [[Extrapyramidal Symptoms and Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome]], [[Tricyclic and SSRI Antidepressants: Drug Lists and Nursing Care]], [[MAOIs and Other Antidepressants: Tyramine Diet and Special Risks]], [[Lithium Therapy and Toxicity]], [[Antianxiety Drugs: Benzodiazepines and Buspirone]].\n- Substance detail: one note per drug family, from [[Alcohol Intoxication and Long-Term Physical Effects]] to [[Opioids: Intoxication, Withdrawal and Naloxone]].\n- Childhood and special groups: [[Autism Spectrum Disorder]], [[Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder]], [[Intellectual Disability]].\n- Mental health is not just the absence of illness: autonomy, self-esteem, reality orientation, stress management and tolerance of uncertainty are the traits listed as healthy.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety of the client and others comes first in every psychiatric question (Maslow: physiologic needs, then safety).\n2. Assess before intervening, especially suicide and violence risk.\n3. Use the least restrictive effective measure.\n4. Then build the relationship and teach.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Stem names a behavior or symptom: first decide whether anyone is in danger now (suicide, violence, command hallucinations, airway after a drug or ECT). Danger beats everything because safety sits right after physiologic needs in Maslow.\n- If safety is not at stake, choose the answer that keeps the nurse in a therapeutic role: open, feeling-focused, honest, non-judgmental.\n- If the question is about a drug, ask what can kill or harm fastest (airway, fever with rigidity, toxic level, hypertensive crisis, low white cells) and rank that first.\n- If two answers both look safe, pick the less restrictive and the more client-centered one.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nQuestions blend a communication choice, a safety decision and a drug point. The right answer is usually the one that is safe, calm, honest and focused on the client's feelings rather than on advice or reassurance.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]], [[Disturbances in Perception and Thought]], [[Unusual Speech Patterns]], [[Schizophrenia]], [[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Delirium versus Dementia]], [[Therapeutic Use of Self and Johari's Window]], [[Psychotropic Medication Classes: Which Drug for Which Problem]], [[Electroconvulsive Therapy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exam weighting between psychiatric and emergency content inside NP5 is UNKNOWN; check the current PRC table of specifications.\n- The review handout uses older labels in several places (mental retardation, somatoform, multiple personality, transvestitism). Newer manuals renamed them; learn both names.",

"id": "np5-therapeutic-communication",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Therapeutic Communication",
"body": "## What it is\nPurposeful, client-centered communication that helps a person express feelings, solve problems and feel understood. It differs from social talk because it has a goal and focuses on the client, not on the nurse.\nHandout goals of therapeutic communication: build the nurse-client relationship, find the client's most important concern right now, learn how the client sees the problem as it unfolded, help the client express emotions, teach the client and family self-care skills, recognize needs, carry out interventions for those needs, and guide the client toward a plan of action that is satisfying and socially acceptable.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Therapeutic techniques: active listening, open-ended questions, reflection (repeating feelings), restating, clarifying, focusing, offering self, giving information, silence, summarizing, and voicing doubt gently when the client holds a false belief.\n- Non-therapeutic blocks: false reassurance, giving advice, approving or disapproving, asking \"why\" (it sounds accusing), changing the subject, defending the staff, stereotyped replies, probing, belittling feelings, and closed questions when feelings are needed.\n- Nonverbal behavior counts: sit at the same level, keep open posture, moderate eye contact (respect cultural norms), and match calm tone.\n- Silence gives the client time to think; it is a tool, not a failure.\n- The full technique list is in [[Therapeutic Communication Techniques with Examples]] (accepting, broad openings, consensual validation, encouraging comparison, encouraging description of perceptions, encouraging expression, exploring, focusing, formulating a plan of action, general leads, giving information, giving recognition, making observations, offering self, placing events in time sequence, presenting reality, reflecting, restating, seeking information, silence, suggesting collaboration, summarizing, translating into feelings, verbalizing the implied, voicing doubt).\n- The full list of blocks is in [[Non-Therapeutic Communication Blocks with Examples]]. The classic ones: advising, agreeing, belittling feelings, challenging, defending, disagreeing, approving, literal responses, implying an outside source, interpreting, changing the subject, stereotyped comments, false reassurance, rejecting, asking for an explanation (especially \"why\"), testing and denial.\n- Reflecting hands a question or feeling back to the client (\"Do you think you should?\"). Restating repeats the main idea in nearly the client's own words. Do not mix them up: reflection targets feeling or the decision, restating targets content.\n- Focusing helps when the client jumps from topic to topic; exploring is for going deeper, but respect the client who does not want to explore a subject.\n- Presenting reality (\"I see no one else in the room\") and voicing doubt (\"That is hard to believe\") are used for false perceptions or beliefs, never to argue.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Whether the client's words and body language agree, the pace and content of speech, and readiness to talk.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Ensure safety and privacy first, then open with a broad, non-leading statement.\n2. Reflect the feeling before exploring the facts; feelings are the priority in these questions.\n3. For a client with hallucinations or delusions, do not argue or agree; state your own perception and acknowledge the client's experience.\n4. For a very anxious or confused client, use short, concrete sentences.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Feeling words in the stem point to the answer that names or explores the feeling (reflect, translate into feelings, verbalize the implied). Reason: the client's emotion is the real need; facts and advice come later.\n- A \"why\" question forces the client to defend or justify and sounds like blame. Replace it in your head with \"what happened\" or \"what led up to it\".\n- A client who says something dramatic or figurative (\"I am dead\", \"a camera is in my head\") should get a reply about the feeling, not a literal answer. Reason: literal replies miss the message and may feed the delusion.\n- A threat of self-harm is never answered with a change of subject, reassurance or a promise to keep secrets. Ask directly, stay, and act to keep the client safe.\n- Silence is chosen when the client is thinking or struggling to find words and the nurse is calm and attentive; it is not chosen for an acutely agitated client who needs a short, clear statement.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nThe stem gives a client statement and four replies. Pick the reply that names or explores the feeling in an open way. Eliminate replies that reassure, advise, ask \"why\", or shut the topic. If the client threatens self-harm, the best reply directly asks about it; never ignore it.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: \"Listen, Reflect, Explore\" and avoid the three A's of blocks: Advice, Approval, Argument.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Nurse-Patient Relationship Phases]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]], [[Disturbances in Perception and Thought]], [[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Therapeutic Communication Techniques with Examples]], [[Non-Therapeutic Communication Blocks with Examples]], [[Therapeutic Use of Self and Johari's Window]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Your textbook may name additional techniques; learn its list and its example wording.\n- The list of technique names and their example sentences differs by book. Learn the names your reviewer uses, since matching a sentence to a technique is a common item.\n- Some books place \"voicing doubt\" as acceptable only when used gently; others warn against it with delusional clients. Check how your reviewer treats it.",

"id": "np5-nurse-patient-relationship",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Nurse-Patient Relationship Phases",
"body": "## What it is\nA professional, goal-directed relationship with defined phases and boundaries. It exists for the client's benefit and ends when goals are met.\nThe handout describes four phases: pre-orientation, orientation, working and termination. It also calls the relationship goal-directed, focused on the needs of the patient, planned, time-limited and professional.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Common phase model (Peplau-style): orientation, identification, exploitation (working), resolution. Some textbooks use pre-interaction, orientation, working and termination; know both.\n- Orientation: introductions, roles, a contract (time, place, purpose, confidentiality limits), building trust, and the client's baseline.\n- Working: exploring problems, trying coping skills, promoting insight and behavior change; testing and resistance often appear here.\n- Termination: begins at the start; review progress, discuss feelings about ending, and avoid new content.\n- Empathy (understanding) differs from sympathy (sharing the feeling); the nurse stays empathic, not sympathetic.\n- Boundaries: no gifts, no personal contact outside role, no secrets, no dual relationships. Transference is the client directing feelings about a past person onto the nurse; countertransference is the nurse doing the same.\n- Pre-orientation: starts when the nurse is assigned to the client, before any meeting. Major task is developing self-awareness. Tasks are gathering data and planning the first interaction.\n- Orientation: begins when nurse and client meet and ends when the client starts to identify problems to work on. Tasks: establishing rapport and trust, assessment, explaining roles and the purpose of meetings, and setting the parameters of later meetings. The major task is a mutually acceptable contract.\n- Working: the longest and most productive phase. Limit setting is used here. It has two parts: problem identification (the client names the issues causing trouble) and exploitation (the nurse guides the client to examine feelings and responses and build better coping and a more positive self-image).\n- Resistance (ambivalence about self-exploration), transference (client unconsciously moves feelings from a past person onto the nurse) and countertransference (nurse moves her own past feelings onto the client) show up mostly in the working phase.\n- Termination (also called resolution): begins when problems are resolved, ends when the relationship ends, and brings anxiety, fear and a sense of loss.\n- Basic elements: TRUST. Trust, Rapport, Unconditional positive regard, Setting limits, Therapeutic communication.\n- Characteristics to remember: goal-directed, client-centered, planned, time-limited, professional.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Trust level, resistance, signs of dependence, and the nurse's own reactions.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Set limits and the contract early; it protects safety and trust.\n2. Be consistent, punctual and honest.\n3. Handle termination openly; do not make promises to stay in touch.\n4. Seek supervision when a client stirs strong personal feelings.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- To name the phase, ask what the task is. Introductions, contract and trust: orientation. Client now names problems: working (identification). Looking at feelings and learning coping: working (exploitation). Talking about progress and feelings about ending: termination. Reviewing a chart and own feelings before meeting: pre-orientation.\n- Boundary questions are won by the answer that keeps the relationship professional and about the client: no gifts, no secrets, no meeting outside the role, no giving personal phone numbers.\n- Strong liking or dislike for a client signals countertransference. Reason: the nurse's reaction is about herself, so the safe step is self-awareness and supervision, not stopping care.\n- A client who is late, silent or changes subject when feelings get close may be showing resistance. Accept it calmly; do not punish or confront.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nIdentify the phase from a described activity, and choose the reply that keeps boundaries. Confidentiality traps: the nurse never promises to keep secrets that threaten safety.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Personality Disorders]], [[Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms]], [[Therapeutic Use of Self and Johari's Window]], [[Therapeutic Communication Techniques with Examples]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Which phase names your school uses, and which tasks sit in which phase (books differ).\n- The handout phases (pre-orientation, orientation, working, termination) differ from the older Peplau names (orientation, identification, exploitation, resolution). Both appear in books; map them onto each other.\n- Which phase \"limit setting\" belongs to differs by book; the handout puts it in the working phase.",

"id": "np5-crisis-intervention",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Crisis and Crisis Intervention",
"body": "## What it is\nA crisis is a temporary state of disequilibrium when a stressor overwhelms usual coping. It is time-limited, commonly lasting a matter of weeks, and can lead to growth or breakdown.\nThe handout defines a crisis as a turning point in a person's life that produces an overwhelming emotional response. It is highly individual, usually lasts about 4 to 6 weeks, makes the person passive and submissive, and affects the person's support system. Crisis intervention enters the life situation of an individual, family, group or community to help them mobilize resources and reduce the effect of the stressful event. The goal is the best possible level of functioning.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Types: maturational (developmental, such as adolescence or retirement), situational (job loss, illness, death) and adventitious (disaster, violence, mass events).\n- Contributors: a hazardous event, a vulnerable state, a precipitating factor, and lack of coping or support.\n- Phases of response run from rising tension, to failed coping, to escalation and disorganization, to resolution or deterioration.\n- Crisis intervention is short-term, focused on the present problem, and aims to restore the previous level of functioning. It is not long-term therapy and does not dig into childhood.\n- Levels of prevention: primary (prevent), secondary (early crisis intervention), tertiary (rehabilitation after).\n- Four phases in the handout: denial (first reaction); increased tension (the person sees the crisis but keeps doing daily activities); disorganization (preoccupied, unable to do daily activities); attempts to reorganize (old coping methods are called up).\n- Two intervention styles: authoritative (assess health status, give information, give feedback about behavior, suggest courses of action) and facilitative (empathy, helping the client name and discuss feelings, acting as a sounding board, affirming self-worth). Details: [[Crisis Types, Phases and Intervention Styles]].\n- The nurse's primary role is active and directive.\n- Rape is a social crisis with its own syndrome and care steps: [[Rape, Rape Trauma Syndrome and Survivor Care]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- The event and its meaning, perceived threat, available support, coping skills tried, and suicide or violence risk.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Assess safety, including suicidal thoughts, first.\n2. Be directive, calm and present; the client may not think clearly.\n3. Help the client state the problem, explore options and choose one.\n4. Mobilize family and community support and link to follow-up.\n5. Evaluate whether functioning returned.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Ask what the client can still do. Still doing daily tasks: increased tension, so help problem-solving. Cannot do daily tasks: disorganization, so the nurse takes a more directive role (simple choices, concrete steps) because the client cannot decide well.\n- The first action is safety (suicide, violence), because a person in crisis may think of self-harm. Then focus on the here and now. Digging into childhood is wrong in a crisis; time is short and the client is overwhelmed.\n- Choose \"mobilize the support system and past coping\" over \"wait for the client to find a solution\".\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA grieving or newly diagnosed client shows disorganization; the answer is short-term, here-and-now, active nursing support, not insight therapy. Distinguish crisis types from stems.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: \"Safe, Name it, Options, Support.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Trauma and Stressor-Related Disorders]], [[Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts|Disaster Nursing]], [[Crisis Types, Phases and Intervention Styles]], [[Rape, Rape Trauma Syndrome and Survivor Care]], [[Acute Stress Disorder versus Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Typical duration and the exact phase model your textbook uses (verify). Philippine crisis lines and program details: verify local policy.\n- The old note says \"commonly lasting a matter of weeks\"; the handout states 4 to 6 weeks. Phase models differ among books (some use four phases from Caplan, some three).\n- The maturational versus situational versus adventitious wording is used in older books; the handout calls the third type social.",

"id": "np5-freud-defense-mechanisms",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms",
"body": "## What it is\nFreud described personality as three structures and development in psychosexual stages. Defense mechanisms are unconscious ways the ego reduces anxiety.\nHandout view of the structures: the id seeks instant gratification, acts impulsively and ignores rules. The superego holds values and parental and social expectations, so it opposes the id. The ego mediates between them; it represents mature, adaptive behavior that lets a person function in the world.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Structures: id (instinct, pleasure, immediate), ego (reality, mediator), superego (conscience, ideals).\n- Levels of mind: conscious, preconscious, unconscious.\n- Psychosexual stages: oral, anal, phallic, latency, genital.\n- Common defenses: repression (pushing thoughts out of awareness), suppression (conscious pushing away), denial, projection (blaming others for own feelings), displacement (shifting feelings to a safer target), rationalization (logical excuses), reaction formation (acting opposite to true feeling), regression (return to earlier behavior), identification, introjection, sublimation (channeling urges into acceptable activity, considered healthy), undoing, compensation, intellectualization, isolation of affect, splitting, conversion and dissociation.\n- Immature defenses often seen in personality and psychotic disorders; sublimation, humor and altruism are mature.\n- Levels of awareness: conscious (what you are aware of now), preconscious (not in awareness now but recalled with effort, for example remembering something from childhood), unconscious (the largest part; it holds motives and most defense mechanisms and material that can never be recalled directly).\n- Repression: unconscious forgetting of painful thoughts (no memory of a mugging that happened yesterday). Suppression: conscious forgetting (trying to forget past money problems).\n- Rationalization: excuses one's behavior to avoid responsibility, conflict or loss of self-respect (blames a failing grade on a mean teacher).\n- Reaction formation: acts opposite to what one feels (a woman who never wanted children becomes a super-mom; despising the boss but praising the boss to everyone).\n- Regression: moves back to an earlier stage to feel safe (a five-year-old asks for a bottle when the baby brother is fed).\n- Displacement: vents intense feelings on a less threatening person (angry at the boss, yells at the spouse).\n- Compensation: overachieves in one area to offset a real or imagined weakness in another (a nurse with low self-esteem works double shifts so the supervisor will like her).\n- Conversion: an emotional conflict becomes a physical symptom, usually in movement or senses (a teenager forbidden to watch x-rated movies develops blindness and is unconcerned).\n- Denial: refuses to admit an unbearable reality (a diabetic eating candy, waiting three days with severe abdominal pain).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Behavior that reduces anxiety but distorts reality, and how rigidly it is used.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Do not confront or strip a defense away in an acutely anxious client; it is protection.\n2. Address the underlying anxiety and teach healthier coping.\n3. Set limits on harmful behavior (such as acting out) while accepting the person.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Ask two questions. Was the forgetting conscious or not (repression versus suppression)? Did the feeling go to a safer target (displacement), come back at the person as a body symptom (conversion), or get turned into its opposite (reaction formation)?\n- Reason about the nurse's response: defenses lower anxiety, so they are not attacked in a client who is acutely anxious. The nurse treats the anxiety first, then helps build healthier coping.\n- Compensation and sublimation are generally healthy or neutral; denial, projection, regression and conversion are the ones linked to illness.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA short vignette is given; name the defense. Look-alikes: repression vs suppression (unconscious vs conscious), displacement vs projection, denial vs rationalization.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: projection = \"it's YOU\"; displacement = \"kick the dog\"; reaction formation = \"protests too much\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[Phobic and Somatic Symptom Disorders]], [[Personality Disorders]], [[Expressive and Insight Therapies]], [[Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care]], [[Somatoform Disorders: Gains, Theories and Management]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact stage ages, and which defenses your textbook classes as mature, neurotic or immature.\n- The handout treats unconscious material as \"can never be recalled\"; most books say it can surface through dreams, free association or therapy. Learn the answer your reviewer expects.\n- Some books list conversion and dissociation as separate defenses; others fold them into the disorders they cause.",

"id": "np5-symptoms-affect-memory",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Disturbances in Affect and Memory",
"body": "## What it is\nVocabulary for describing mental status findings. Mood is the sustained inner emotion; affect is the outward expression seen by others.\n\n## Must-know facts\nAffect:\n- Flat: almost no expression. Blunted: reduced. Constricted: narrowed range. Labile: swings rapidly. Inappropriate (incongruent): does not match the content. Broad: normal range.\n- Euphoria: intense elation. Elation: joy with confidence. Ambivalence: opposite feelings together. Apathy: lack of interest. Anhedonia: no pleasure from usual activities. Dysphoria: unpleasant mood. Anxiety, fear and depersonalization also belong in this group.\n\nMemory:\n- Amnesia: loss of memory (anterograde forward, retrograde backward).\n- Confabulation: filling gaps with made-up details, without intent to lie, typical in alcohol-related memory disorders.\n- Paramnesia includes deja vu (feeling already seen) and jamais vu (familiar feels new).\n- Hypermnesia: excessive recall. Dissociative amnesia follows stress; organic amnesia follows brain injury or illness.\n- Handout affect terms: inappropriate affect (the emotion does not fit the situation), blunted affect (severe reduction), flat affect (almost none), apathy (indifference toward people, activities and events), ambivalence (two opposing feelings at once).\n- Depersonalization: feeling detached from oneself; the client may state his name correctly but feel the body belongs to someone else or that the spirit has left the body. Derealization: strangeness toward the environment; objects seem larger, smaller or unfamiliar, the world looks changed or gray and dull. Mnemonic: depersonalization is about \"me\", derealization is about \"it\" (the world).\n- Motor disturbances: echopraxia (pathologic imitation of another person's movements or posture) and waxy flexibility (holding an awkward position for long periods without discomfort), both catatonic signs.\n- Confabulation: filling a memory gap with a detailed fantasy that the person believes, and its purpose is to protect self-esteem. Example: a resident insists she played cards with the husband of another resident who died years before she met them. Amnesia: inability to recall past events.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Mental status exam: appearance, behavior, speech, mood and affect, thought, perception, cognition, insight and judgment.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Describe what you see in objective words, not labels.\n2. Rule out physical causes for sudden memory change.\n3. Orient gently and ensure safety when memory is impaired.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Find the clue word in the stem. \"Cannot remember, then makes up a story\" is confabulation (protects self-esteem), not lying. \"Feels body belongs to someone else\" is depersonalization. \"Everything looks gray and unreal\" is derealization. \"Shows no facial reaction at all\" is flat; \"a little reaction\" is blunted.\n- For a client with memory gaps, safety and orientation come before testing memory. Reason: arguing with or quizzing a confabulating client harms self-esteem and gains nothing.\n- Sudden new confusion or memory loss is a medical problem until proven otherwise (low oxygen, infection, glucose, drugs).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the term to the example. Traps: flat vs blunted; confabulation vs lying; mood vs affect.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Schizophrenia]], [[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Delirium versus Dementia]], [[Unusual Speech Patterns]], [[Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care]], [[Schizophrenia]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Definitions of paramnesia subtypes differ by source.\n- Handout lists ambivalence as an affect disturbance; some books place it with thought or with schizophrenia's signs. Depersonalization is both a symptom and a disorder, so read the stem for which is asked.",

"id": "np5-symptoms-perception-thought",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Disturbances in Perception and Thought",
"body": "## What it is\nPerception disturbances are abnormal sensory experiences; thought disturbances are abnormal content (what is thought) or process (how thoughts flow).\n\n## Must-know facts\nPerception:\n- Hallucination: sensory experience with no external stimulus (auditory most common in schizophrenia; visual suggests delirium or substance effects; tactile suggests withdrawal or substance use; olfactory or gustatory may suggest seizure or brain disease).\n- Illusion: misreading a real stimulus. Depersonalization: feeling unreal in oneself. Derealization: surroundings feel unreal. Hypnagogic and hypnopompic: at sleep onset or waking.\n\nThought content:\n- Delusion: fixed false belief resistant to proof. Types: persecutory, grandiose, somatic, religious, jealous, reference, nihilistic.\n- Ideas of reference, thought broadcasting, insertion and withdrawal, obsession, compulsion, phobia, magical thinking, overvalued idea, poverty of content.\n\nThought process:\n- Loose associations, flight of ideas, tangentiality, circumstantiality, blocking, perseveration, neologism, concrete thinking, thought poverty.\n- Handout definitions: a delusion is a false belief inconsistent with the person's knowledge and culture (for example claiming to be engaged to a movie star or the child of a national leader). A hallucination is a false sensory perception, a perceptual experience that does not really exist (for example hearing a voice that commands harm to another person).\n- Thought-process terms with handout examples: circumstantial thinking (too many unnecessary details, but finally reaches the point), tangential thinking (too many details and never gets to the point), word salad (jumbled disconnected words), verbigeration (stereotyped repetition of words or phrases), perseveration (keeps returning to one idea or repeats a word despite attempts to change the topic), echolalia (repeats what the nurse says), flight of ideas (a rush of fragmented or unrelated ideas), loose associations (jumps from idea to idea with little connection), neologism (invented word).\n- More detail on delusion types, thought broadcasting, insertion and blocking is in [[Delusion Types and Thought Disturbances in Schizophrenia]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Content of voices (especially commands to harm self or others), distress, and how much the client acts on them.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Ask about command hallucinations; if present, safety is the priority.\n2. Do not argue with or confirm a delusion; present reality calmly and focus on feelings.\n3. Reduce stimulation; stay with a frightened client.\n4. Give antipsychotics as ordered and watch effects.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Separate form from content. Content is what the person believes (delusion, obsession). Form is how thoughts flow (tangential, flight of ideas). Perception is what the senses report (hallucination, illusion).\n- Circumstantial versus tangential: both wander, only circumstantial returns. Perseveration versus verbigeration: perseveration sticks to a topic or idea despite redirection; verbigeration is empty repeated words.\n- Nursing reasoning: a voice that gives commands is a safety question first (what does it say, will you obey it). Disagreeing with a delusion (challenging) makes the client defend it. Agreeing confirms it. Present reality calmly, state what you see, and talk about the feeling the belief produces.\n- Fear is the common ground: a frightened client needs a calm nurse, low stimulation and short sentences.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nIdentify the term from an example, or choose the reply to a client who says people are following them: acknowledge fear, state you see no evidence, and offer safety.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: hallucination = sees without a screen; illusion = misreads the screen.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Schizophrenia]], [[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Unusual Speech Patterns]], [[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]], [[Delusion Types and Thought Disturbances in Schizophrenia]], [[Non-Therapeutic Communication Blocks with Examples]], [[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Fine definitions (for example circumstantiality vs tangentiality) vary by text.\n- Some books class ideas of reference as delusions, others as milder ideas short of a delusion. Verify.",

"id": "np5-unusual-speech",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Unusual Speech Patterns",
"body": "## What it is\nAbnormal form or amount of speech that points to disordered thinking, mood episodes or brain disease.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Echolalia: repeating what another says. Palilalia: repeating own words. Verbigeration: meaningless repetition of words or phrases.\n- Clang association: choosing words by sound (rhyming), often in mania.\n- Word salad: jumbled words with no meaning. Neologism: invented word. Mutism: not speaking. Pressured speech: rapid, hard to interrupt, typical of mania. Poverty of speech: very little speech.\n- Loose associations and flight of ideas are process disturbances that show up in speech.\n- Aphasia is a language deficit from brain injury and is not a psychiatric symptom, but may appear in questions as a contrast.\n- Echopraxia is imitation of movements, listed beside echolalia in catatonia.\n- Handout examples to anchor the terms. Word salad: \"corn, potatoes, jump up, play games, grass, cupboard.\" Verbigeration: \"I want to go home, go home, go home.\" Neologism: \"I'm afraid of grittiz.\" Echolalia: nurse asks \"How are you feeling?\" and client says \"How are you feeling?\" Flight of ideas: \"The sun is shining. Where is my sun? I love Lucy. Let us play ball.\" Loose associations: one thought slides to another with no visible link (candy bar to yen to the Swiss to holes).\n- Clang associations (in schizophrenia notes of the handout): words chosen by sound or rhyme, such as \"pill, hill, Jill, kill.\"\n- Circumstantial and tangential speech are about excess detail; circumstantial reaches the point, tangential does not.\n- Thought blocking: stopping abruptly in the middle of a sentence and sometimes unable to continue. Alogia: poverty of content, speech that has little real meaning (a vague answer such as \"I do not know... it's hard to tell\").\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Rate, volume, fluency and logic, and whether speech makes sense in context.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Stay calm; use short, clear statements.\n2. Do not pretend to understand; say what you did understand and ask again.\n3. Check for medical causes if speech changes suddenly (stroke, delirium).\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Judge by what the listener can follow. Cannot follow at all: word salad. Can follow, but the speaker takes a long road: circumstantial. Rapid, pressured and topic changes by link of meaning: flight of ideas (mania). Topic changes by sound: clang. Silence mid-sentence: blocking.\n- Nursing action follows the same logic: when speech is disorganized, use short, concrete questions, one at a time. Do not pretend to understand and do not correct the speech.\n- A new speech change in an older person is a medical alert (stroke, delirium), not just a psychiatric symptom.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nName the term from an example. Memorize pairs: echolalia (copies others), clang (rhymes), word salad (jumble), neologism (new word).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Disturbances in Perception and Thought]], [[Schizophrenia]], [[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Delusion Types and Thought Disturbances in Schizophrenia]], [[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]], [[Therapeutic Communication Techniques with Examples]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some books treat catatonia and speech terms differently.\n- Handouts differ on whether word salad is the same as severe loose associations; many texts treat word salad as the extreme end. Palilalia is not in the handout.",

"id": "np5-behavioral-management",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation",
"body": "## What it is\nMethods to keep clients and staff safe when behavior becomes aggressive or self-harming. The principle is the least restrictive, effective measure, used for the shortest time.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Escalation order: verbal de-escalation and environment changes first; then voluntary time-out or medication as ordered; then seclusion or physical restraint only when there is imminent danger and less restrictive options fail.\n- Restraints and seclusion require a prescriber order and face-to-face evaluation within a short time after starting; renewal is time-limited. Exact time limits depend on age and policy: verify.\n- Restraints are never for convenience, punishment or staff shortage.\n- Continuous or frequent observation, circulation and skin checks, hydration, toileting, comfort and dignity are required; document reason, alternatives tried, client response and release criteria.\n- Behavior therapy tools: positive reinforcement, token economy, limit setting, extinction, time-out, modeling.\n- Behavior modification (see [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]]) strengthens a wanted behavior with reinforcement. Positive reinforcement adds something pleasant after the behavior. Negative reinforcement removes something unpleasant right after it. Both make the behavior more likely; neither is punishment.\n- Milieu therapy uses a deliberately controlled environment so the setting itself supports change; see [[Group, Milieu and Family Therapy]].\n- The least restrictive effective measure is the guiding rule in every restraint or seclusion question.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Triggers, early warning signs (pacing, clenched fists, loud voice, threats), intoxication, pain, delirium or unmet needs, and weapons risk.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Protect safety and call for help early; keep a team.\n2. Approach calmly, give space, speak low and slow, offer choices, and set clear, short limits.\n3. Remove the audience and reduce stimulation.\n4. If restraint is unavoidable, use trained staff, maintain airway and breathing, and monitor continuously.\n5. Debrief the client and staff afterward.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Walk the ladder from the bottom: space and calm voice, then reduce stimulation, then a voluntary time-out, then medication as ordered, and only then seclusion or restraint if there is immediate danger. Reason: each step takes away more freedom, so it is justified only when the one before cannot keep people safe.\n- While restrained, the client is a physiologic priority: airway, breathing, circulation, hydration, toileting, skin and circulation of limbs. Reason: a restrained, agitated or sedated person can aspirate, overheat or injure nerves, and cannot call for help.\n- After the event the order is client safe, then debrief the client, then staff, then document. Never leave \"convenience\" or \"punishment\" as the reason.\n- If the stem says the client is intoxicated, delirious or in pain, treat that cause: the aggression may be a symptom.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nChoose the least restrictive first step. Trap: jumping to restraint or injection before verbal de-escalation, or leaving a restrained client unobserved. Another trap: arguing with a client who is escalating.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: \"Talk, Space, Medicine, Hold.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]], [[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]], [[Schizophrenia]], [[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]], [[Group, Milieu and Family Therapy]], [[Antipsychotic Anticholinergic Effects, Clozapine Alert and Client Teaching]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Order and duration rules; Philippine mental health law and facility policy: verify local policy.\n- Time limits for orders, face-to-face evaluation and renewal come from law and facility policy, not from this handout.",

"id": "np5-anxiety",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nAnxiety is a vague, uneasy feeling of dread from an unknown or unrecognized threat; fear is a response to a known threat. Anxiety disorders are anxiety that is excessive, persistent and disabling.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Levels: mild (alert, learning improves), moderate (narrowed attention, selective inattention, can still follow direction), severe (perception greatly narrowed, focus on scattered details, cannot learn), panic (loss of control, disorganized, terror, possible harm).\n- Disorders in this family: generalized anxiety disorder (chronic worry about many things, for months), panic disorder (sudden attacks of intense fear with physical symptoms, usually peaking within minutes), agoraphobia, social anxiety, specific phobia, separation anxiety. OCD and trauma disorders are now grouped separately: see [[Obsessive-Compulsive and Related Disorders]] and [[Trauma and Stressor-Related Disorders]].\n- Physical signs: tachycardia, rapid breathing, sweating, tremor, dry mouth, nausea, dizziness. Rule out medical causes (thyroid, cardiac, caffeine, drug effects).\n- Handout level details: mild anxiety widens the perceptual field and sharpens learning and problem solving; moderate narrows the field to the immediate task and the person can learn or solve problems only with help; severe brings dread or terror, scattered details, tachycardia, sweating and chest pain (these clients often go to the emergency room fearing a heart attack); panic brings loss of rational thought, possible delusions or hallucinations, immobility or muteness, or bolting and running aimlessly with risk of injury.\n- Type-by-type criteria: [[Panic Disorder, Agoraphobia, Social Phobia and Generalized Anxiety Disorder]] and [[Acute Stress Disorder versus Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder]].\n- Handout principles of care: stay calm, give medication as ordered, listen to the concern, reduce environmental stimuli. Priority diagnoses: anxiety and ineffective individual coping.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Anxiety level, triggers, physical symptoms, coping, safety, substance use.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Stay with the client; a calm presence is first, especially in panic. Maslow: safety.\n2. Reduce stimulation, use short, simple directions, and move to a quiet place.\n3. Do not touch without permission in severe anxiety.\n4. Once calmer, explore triggers and teach breathing, relaxation and grounding.\n5. Teach only when anxiety is mild; learning fails at severe levels.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Benzodiazepines act quickly but risk dependence and sedation; use short term. Antidepressants (SSRIs, SNRIs) are common first-line for long-term control. Buspirone is non-sedating and slow acting. Beta-blockers can reduce performance anxiety. Cognitive behavioral therapy and relaxation are core.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Avoid caffeine and alcohol, do not stop medication suddenly, practice relaxation daily, and report worsening mood or thoughts of self-harm.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Chest pain, sweating and a racing pulse in an anxious client are first treated as possibly cardiac. Reason: a physical emergency outranks a psychological label, so assess before assuming anxiety.\n- The nurse's calm presence is the first intervention at every level. As the level rises, the nurse does less asking and teaching and more staying, simplifying and protecting.\n- Learning is possible only at mild or low moderate levels. A teaching answer is wrong for severe or panic anxiety.\n- Medication follows safety: anxiolytics relieve symptoms but do not treat the cause, so coping skills are still taught afterward.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nIdentify the level from behaviors, then choose an action that fits the level. Trap: teaching or asking for decisions in severe or panic anxiety.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: mild = \"I notice\", moderate = \"I narrow\", severe = \"I scatter\", panic = \"I flee\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms]], [[Phobic and Somatic Symptom Disorders]], [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Panic Disorder, Agoraphobia, Social Phobia and Generalized Anxiety Disorder]], [[Anxiolytic Drugs and Cognitive-Behavioral Techniques for Anxiety]], [[Antianxiety Drugs: Benzodiazepines and Buspirone]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Drug onset and duration details; the exact diagnostic grouping your textbook uses (older books keep OCD and PTSD under anxiety).\n- Handout says panic attacks last 15 to 30 minutes; many books say they peak within about 10 minutes. Handout names SSRIs as safest drug during a panic attack, which is odd because SSRIs act slowly; benzodiazepines are the rapid option. Verify both.",

"id": "np5-ocd",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Obsessive-Compulsive and Related Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nOCD has obsessions (unwanted, intrusive thoughts, images or urges that cause distress) and compulsions (repetitive acts or mental rituals done to reduce that distress). The rituals relieve anxiety briefly but reinforce the cycle.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Common themes: contamination with washing, doubt with checking, order and symmetry, and unacceptable thoughts. Insight is usually present: the client knows it is excessive.\n- Related disorders: hoarding disorder, body dysmorphic disorder (preoccupation with a perceived flaw), trichotillomania (hair pulling) and excoriation (skin picking).\n- Different from obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, which is a rigid pattern without true rituals and is seen by the person as normal.\n- Compulsions are time-consuming and impair work and relationships.\n- Handout contrast: obsessions are thoughts, impulses or images that cause marked anxiety; compulsions are repetitive acts or mental acts done to neutralize the anxiety. Examples: fear of dirt and germs leads to excessive hand washing; fear of burglary leads to repeated checking of door and window locks.\n- Obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (handout cluster C): preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and control; nursing care is to encourage negotiation with others, help the client make timely decisions and finish work, and use cognitive restructuring. See [[Nursing Care in Cluster C Personality Disorders]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Type and time spent on rituals, distress, skin damage from washing, depression and suicide risk.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Do not forbid the ritual abruptly; this raises anxiety to severe levels.\n2. Allow limited ritual time at first, then gradually reduce it with the client's agreement.\n3. Provide structure and keep the routine predictable.\n4. Protect skin and meet basic needs that rituals interfere with.\n5. Praise efforts to resist; do not criticize.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- SSRIs (often at higher dosing than for depression: verify) and clomipramine, with cognitive behavioral therapy using exposure and response prevention.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Family should avoid joining rituals or giving constant reassurance; teach that treatment takes weeks.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- The ritual is the client's only way to lower anxiety. Remove it suddenly and anxiety jumps to severe or panic. So the nurse allows the ritual at first, schedules it, and reduces it slowly while teaching other ways to cope.\n- Separate disorder from personality: ritual plus distress and awareness means OCD; rigid perfectionism that the person considers right and normal means the personality disorder.\n- Check the skin and hydration of a client who washes constantly; physical harm from the ritual is a real priority.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nChoose the supportive, gradual approach. Trap: stopping the ritual at once, or telling the client to just stop.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Personality Disorders by Cluster]], [[Nursing Care in Cluster C Personality Disorders]], [[Tricyclic and SSRI Antidepressants: Drug Lists and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Classification (separate chapter in the newer manual), and drug dosing statements.\n- Handout lists SSRIs as effective for OCD; fluvoxamine and clomipramine are the classic drugs. Dosing is not given here.",

"id": "np5-phobia-somatic",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Phobic and Somatic Symptom Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nPhobias are intense, irrational fear of a specific object or situation with avoidance. Somatic symptom disorders are physical complaints, with excessive thoughts and worry, that are not fully explained by medical findings; the distress is real.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Phobia types: specific (animals, heights, blood, injury), social (scrutiny by others), agoraphobia (situations where escape is hard). Fear is out of proportion; the client often knows it, yet avoids.\n- Defense: displacement and avoidance of the true fear.\n- Treatment of choice is behavioral: systematic desensitization (gradual exposure with relaxation), flooding (rapid prolonged exposure, used carefully), and modeling.\n- Somatic types: somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder (fear of having illness with few symptoms), conversion disorder or functional neurological symptom disorder (loss of motor or sensory function with no organic cause, often following stress; \"la belle indifference\", calm unconcern, may appear), pain disorder. Factitious disorder and malingering differ: symptoms are produced on purpose (for attention or for gain).\n- Handout specific phobia: marked anxiety to a specific object or situation, avoidance or suffered endurance, impaired routine, and the adolescent or adult knows the fear is excessive. Treatment: anti-anxiety drugs and systematic desensitization, where the therapist gradually exposes the client to the feared object in a safe setting until anxiety falls.\n- Social phobia: fear of embarrassment or inability to perform, belief that others judge negatively, avoidance; managed by anti-anxiety drugs and social skills training. See [[Panic Disorder, Agoraphobia, Social Phobia and Generalized Anxiety Disorder]].\n- Handout somatoform features: conversion disorder shows sudden sensory or motor loss with \"la belle indifference\"; pain disorder is pain that analgesics rarely relieve; hypochondriasis is disease conviction or fear of getting a disease; body dysmorphic disorder is preoccupation with an imagined or exaggerated defect. Full detail: [[Somatoform Disorders: Gains, Theories and Management]], [[Malingering, Factitious Disorder and Munchausen Syndrome]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Symptom history, stressors, secondary gain, functional loss, and always rule out physical illness first.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Take every physical complaint seriously and check it; do not assume it is psychological.\n2. Accept the client without confronting (\"it's all in your head\").\n3. Focus on feelings and coping instead of symptoms; give attention when the client is well, not only when sick.\n4. Encourage independence in daily activities.\n5. For phobia, expose gradually; never force exposure.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Antidepressants and short-term anxiolytics as ordered; psychotherapy is central.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Phobia answer: gradual exposure with relaxation, never forced exposure. Reason: forcing raises anxiety to panic and teaches the client the situation is dangerous.\n- Somatic answer: believe the symptom is real, rule out physical disease, build trust, and delay confronting the cause until the client has other coping tools. Reason: the symptoms ease anxiety (primary gain), so removing them without replacements raises anxiety.\n- Conversion versus malingering: unconscious loss with calm unconcern versus conscious faking for a reward.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nChoose systematic desensitization for a phobia, and a non-judgmental, symptom-limiting approach for somatic clients. Trap: telling the client the symptoms are not real.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms]], [[General Psychiatric Disorders]], [[Panic Disorder, Agoraphobia, Social Phobia and Generalized Anxiety Disorder]], [[Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Names and criteria changed between manual editions; confirm which terms your reviewer uses.\n- Handout term \"somatoform\" is replaced in newer manuals; hypochondriasis became illness anxiety disorder.",

"id": "np5-trauma-stressor",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Trauma and Stressor-Related Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nDisorders that follow exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury or violence. They include acute stress disorder, post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) and adjustment disorder.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Acute stress disorder: symptoms within the first weeks after the event, lasting a short time. PTSD: symptoms persist beyond about a month (verify cutoff in your text); can start late.\n- PTSD symptom clusters: re-experiencing (flashbacks, nightmares, intrusive memories), avoidance, negative changes in mood and thinking, and hyperarousal (startle, irritability, poor sleep, hypervigilance).\n- Adjustment disorder: distress out of proportion to a stressor, within a few months of it, and not meeting other diagnoses.\n- Related: dissociation, depression, substance misuse and suicide risk are common.\n- Handout acute stress disorder: starts within 1 month of an extreme stressor and lasts 2 days to 4 weeks, with intense fear, re-experiencing, arousal and at least three dissociative signs (numbing, feeling dazed, derealization, depersonalization, dissociative amnesia).\n- Handout PTSD: re-experiencing, avoidance and numbing, and persistent arousal (sleep disturbance, irritability, poor concentration, hypervigilance, exaggerated startle). Management: antianxiety drugs, antidepressants and group therapy.\n- Full comparison: [[Acute Stress Disorder versus Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder]]. Care after sexual assault: [[Rape, Rape Trauma Syndrome and Survivor Care]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Safety, suicidal thoughts, sleep, triggers, substance use, social support, and the client's pace in talking about the event.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Ensure physical and emotional safety; a calm, predictable setting.\n2. Do not force the client to retell the trauma; let the client lead.\n3. During a flashback, speak calmly, orient to the present place and time, and avoid sudden touch.\n4. Encourage use of supports and teach grounding and relaxation.\n5. Refer for trauma-focused therapy.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- SSRIs are commonly used; other drugs target sleep or nightmares. Trauma-focused cognitive behavioral therapy and exposure therapy are core.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Symptoms are a normal reaction to abnormal events; recovery is possible; avoid alcohol; keep routine.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Use the clock: symptoms for under a month after the event is acute stress disorder; beyond a month is PTSD.\n- A flashback is a present-time experience for the client. Ground in the present: say the date and place, you are an adult and safe, stand and walk, focus on the room's sights and sounds. Avoid sudden touch and restraining if possible.\n- Do not force retelling. Reason: pushing for details retraumatizes and breaks trust.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nName the disorder from the time course, or choose the supportive, non-pressuring action. Trap: urging the client to talk about details immediately.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]], [[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts|Disaster Nursing]], [[Acute Stress Disorder versus Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder]], [[Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care]], [[Crisis Types, Phases and Intervention Styles]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Time cutoffs and cluster wording by manual edition; Philippine services after disasters: verify local policy.\n- Handout lists fear, helplessness or horror as required; newer manuals dropped that wording.",

"id": "np5-eating-disorders",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Eating Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nDisorders of eating with distorted body image and serious physical risk. Main types: anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Anorexia nervosa: restriction of intake, intense fear of weight gain, very low body weight, distorted body image; subtypes restricting and binge-purge. Complications include bradycardia, hypotension, electrolyte imbalance (especially low potassium), amenorrhea, osteoporosis and heart rhythm problems.\n- Bulimia nervosa: recurrent binges followed by compensatory behavior (vomiting, laxatives, diuretics, fasting, excess exercise); weight is often normal. Signs: dental erosion, swollen salivary glands, scars on knuckles, low potassium, metabolic alkalosis.\n- Binge-eating disorder: binges without compensatory behavior, with distress, often with obesity.\n- Common themes: control, perfectionism, low self-esteem, family and cultural pressure.\n- Handout anorexia details: weight about 85 percent of expected or lower, amenorrhea, cold intolerance, low blood pressure, temperature and pulse, raised BUN, leukopenia, mild anemia and enlarged salivary glands. Bulimia: binge and compensate at least twice a week for three months, enamel loss, esophageal tears, metabolic alkalosis from vomiting, acidosis from laxative diarrhea, mildly raised amylase. Full side-by-side: [[Anorexia Nervosa and Bulimia Nervosa: Findings and Treatment]].\n- Common diagnoses: body image disturbance, self-esteem disturbance, ineffective individual coping.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Weight and vital signs, electrolytes, heart rhythm, hydration, eating habits, purging, suicide risk, body image.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat life-threatening physical problems first: vital signs, rhythm, fluids and electrolytes, then nutrition. Maslow: physiologic.\n2. Supervise meals and stay during and after (for a set time) to prevent purging; keep bathroom access supervised.\n3. Use a firm, matter-of-fact approach, a set eating plan and a contract; avoid power struggles and long talks about food.\n4. Weigh regularly in a consistent way (same scale, similar time, gown).\n5. Build self-esteem; address feelings rather than food.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Nutritional rehabilitation, family-based therapy for adolescents, cognitive behavioral therapy; antidepressants may help bulimia and binge eating. Feeding must be re-started carefully to avoid refeeding problems: verify.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Rank problems: unstable heart rate, rhythm or electrolytes first, then fluids and nutrition, then behavior contract, then body image work. Reason: Maslow physiologic needs and the fastest threat to life.\n- Weight tells the disorder: very low weight is anorexia; usually normal weight with binge-purge cycles is bulimia.\n- Eating plan and observation after meals prevent purging; bargaining over portions rewards manipulation.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPrioritize electrolyte or cardiac findings over body image work. Trap: negotiating with the client over meals or bargaining.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Personality Disorders]], [[Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms]], [[Anorexia Nervosa and Bulimia Nervosa: Findings and Treatment]], [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact weight criteria and refeeding steps.\n- Handout bulimia frequency is older; newer manuals use once a week for three months. Drug choices in the handout are dated.",

"id": "np5-personality-disorders",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Personality Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nLong-standing, inflexible patterns of thinking, feeling and behaving that deviate from cultural expectations, start by adolescence or early adulthood, cause distress or impairment, and are usually ego-syntonic (the person does not see them as a problem).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Three clusters: A odd or eccentric, B dramatic or emotional, C anxious or fearful. See [[Personality Disorders by Cluster]] for each disorder.\n- Common defenses: splitting, projection, denial, acting out.\n- Manipulation, testing limits and staff splitting are typical in cluster B.\n- Handout definition: an ingrained, enduring pattern of behaving and relating to self, others and the environment, including perception, attitudes and emotions. Nursing care by disorder is in [[Nursing Care in Cluster A and Cluster B Personality Disorders]] and [[Nursing Care in Cluster C Personality Disorders]].\n- Confrontation is the handout's tool for manipulative or deceptive behavior: the nurse states facts about the behavior calmly (\"You said you want to manage anger but missed the last three groups\"), without anger or punishment.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Pattern across time, relationships, impulsivity, self-harm and suicide risk, substance use.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first for self-harm or suicidal behavior; take every threat seriously.\n2. Set clear, consistent, enforceable limits and apply them the same way across the team.\n3. Be neutral, calm and non-punitive; avoid arguing, bargaining or special favors.\n4. Hold the client responsible for behavior; do not rescue.\n5. Encourage expression of feelings in words rather than actions.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- No drug treats the personality itself; medications target symptoms (mood, anxiety, impulsivity, psychosis). Dialectical behavior therapy is known for borderline pattern.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Practice coping skills, avoid substances, and keep to the agreed plan.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cluster B clients test limits, split staff and manipulate. The answer that wins is consistent team response, written plan, calm firmness and no special favors. Reason: inconsistency rewards the testing and divides staff.\n- Self-harm or suicide threats are taken seriously even when they look manipulative. Safety first, then limits.\n- Cluster A and C clients need different tone: formal and honest for paranoid, steady encouragement for avoidant, independence for dependent.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems describe staff conflict, manipulation or gift offers; the answer is team consistency and boundaries. Trap: agreeing to a special exception.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Personality Disorders by Cluster]], [[Nurse-Patient Relationship Phases]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]], [[Nursing Care in Cluster A and Cluster B Personality Disorders]], [[Nursing Care in Cluster C Personality Disorders]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Prevalence statements and manual wording.\n- Handout phrasing on touching and distance (for histrionic clients) is unusual; follow your text.",
