"title": "Personality Disorders by Cluster",
"body": "## What it is\nDetailed comparison of the ten personality disorders by cluster, for fast recognition.\n\n## Must-know facts\nCluster A (odd, eccentric; related to the psychotic spectrum):\n- Paranoid: distrust and suspicion, assumes others harm them. Schizoid: detached, little interest in closeness, flat affect. Schizotypal: odd beliefs, magical thinking, eccentric dress and speech.\n\nCluster B (dramatic, emotional, erratic):\n- Antisocial: disregard for rights of others, deceit, lack of remorse, history of conduct problems in youth. Borderline: unstable relationships, self-image and mood, fear of abandonment, impulsivity, self-harm, splitting (idealize then devalue). Histrionic: attention seeking, shallow shifting emotion, seductive. Narcissistic: grandiosity, need for admiration, lacks empathy, fragile self-esteem.\n\nCluster C (anxious, fearful):\n- Avoidant: social inhibition, fear of rejection, feels inadequate. Dependent: needs to be taken care of, submissive, cannot decide alone. Obsessive-compulsive: perfectionism, order and control, rigid, not ritual-driven.\n- Handout adds: paranoid uses projection; schizoid reports no pleasurable activities and prefers things to people; schizotypal shows ill-fitting, mismatched, dirty clothes, ideas of reference and magical thinking; antisocial shows lying, rationalization, thrill-seeking, poor work history and irresponsibility; borderline shows fear of abandonment, unstable intense relationships, self-mutilation or suicidal gestures and brief psychotic symptoms; histrionic is excessive emotionality and attention seeking, even in work settings; narcissistic is grandiosity, need for admiration and lack of empathy; avoidant fears rejection, criticism and shame and feels inferior; dependent clings, is submissive and fears separation; obsessive-compulsive centers on order, perfectionism and control.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety and suicide risk, especially borderline.\n2. Consistent limits across all staff (antisocial, borderline).\n3. Paranoid: be honest, avoid whispering or laughing near the client, do not argue about suspicions.\n4. Avoidant and dependent: encourage small independent decisions and gradual social steps.\n5. Narcissistic: respond with calm respect and set limits without being drawn into praise or attack.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- To place a case, ask what the person wants from others. Stays away and does not care: schizoid. Stays away but longs for others and fears rejection: avoidant. Wants to be cared for: dependent. Wants admiration: narcissistic. Wants attention: histrionic. Fears abandonment with stormy swings: borderline. Violates others' rights without remorse: antisocial.\n- Nursing care mirrors the problem; see the two care notes for each disorder.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch behavior to the disorder; look-alikes are schizoid vs avoidant (does not want closeness vs wants it but fears rejection) and schizotypal vs schizophrenia (no full psychosis).\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: A = \"Awkward/odd\", B = \"Bold/dramatic\", C = \"Cautious/careful\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Personality Disorders]], [[Schizophrenia]], [[Obsessive-Compulsive and Related Disorders]], [[Nursing Care in Cluster A and Cluster B Personality Disorders]], [[Nursing Care in Cluster C Personality Disorders]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Cluster membership is standard, but check any extra features your reviewer lists.\n- Handout places OCPD with the obsession and compulsion definitions; keep personality disorder separate from OCD.",

"id": "np5-substance-basics",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Substance Use Disorders: Basic Concepts",
"body": "## What it is\nSubstance use disorder is a pattern of use despite harm, with craving, loss of control and impaired function. It ranges from mild to severe.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Tolerance: needing more for the same effect. Dependence or addiction: strong compulsion to use with withdrawal when stopped. Withdrawal: symptoms after stopping or lowering a substance. Intoxication: reversible effects of recent use. Cross-tolerance: tolerance to one drug extends to similar ones. Dual diagnosis: substance use plus another mental disorder.\n- Defenses typical in addiction: denial, rationalization, projection, minimization.\n- Enabling: others protect the person from consequences. Codependency: a relationship pattern centered on the addicted person.\n- Stages of change: precontemplation, contemplation, preparation, action, maintenance (relapse is part of the process).\n- Impaired nurses: report according to policy to protect clients and offer help.\n- Handout terms: intoxication (use that causes maladaptive behavior), withdrawal syndrome (negative reactions when use stops or falls sharply), detoxification (safe withdrawal), substance abuse (use against medical or social norms despite problems in social, work or legal areas), substance dependence (tolerance, withdrawal and failed attempts to stop), blackout (the person functions but has no conscious awareness of the behavior), tolerance, tolerance break (intoxication from a small amount after heavy drinking), spontaneous remission (natural recovery without a program).\n- Causes: biologic (children of alcoholic parents have higher risk; the drug alters neurotransmitters that control pleasure, pain and reward), psychologic (inconsistent parenting, poor role models, lack of nurturing), social (culture, attitudes, peers, laws, cost, availability).\n- Nursing interventions: teach client and family, dispel myths, reduce codependent behavior, refer family, build coping skills, role-play hard situations, stay in the here and now, set realistic goals such as staying sober today.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Substances used, amount, route, last use, withdrawal signs, medical complications, safety, and readiness to change.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat life-threatening intoxication or withdrawal first: airway, breathing, circulation, vital signs. Maslow: physiologic.\n2. Use a non-judgmental, firm approach; confront denial with facts without attacking.\n3. Provide a safe, quiet environment.\n4. Link to treatment, mutual-help groups and family support.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Relapse prevention, triggers, avoid enabling, and use of community resources.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- First ask if the client is in danger from the drug (overdose or withdrawal). Then counseling. Reason: physiologic needs before psychological work.\n- Short-term goals (\"stay sober today\") work better than distant promises.\n- Drug-family notes: [[Alcohol Intoxication and Long-Term Physical Effects]], [[Sedatives, Hypnotics and Benzodiazepines: Intoxication and Withdrawal]], [[Stimulants: Amphetamine, Cocaine and Methamphetamine]], [[Cannabis Use]], [[Opioids: Intoxication, Withdrawal and Naloxone]], [[Hallucinogens: LSD, PCP and Flashbacks]], [[Inhalant Use]], [[Recovery Programs and Drugs Used in Substance Use]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nRecognize denial and enabling; pick the safe medical priority before counseling. Trap: confronting a client who is intoxicated and aggressive.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Downers: Barbiturates and Opioids]], [[Uppers and Hallucinogens]], [[Recovery Programs and Drugs Used in Substance Use]], [[Disulfiram Therapy and Hidden Alcohol Sources]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Philippine drug law and rehabilitation program details: verify local policy.\n- Handout uses \"abuse\" and \"dependence\"; newer manuals combine them as substance use disorder with severity levels.",

"id": "np5-alcohol",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Alcohol Use Disorder",
"body": "## What it is\nProblem drinking with craving, loss of control, tolerance and withdrawal. Alcohol is a central nervous system depressant.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Withdrawal starts hours after the last drink. Early signs: tremor, sweating, anxiety, nausea, rapid pulse, high blood pressure, insomnia. Seizures can occur. Delirium tremens (a medical emergency) brings confusion, vivid hallucinations, severe autonomic arousal and fever, and carries a real risk of death.\n- Wernicke encephalopathy: confusion, ataxia and eye movement problems from thiamine deficiency. Korsakoff syndrome: lasting memory loss with confabulation.\n- Other effects: liver disease, gastritis, pancreatitis, neuropathy, cardiomyopathy, fetal alcohol spectrum disorders.\n- Disulfiram makes alcohol cause a severe reaction; naltrexone and acamprosate reduce craving.\n- Handout withdrawal timeline: symptoms begin 4 to 12 hours after stopping or sharply cutting intake and may last 1 to 2 weeks. Signs: coarse tremor, sweating, high pulse and blood pressure, insomnia, anxiety, nausea or vomiting, transient hallucinations, seizures, delirium tremens. Treatment: benzodiazepines such as lorazepam, chlordiazepoxide, diazepam.\n- Handout long-term effects: cardiomyopathy, Wernicke encephalopathy, Korsakoff psychosis, pancreatitis, esophagitis, hepatitis, cirrhosis, ascites, leukopenia, thrombocytopenia. See [[Alcohol Intoxication and Long-Term Physical Effects]].\n- Thiamine prevents or treats Wernicke and Korsakoff; vitamin B12 and folic acid treat deficiency. Disulfiram: [[Disulfiram Therapy and Hidden Alcohol Sources]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Time of last drink, amount, withdrawal scale scores, vital signs, hydration, nutrition, mental status.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, circulation; monitor vital signs and level of consciousness closely; seizure precautions.\n2. Give withdrawal medications as ordered (benzodiazepines are the usual drug class); give thiamine and replace fluids and vitamins as ordered.\n3. Place in a quiet, well-lit room, reorient, and observe frequently.\n4. After stabilization, motivate for treatment and link to mutual-help groups and family support.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- No alcohol while on disulfiram, including hidden sources (some syrups, sauces and mouthwash); support groups; relapse triggers.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Time since last drink tells you what to expect: hours 4 to 12 means tremor and anxiety; seizures and later delirium tremens are the dangerous stages. Monitor vital signs and level of consciousness most closely early.\n- Reason for benzodiazepines: alcohol and benzodiazepines act on the same calming system, so the drug replaces alcohol and is tapered, preventing seizures. Stimulants are wrong.\n- Counseling and group referral come after the client is medically stable.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nOrder of priority: withdrawal complications before counseling. Identify a time course and symptoms of delirium tremens.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: \"Thiamine before sugar\" for a malnourished drinker; verify with your protocol.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Substance Use Disorders: Basic Concepts]], [[Delirium versus Dementia]], [[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]], [[Alcohol Intoxication and Long-Term Physical Effects]], [[Disulfiram Therapy and Hidden Alcohol Sources]], [[Recovery Programs and Drugs Used in Substance Use]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Withdrawal timeline, and medication choices in your local protocol.\n- Handout lists dialysis and lavage for intoxication; these are for extreme cases only.",

"id": "np5-downers",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Downers: Barbiturates and Opioids",
"body": "## What it is\nDowners (depressants) slow the central nervous system: barbiturates, benzodiazepines and sedative-hypnotics, alcohol, and opioids (narcotics) such as heroin, morphine and similar drugs.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Sedatives and barbiturates: intoxication looks like alcohol (slurred speech, ataxia, drowsy); overdose causes respiratory depression, low blood pressure and coma. Withdrawal can be dangerous (seizures, delirium), so doses are lowered gradually under care.\n- Opioids: intoxication brings euphoria, then drowsiness, constricted (pinpoint) pupils, slow breathing. Overdose is treated with naloxone, an opioid antagonist; the effect may wear off sooner than the opioid, so repeated observation is needed.\n- Opioid withdrawal is uncomfortable, rarely fatal: yawning, runny nose, tearing, dilated pupils, muscle aches, goose flesh, abdominal cramps, diarrhea, sweating, anxiety. Methadone or buprenorphine are used for maintenance or detox; clonidine can ease autonomic signs.\n- Injection use brings risk of HIV, hepatitis and infection.\n- Details by family: [[Sedatives, Hypnotics and Benzodiazepines: Intoxication and Withdrawal]] and [[Opioids: Intoxication, Withdrawal and Naloxone]]. Key contrast from the handout: benzodiazepine overdose is rarely fatal alone (lethargy, confusion), barbiturate overdose can kill through coma, respiratory arrest and cardiac failure.\n- Barbiturate withdrawal must be tapered; stopping suddenly can cause coma and death. Opioid withdrawal needs no drug to protect life; methadone can replace the opioid.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Respiratory rate and depth, level of consciousness, pupils, vital signs, track marks, time of last use.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway and breathing first; support ventilation; give antagonist as ordered for opioid overdose.\n2. Continuous monitoring; do not leave a sedated client alone.\n3. For withdrawal: safety, fluids, comfort, seizure precautions (sedative withdrawal) and medication as ordered.\n4. Then education and referral; teach harm reduction and infection risks.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Pupils help: pinpoint pupils and slow breathing point to opioids, and the answer is airway, then naloxone. Slurred speech and unsteady gait with a depressed state suggests sedatives, where the danger is the airway and, in withdrawal, seizures.\n- Opioid withdrawal is miserable but rarely fatal; sedative withdrawal can kill. So sedative withdrawal gets the higher priority.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch signs to the substance (pinpoint pupils means opioid; dilated pupils in withdrawal). Priority is breathing over everything else.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Substance Use Disorders: Basic Concepts]], [[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey|Primary Survey]], [[Sedatives, Hypnotics and Benzodiazepines: Intoxication and Withdrawal]], [[Opioids: Intoxication, Withdrawal and Naloxone]], [[Recovery Programs and Drugs Used in Substance Use]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Antagonist dosing and observation periods: verify.\n- Handout lists propoxyphene and normethadone among opioids; both are rarely used now.",

"title": "Uppers and Hallucinogens",
"body": "## What it is\nUppers (stimulants) speed up the central nervous system: cocaine, amphetamines and methamphetamine, caffeine and nicotine. Hallucinogens distort perception: LSD, mescaline, psilocybin, phencyclidine (PCP) and cannabis in some effects.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Stimulant intoxication: euphoria, high energy, dilated pupils, fast pulse, high blood pressure, agitation, paranoia, decreased appetite, insomnia. Severe toxicity can bring chest pain, arrhythmia, stroke, seizures, hyperthermia and psychosis that looks like schizophrenia.\n- Stimulant withdrawal (\"crash\"): fatigue, depression, increased appetite, long sleep and craving; suicide risk is a concern.\n- Hallucinogens: vivid hallucinations, altered perception, anxiety or panic (\"bad trip\"), and flashbacks later. PCP can cause violence, strange behavior and pain insensitivity.\n- Cannabis: relaxed mood, red eyes, increased appetite, slowed time sense, anxiety in some; heavy use affects motivation.\n- Details: [[Stimulants: Amphetamine, Cocaine and Methamphetamine]], [[Cannabis Use]], [[Hallucinogens: LSD, PCP and Flashbacks]]. Handout: stimulant withdrawal appears within hours to days, is not life-threatening, is not treated with drugs, and carries depression with suicidal thoughts. Hallucinogens have no withdrawal syndrome but can produce flashbacks.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Vital signs, temperature, cardiac rhythm, mental status, risk of violence or self-harm, hydration.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat life threats first: cardiac and neurologic problems, temperature, airway; seizure precautions.\n2. For a bad trip or agitation, place in a quiet, low-stimulation room, \"talk down\" with calm short sentences and stay with the client; avoid physical restraint if possible.\n3. Observe for suicide during the crash.\n4. Use medication for agitation as ordered.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Risks of mixing substances, how to recognize relapse triggers, available counseling.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Tell the families apart by what the drug does to the body: stimulants speed everything (fast pulse, high pressure, wide pupils), opioids and sedatives slow it. Cardiac rhythm, temperature and seizures lead the priority list for stimulants.\n- For hallucinogen panic, remove stimulation and talk the client down; restraint raises agitation and injury risk.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTell uppers from downers by pupils, pulse and energy. Choose low stimulation for hallucinogen reactions.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Substance Use Disorders: Basic Concepts]], [[Downers: Barbiturates and Opioids]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]], [[Stimulants: Amphetamine, Cocaine and Methamphetamine]], [[Cannabis Use]], [[Hallucinogens: LSD, PCP and Flashbacks]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Which drug class your reviewer places cannabis in; signs by drug may differ in detail.\n- Cannabis is usually classed with its own category; confirm your reviewer's placement.",

"id": "np5-schizophrenia",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Schizophrenia",
"body": "## What it is\nA chronic psychotic disorder with disturbed thinking, perception, emotion and behavior, usually starting in late adolescence or early adulthood. Contributing factors include genetics, brain chemistry (dopamine and other messengers) and stress.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Positive symptoms (added): hallucinations (most often auditory), delusions, disorganized speech and behavior. Respond better to antipsychotics.\n- Negative symptoms (lost): flat affect, alogia, avolition, anhedonia, social withdrawal. Harder to treat; atypical drugs help more.\n- Cognitive symptoms: poor attention, memory and planning.\n- Subtypes in older books: paranoid, disorganized, catatonic, undifferentiated, residual. Catatonia brings stupor, waxy flexibility, mutism, posturing, or excitement.\n- Course: prodromal, active, residual phases. Suicide risk is significant, especially after hospital discharge.\n- Related: schizophreniform, schizoaffective, brief psychotic and delusional disorders differ mainly by duration and mood involvement.\n- Handout core: Bleuler coined the term for lack of integration of the person's functions; thought and perception disturbed for at least six months; diagnosed in late adolescence or early adulthood; main nursing problem is altered thought process.\n- Positive (hard) symptoms: hallucinations, delusions, ambivalence, loose associations, echopraxia, flight of ideas, ideas of reference, perseveration. Negative (soft): alogia, anhedonia, apathy, blunted or flat affect, catatonia, avolition. Hallucinations are the hallmark.\n- Detail on delusions, broadcasting, insertion, clang, blocking: [[Delusion Types and Thought Disturbances in Schizophrenia]].\n- Nursing interventions in the handout: clear communication, promote medication compliance, help with grooming and hygiene, organize behavior, promote social interaction and social skills, promote reality-based perceptions because hallucinations frighten clients.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Command hallucinations, delusion content, suicide and violence risk, self-care, medication adherence, side effects, substance use.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first: assess command hallucinations and potential for harm.\n2. Establish trust with brief, regular, consistent contacts; keep communication simple and concrete.\n3. Do not argue with delusions; reality-orient gently and focus on the feeling underneath.\n4. Reduce stimulation; give a structured routine.\n5. Promote medication adherence and support daily living skills.\n6. For catatonic stupor, meet physical needs (fluids, nutrition, elimination, skin) and protect from injury.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Antipsychotics; see [[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]]. Long-acting injections help with non-adherence. Psychosocial rehabilitation, family education and community programs reduce relapse.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Illness is treatable; keep taking medication even when well; early warning signs of relapse; avoid substances; reduce high-conflict home stress.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Frightened clients with hallucinations need safety and a calm approach first. Then simple words, reality, routine.\n- Positive symptoms respond best to antipsychotics; negative symptoms respond less, so they remain in care plans (hygiene, activity, social skills).\n- Drug monitoring follows [[Antipsychotic Anticholinergic Effects, Clozapine Alert and Client Teaching]] and [[Extrapyramidal Symptoms and Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSeparate positive from negative symptoms, choose a non-argumentative reply to a delusion, and prioritize safety. Trap: pretending to share a delusion.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Disturbances in Perception and Thought]], [[Unusual Speech Patterns]], [[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]], [[Delusion Types and Thought Disturbances in Schizophrenia]], [[Psychotropic Medication Classes: Which Drug for Which Problem]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Newer manuals no longer use the classic subtypes; confirm what your reviewer expects. Philippine mental health program details: verify local policy.\n- Handout says antipsychotics are best taken after meals; many sources say timing is flexible. Verify.",

"id": "np5-antipsychotics",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics",
"body": "## What it is\nAntipsychotics reduce psychosis by blocking dopamine receptors. First-generation (typical) drugs mainly block dopamine and cause more movement side effects. Second-generation (atypical) drugs also affect serotonin and cause fewer movement problems but more metabolic effects.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Typical examples: chlorpromazine (low potency, more sedation and anticholinergic effects) and haloperidol (high potency, more movement side effects). Atypical examples: clozapine, risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, aripiprazole.\n- Extrapyramidal side effects: acute dystonia (muscle spasm, early; treat as an emergency), akathisia (restlessness), parkinsonism (tremor, rigidity, shuffling), tardive dyskinesia (late, involuntary movements of mouth and tongue, may be permanent). Anticholinergic drugs treat the acute ones.\n- Neuroleptic malignant syndrome: fever, severe muscle rigidity, altered consciousness, unstable vital signs; life-threatening. Stop the drug and give urgent care.\n- Other effects: sedation, orthostatic hypotension, dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, photosensitivity, QT changes, weight gain and blood sugar and lipid changes (atypicals).\n- Clozapine: risk of severe drop in white blood cells (agranulocytosis), seizures and myocarditis, so blood counts must be monitored on a schedule set by protocol (verify).\n- Handout drug lists. Typical: haloperidol, chlorpromazine, perphenazine, fluphenazine, thioridazine, mesoridazine, trifluoperazine. Atypical: clozapine, risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, ziprasidone; aripiprazole as a newer generation drug.\n- Uses: primary treatment of schizophrenia, also psychotic depression, acute mania and drug-induced psychosis. EPS come from blocking D2 receptors; first-aid details are in [[Extrapyramidal Symptoms and Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome]].\n- QT alert: droperidol, thioridazine and mesoridazine can lengthen the QT interval and cause dangerous rhythms or arrest.\n- Clozapine: baseline white count and differential before starting, weekly counts during treatment and for 4 weeks after stopping (handout schedule).\n- More detail: [[Antipsychotic Anticholinergic Effects, Clozapine Alert and Client Teaching]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Baseline weight, blood sugar, lipids, vital signs; movement signs using a rating scale; sedation; blood counts when ordered.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Recognize an emergency (dystonia, laryngeal spasm, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, fever with rigidity) and act at once.\n2. Check for orthostatic hypotension; rise slowly.\n3. Monitor metabolic effects and movement symptoms regularly.\n4. Do not stop abruptly; report sore throat or fever with clozapine.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Avoid alcohol, use sun protection, take medication regularly, sip fluids and chew sugarless gum for dry mouth, report muscle stiffness, fever or restlessness.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Rank adverse effects by speed of harm: laryngospasm/dystonia and NMS first, agranulocytosis signs (fever, sore throat) next, QT changes, then tardive dyskinesia screening, then comfort effects (dry mouth, constipation).\n- Atypicals trade fewer movement problems for weight, blood sugar and lipid changes, so those are monitored.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich finding is most urgent (rigidity with fever over a dry mouth), or which drug needs blood count monitoring. Trap: treating dystonia as non-urgent.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Schizophrenia]], [[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Circulation and Shock Overview|Shock]], [[Antipsychotic Anticholinergic Effects, Clozapine Alert and Client Teaching]], [[Psychotropic Medication Classes: Which Drug for Which Problem]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- No doses given; verify monitoring schedules and drug-specific warnings.\n- Handout white-cell schedule for clozapine may be out of date; current protocols change. Thioridazine and mesoridazine are seldom used now.",

"id": "np5-mdd",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Major Depressive Disorder",
"body": "## What it is\nA mood disorder with persistent low mood or loss of interest lasting at least two weeks, with changes in sleep, appetite, energy, concentration, self-worth and thoughts of death. It is a medical illness, not weakness.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Symptoms: sadness, anhedonia, guilt or worthlessness, hopelessness, psychomotor slowing or agitation, fatigue, poor concentration, appetite and weight change, sleep change, suicidal thoughts.\n- Specifiers: with psychotic features, melancholic, atypical (reactive mood, increased sleep and appetite), seasonal, postpartum. Persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia) is milder but lasts years.\n- Causes: low activity of serotonin, norepinephrine and dopamine, genetics, loss, stress, medical illness, some drugs.\n- Suicide risk rises when energy returns but mood is still low, and in the first weeks of antidepressants, especially in youth.\n- Handout criteria: at least 2 weeks of depressed mood or loss of pleasure in nearly all activities, plus other symptoms nearly every day (appetite or weight change, sleep or psychomotor change, low energy, worthlessness or guilt, poor concentration or indecision, suicidal ideas, plans or attempts). Handout lists four additional symptoms.\n- Treatment modalities: ECT, and drugs (tricyclics, MAOIs, SSRIs). See [[Electroconvulsive Therapy]], [[Tricyclic and SSRI Antidepressants: Drug Lists and Nursing Care]], [[MAOIs and Other Antidepressants: Tyramine Diet and Special Risks]].\n- Theories and variants (seasonal, postpartum, dysthymia): [[Mood Disorder Theories and Variants]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Suicidal thoughts, plan, means and intent; self-care; sleep; nutrition; support; substance use.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first. Ask directly about suicide; asking does not plant the idea. If risk is present, stay with the client, remove dangerous objects, and follow suicide precautions with frequent observation.\n2. Meet basic needs: nutrition, fluids, sleep, hygiene.\n3. Use a nonjudgmental, accepting, brief and regular presence; do not push cheerfulness or give advice.\n4. Encourage small, achievable activities; praise effort.\n5. Teach after mood begins to lift; severe depression limits learning.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Antidepressants (see [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]]), psychotherapy (cognitive behavioral, interpersonal), and electroconvulsive therapy for severe, psychotic or suicidal depression or when drugs fail. ECT needs consent, fasting before, and monitoring of recovery, with short-term memory change expected.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Medicines take weeks; continue even when well; report worsening mood; avoid alcohol; signs of relapse; warning signs of suicide.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Suicide check outranks everything. Ask directly, then remove means, observe closely, and stay with the client.\n- Energy often returns before mood lifts, so risk rises early in recovery.\n- Reply to hopeless statements with the feeling, not cheering up; severe depression limits learning, so delay teaching.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPriority of suicide assessment over other tasks, and a reply that acknowledges feelings. Trap: leaving a suicidal client alone, or false reassurance.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]], [[Mood Disorder Theories and Variants]], [[Electroconvulsive Therapy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- ECT details and any minimum-duration criteria; local suicide-prevention resources: verify local policy.\n- Handout says four of the listed symptoms; manuals now require five total. Verify.",

"id": "np5-bipolar",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Bipolar Disorder and Mania",
"body": "## What it is\nA mood disorder with episodes of mania or hypomania and often depression. Bipolar I has full manic episodes; bipolar II has hypomania with major depression. Cyclothymia is a milder, long pattern.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Mania: elevated, expansive or irritable mood, decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, flight of ideas, grandiosity, distractibility, risky behavior (spending, sex), poor judgment, and sometimes psychosis. Hypomania is similar but milder and without major impairment or psychosis.\n- Manic clients burn calories and neglect food and fluids, risking exhaustion.\n- Depressive phases resemble [[Major Depressive Disorder]].\n- Mixed features and rapid cycling can occur.\n- Handout mania: abnormally elevated, expansive or irritable mood lasting about 1 week with at least 3 of: grandiosity, decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, flight of ideas, distractibility, psychomotor agitation, hallucinations. Hypomania lasts about 4 days without impairment or psychosis. Bipolar I has mania or mixed episodes; bipolar II has depression plus hypomania. Dysthymia: 2 years of milder depression; cyclothymia: 2 years of hypomanic and depressive symptoms.\n- Nursing care in the handout: protect the client and others, assess suicidal and violent thoughts, limit behavior and remind the client to respect others' space, clarify the meaning of communication, frequent high-calorie high-protein finger foods, reduce stimulation to promote rest, bedtime routine, matter-of-fact non-judgmental approach, and treat the client with dignity despite inappropriate behavior.\n- Lithium: [[Lithium Therapy and Toxicity]], [[Lithium Therapy: Nursing Responsibilities]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Mood, sleep, energy, nutrition and hydration, safety, impulsivity, psychosis, suicide risk (also high in bipolar), adherence.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety and physical needs first: hydration, calories (finger foods the client can eat while moving), rest.\n2. Lower stimulation: quiet room, few visitors, short calm contacts.\n3. Set firm, consistent limits on intrusive or dangerous behavior, without argument or humor at the client's expense.\n4. Redirect energy to safe physical activity.\n5. Protect from financial or social harm; do not take hostility personally.\n6. Give mood stabilizers as ordered and monitor them.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Lithium and anticonvulsant mood stabilizers, atypical antipsychotics for acute mania; see [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]]. ECT for severe or resistant mania.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Keep routine and sleep, avoid stimulants and alcohol, recognize early warning signs, never stop medication abruptly.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Mania burns energy: nutrition and rest come first because the client will not stop to eat. Finger foods fit a client who cannot sit.\n- Manic clients cannot see their own behavior, so limits come from the nurse, said briefly and calmly.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nChoose finger foods, low stimulation and limit setting for acute mania; distinguish bipolar I from II; priority of dehydration or exhaustion.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: \"DIG FAST\" style mania lists exist; learn your textbook's list or build your own.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]], [[Mood Disorder Theories and Variants]], [[Lithium Therapy: Nursing Responsibilities]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Duration criteria for manic and hypomanic episodes.\n- Handout calls mixed episode and rapid cycling the same; they usually differ.",

"title": "Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers",
"body": "## What it is\nDrug classes used for depression, anxiety and mania, with the monitoring and teaching that exams stress.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- SSRIs (fluoxetine, sertraline, paroxetine, citalopram, escitalopram): first line, fewer side effects. Risks: nausea, sexual dysfunction, insomnia or agitation, bleeding risk, hyponatremia in older clients, and serotonin syndrome (agitation, tremor, sweating, fever, diarrhea, fast pulse) especially when combined with other serotonergic drugs.\n- SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine): can raise blood pressure.\n- Tricyclics (amitriptyline, imipramine): sedation, dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, orthostatic hypotension and heart rhythm effects; dangerous in overdose.\n- MAOIs (phenelzine, tranylcypromine): hypertensive crisis if tyramine foods (aged cheese, cured meats, fermented foods) or interacting drugs are taken; many drug interactions.\n- Atypical agents (bupropion, mirtazapine, trazodone) have their own profiles; bupropion lowers seizure threshold.\n- All antidepressants take weeks to work; never stop suddenly (discontinuation symptoms); watch suicide risk early and in youth.\n- Lithium: narrow safety margin. Toxicity signs include coarse tremor, vomiting, diarrhea, confusion, ataxia. Needs regular blood levels (target range: verify in your textbook), steady sodium and fluid intake, and caution with dehydration, sweating, low-salt diets, NSAIDs and diuretics. Effects on thyroid and kidneys need monitoring.\n- Anticonvulsant mood stabilizers (valproate, carbamazepine, lamotrigine): monitor levels, blood counts, liver function; lamotrigine requires attention to rash.\n- Handout classes: SSRIs replaced tricyclics as first choice (equal efficacy, fewer side effects); TCAs are lethal in overdose, so limit prescriptions in impulsive or depressed clients; MAOIs risk hypertensive crisis; bupropion lowers seizure threshold (handout says about four times other antidepressants). Details: [[Tricyclic and SSRI Antidepressants: Drug Lists and Nursing Care]], [[MAOIs and Other Antidepressants: Tyramine Diet and Special Risks]].\n- Mood stabilizers: lithium, carbamazepine, valproic acid, gabapentin, lamotrigine. Valproic acid can cause liver failure; carbamazepine can cause aplastic anemia and agranulocytosis; lamotrigine can cause Stevens-Johnson syndrome. Lithium above 3.0 mEq/L means dialysis in the handout. See [[Lithium Therapy and Toxicity]].\n- Teaching: normal sodium intake, normal fluid range, avoid heavy sweating on lithium; for sedation, give the larger anticonvulsant dose at bedtime.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Assess suicide risk before and after starting an antidepressant.\n2. Hold and report signs of lithium toxicity or serotonin syndrome; these are emergencies.\n3. Check blood levels and baseline labs as ordered.\n4. Teach diet and drug interactions, especially with MAOIs.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Do not stop medication on your own, avoid alcohol, report new rash, fever, bleeding or severe agitation, and maintain steady salt and fluid intake on lithium.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Rank: serotonin syndrome, hypertensive crisis, lithium toxicity and rash with fever first; suicide risk early in treatment second; comfort effects last.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch adverse effect to drug class, pick the foods to avoid on MAOIs, and recognize toxicity.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[Psychotropic Medication Classes: Which Drug for Which Problem]], [[Tricyclic and SSRI Antidepressants: Drug Lists and Nursing Care]], [[MAOIs and Other Antidepressants: Tyramine Diet and Special Risks]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- No doses or level ranges here; verify all values and monitoring schedules.\n- Handout lists mirtazapine, maprotiline and amoxapine among tricyclics; most books class them separately. Verify bupropion seizure figure.",

"id": "np5-delirium-dementia",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Delirium versus Dementia",
"body": "## What it is\nBoth cause confusion, but delirium is an acute, usually reversible medical emergency, and dementia is a slow, progressive loss of cognition.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Delirium: sudden onset over hours to days; fluctuating course; attention and awareness are impaired; worse at night; disorganized thinking; visual hallucinations and agitation or drowsiness. Causes: infection, drugs, withdrawal, metabolic disturbance, low oxygen, dehydration, pain, urinary retention, surgery.\n- Dementia: gradual onset over months to years; stable day to day until late; attention is relatively preserved early; memory loss first; progressive decline in language, judgment and function; level of consciousness normal until late.\n- A person with dementia can develop delirium on top of it.\n- Depression in older adults (\"pseudodementia\") can mimic dementia: onset is quicker, the client complains of memory loss, and answers \"I don't know\".\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Onset, course, level of consciousness, orientation, attention, vital signs, oxygen, glucose, medications, hydration, and pain.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat delirium as an emergency: look for and treat the cause (oxygenation, infection, glucose, fluids, drugs). Maslow: physiologic.\n2. Ensure safety: prevent falls and wandering; avoid restraints if possible.\n3. Orient gently, use clocks, glasses and hearing aids, keep lights on by day and dark at night, consistent staff and family presence.\n4. Do not argue with confusion; use short, simple statements.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Treat the cause; limited medication for severe agitation; avoid sedatives that worsen confusion where possible.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Sudden confusion means look for a reversible cause before calling it dementia: oxygen, glucose, infection, drugs, retention, pain. Reason: delirium can kill if its cause is missed, while dementia is slow.\n- Safety measures: prevent falls and wandering, avoid restraints, keep orientation cues. Short, calm statements beat arguing.\n- Depression in the elderly can look like dementia; the pseudodementia client complains of memory loss, answers \"I don't know\" and has a faster onset.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nCompare features in a table: onset, course, consciousness, reversibility. Priority is a sudden change in a known dementia client: look for a cause.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: delirium = \"Deadly Dash\" (fast, urgent); dementia = \"Drip\" (slow).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Alzheimer's Disease]], [[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Specific delirium screening tools your school uses.\n- This topic is not in the psychiatric review handouts used for these notes, so all content is general knowledge. Verify with your text.",

"id": "np5-alzheimers",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Alzheimer's Disease",
"body": "## What it is\nThe most common cause of dementia: a progressive degenerative brain disease with plaques and tangles and loss of the neurotransmitter acetylcholine.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Early: short-term memory loss, word-finding trouble, misplacing things. Middle: disorientation, wandering, poor judgment, personality change, agitation, delusions, sleep reversal, \"sundowning\" (worse confusion in the evening). Late: loss of speech, incontinence, difficulty swallowing, immobility, full dependence.\n- Cognition declines gradually, and the course is long. Risk factors: age, family history, vascular risk and head injury.\n- Other dementias: vascular (stepwise), Lewy body (visual hallucinations, fluctuating alertness), frontotemporal (behavior change).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Cognitive and functional level, behavior, nutrition, safety risks, caregiver strain.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety: prevent wandering and falls (identification bracelet, locked exits, clear walkways, supervision); then basic needs.\n2. Keep a stable routine, familiar environment and low stimulation, especially at the end of the day.\n3. Communicate with short, simple, one-step directions, face-to-face, calm tone.\n4. Do not argue or quiz; use validation and redirection.\n5. Support independence as long as safe; preserve dignity.\n6. Support and refer caregivers.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Cholinesterase inhibitors (such as donepezil) may slow symptoms for a time; they can cause nausea, slow pulse and vomiting. Memantine acts on another system. Neither cures the disease. Avoid sedatives when possible.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Plan early for care and legal issues; simplify the home; caregiver respite; expect decline.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Memory loss steals independence in a set order: new learning, then orientation, then self-care, then swallowing and movement. Plan safety for the stage the client is in.\n- Confusion that worsens late in the day (sundowning) is handled by a calm, bright, predictable environment, not by arguing or sedating first.\n- Repeated questions are answered kindly each time. Reason: the question comes from anxiety, not forgetfulness alone.\n- Caregiver strain is a nursing problem too; refer for respite and teaching.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nBest response to repeated questions or sundowning, and safety priorities. Trap: reality-orienting forcefully, or restraining.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Delirium versus Dementia]], [[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]], [[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Stage names (mild, moderate, severe) and drug details; verify local policy on caregiver services.\n- This topic is not in the psychiatric review handouts used for these notes; staging and drug details are general knowledge.",

"title": "Developmental Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nConditions that start in childhood and affect development, learning, communication or behavior. Often called neurodevelopmental disorders.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Autism spectrum disorder: persistent difficulty in social communication and interaction, with restricted, repetitive behaviors and sensory differences. Begins early; severity varies. Routine changes may trigger distress.\n- ADHD: inattention, hyperactivity and impulsivity beginning in childhood, in more than one setting. Stimulant drugs are common first line; they can reduce appetite, delay sleep and affect growth, so weight and height are monitored. Non-stimulants exist.\n- Intellectual disability: limits in intellectual and adaptive function, graded as mild, moderate, severe, profound.\n- Learning, communication and tic disorders; Tourette disorder has motor and vocal tics.\n- Conduct disorder (violating rights of others) and oppositional defiant disorder (defiance toward authority) are behavior disorders; conduct disorder can precede antisocial personality disorder.\n- Separation anxiety disorder: excessive fear of leaving attachment figures.\n- Handout detail on autism, ADHD and intellectual disability is in [[Autism Spectrum Disorder]], [[Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder]], [[Intellectual Disability]] and [[Intellectual Disability: Levels and Care]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Developmental milestones, communication, behavior across settings, safety, family coping.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Ensure safety (impulsive, self-injurious or elopement behavior).\n2. Keep a consistent, structured routine with clear, simple rules, praise and rewards (positive reinforcement).\n3. Use short, concrete language; use visual aids for autism.\n4. Introduce changes gradually; reduce sensory overload.\n5. Support parents; teach behavior strategies; coordinate school.\n\n## Medications and treatments (general terms)\n- Behavioral and educational therapy first; drugs treat targeted symptoms.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Safety first (head banging, impulsivity), then routine, then teaching. A calm, repeated routine lowers anxiety for children who cannot handle change.\n- Reward wanted behavior at once and consistently. Reason: young children and children with limited cognition link consequences only when they are immediate.\n- Match teaching to the child's ability, not age.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSelect structured routine, consistent limits and reward for desired behavior; identify the disorder from a childhood description.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Personality Disorders by Cluster]], [[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[General Psychiatric Disorders]], [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]], [[Autism Spectrum Disorder]], [[Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Diagnostic wording by manual edition; growth and development content is also in NP2. Philippine school and special education services: verify local policy.",

"id": "np5-general-psychiatric",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "General Psychiatric Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nA catch-all review of other psychiatric conditions that appear on exams beside the big topics.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Dissociative disorders: dissociative amnesia (memory loss for personal information after stress), dissociative fugue as a feature (sudden travel and confusion over identity), dissociative identity disorder (two or more distinct identities, with gaps in memory), depersonalization-derealization disorder.\n- Factitious disorder (intentionally producing symptoms to take the sick role) and malingering (for external gain) differ from somatic disorders.\n- Impulse-control disorders: intermittent explosive disorder, kleptomania, pyromania, gambling disorder.\n- Sleep-wake disorders: insomnia, hypersomnia, narcolepsy, sleep apnea, night terrors and sleepwalking.\n- Sexual and gender-related disorders: sexual dysfunctions and paraphilic disorders; gender dysphoria is distress about a mismatch and is not itself an illness of identity. Care must be respectful.\n- Suicide: the leading psychiatric emergency. Risk factors include prior attempts, plan and access to means, hopelessness, substance use and recent loss. Protective: supports, treatment, reasons for living.\n- Grief: normal grief stages (denial, anger, bargaining, depression, acceptance) are not always linear; complicated grief lasts and impairs.\n- Postpartum mood changes: \"blues\" are mild and brief; postpartum depression and psychosis are serious.\n- Detail notes: [[Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care]], [[Malingering, Factitious Disorder and Munchausen Syndrome]], [[Paraphilias]], [[Somatoform Disorders: Gains, Theories and Management]], [[Mood Disorder Theories and Variants]], [[Electroconvulsive Therapy]].\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Safety first (suicide and violence), then symptoms, functioning, stressors, supports.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety: ask directly about suicide; supervise and remove means when risk is present.\n2. Rule out physical and substance causes.\n3. Use accepting, consistent communication; avoid labeling or humiliating.\n4. Treat sleep, nutrition and hydration.\n5. Refer to specialized services and teach coping.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- When a question lists several disorders, ask whether the symptoms are conscious or unconscious, and whether there is a victim. Conscious plus reward: malingering; conscious plus sick role: factitious; unconscious: somatic or conversion. A victim: coercive paraphilia, which also brings reporting duties.\n- Suicide screening and physical causes come before labeling.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nName the disorder, pick the safest first action, and avoid blaming language.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Phobic and Somatic Symptom Disorders]], [[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]], [[Developmental Disorders]], [[Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care]], [[Paraphilias]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Terminology changed between manual editions (for example fugue, gender terms); check your reviewer's wording.",

"id": "np5-er-nursing-issues",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Issues in Emergency Room Nursing",
"body": "## What it is\nEmergency nursing means caring for people with sudden, unplanned, often unclear problems, usually with little history and little time. The issues are the recurring practical, legal and ethical problems that come with this setting.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Overcrowding and long waits: sicker clients must not wait behind less sick ones, which is why triage exists ([[Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage]]).\n- Rapid decisions with incomplete information: treat life threats first, then gather history. A focused history can wait until the airway, breathing and circulation are safe.\n- Consent: in a true emergency where the client cannot decide and no representative is available, treatment to save life or limb is given under implied consent, because delay would cause greater harm. Review materials commonly put it as \"consent is waived in an emergency\". Competent clients can still refuse. See [[Informed Consent]].\n- Normal consent rules otherwise: the client, or for a minor or a mentally ill client the parent or legal guardian, consents; the physician obtains it and the nurse may witness. A signed form is proof.\n- Advance directives: a living will (for example do-not-resuscitate or do-not-intubate wishes) and a health care proxy guide decisions when the client cannot speak ([[Advance Directives]]). A valid DNR order is followed; an unclear one is clarified quickly with the provider.\n- Medico-legal cases (assault, gunshot, stab wound, suspected abuse, poisoning, rape): document objectively in the client's own words, describe wounds without guessing the weapon or the cause, preserve evidence (bag clothing separately in paper, label, avoid cutting through holes), keep chain of custody, and report as required by law.\n- Documentation rule: if it was not charted, it was not done. Chart promptly, fully, legibly and factually; never alter or backdate a record.\n- Violence and burnout: staff safety, security support and stress recovery are real issues.\n- Access to emergency care: many countries restrict turning away or delaying emergency clients over payment or deposits. Philippine rules exist on this; verify local policy.\n- Confidentiality still applies in a busy, open ER: do not discuss clients in corridors or on social media ([[Privacy, Confidentiality and Defamation]]).\n- Unidentified unconscious client: treat first, identify later; check for medical alert bracelets and cards, and keep belongings listed and secured.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Immediate threats to life before anything else; then pain, vital signs, mental status, allergies, medicines, last meal and events leading up to the problem.\n- Safety of the environment, including weapons and aggressive visitors.\n- Signs of abuse or neglect: injuries that do not match the story, delay in seeking care, repeated injuries, a partner who answers for the client.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause: a crowded department with limited staff and incomplete information. Finding: clients of very different urgency arrive together. Priority action: sort by threat to life, treat that first, and explain waits to the others.\n- Why this order: Maslow puts physiological survival before everything else, and an unstable client can die while paperwork is completed. So treatment is never delayed to find a relative, a signature or a deposit.\n- Safety comes before the client: a nurse who is hurt cannot help anyone.\n- Assess before you act, except when the airway, breathing or circulation is failing; there you act and assess together.\n- Evidence is protected without delaying life-saving care: save life first, then preserve evidence as you go.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Ensure scene and staff safety (you cannot help if you become a casualty).\n2. Life threats first using the ABC order ([[Primary vs Secondary Survey]]).\n3. Triage and reassess, since clients change while waiting.\n4. Document clearly, protect evidence, report as required.\n5. Communicate with family calmly ([[Therapeutic Communication]]); involve social support and psychiatric help when needed ([[Crisis and Crisis Intervention|Suicide Risk]]).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nQuestions give a crowded ER and ask who to see first, what to do about an unconscious client with no relatives, how to handle clothing from a stabbing victim, or what to do when relatives demand to know a client's condition. Life threat and safety win; do not delay emergency care to find consent or payment. Traps: waiting for a signature before treating a bleeding unconscious client, washing or discarding a victim's clothing, and giving details to a caller who cannot be identified.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention|Crisis Intervention]], [[Rape, Rape Trauma Syndrome and Survivor Care]], [[Informed Consent]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact Philippine laws on emergency care and deposits, mandatory reporting rules and medico-legal procedure (verify local policy).\n- Evidence-handling details (paper bags, labelling, who may take custody) vary by hospital policy.\n- Consent for a minor in an emergency without a parent: confirm the exact wording your reviewer uses.",

"id": "np5-triage-basics",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage",
"body": "## What it is\nTriage (from the French word for sorting) means ranking clients by how urgently they need care, so the sickest are treated first and resources are used well. It is an ongoing process, not a one-time label.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The purpose is the greatest benefit for the most people, ranked by urgency and not by arrival order or social status.\n- A triage nurse does a fast, focused assessment: chief complaint, airway, breathing, circulation, level of consciousness, vital signs, pain and risk factors. It usually takes a minute or two, not a full workup.\n- Simple three-level systems sort clients into emergent (immediate threat to life or limb), urgent (serious but can wait a short time) and non-urgent (minor, can wait longer). Colour tags (commonly red, yellow, green) often accompany these; exact definitions and waiting times differ by hospital and system, so state only the idea.\n- Typical examples. Emergent: airway obstruction, severe breathing difficulty, cardiac arrest, major bleeding, shock, seizure that is ongoing, severe head injury, burns with airway involvement, chest pain with instability. Urgent: stable fracture, moderate abdominal pain, high fever in an otherwise stable adult, small controlled bleeding. Non-urgent: minor sprains, small cuts, cold symptoms, medication refills.\n- Higher-level systems use five levels ([[CTAS and ESI Triage Scales]]).\n- Triage is repeated: a waiting client who worsens is re-triaged upward. Pain, vital signs and mental status are rechecked at intervals.\n- Do not diagnose and do not send anyone away without screening.\n- Disaster triage is different: it also weighs survivability and resources ([[NATO Disaster Triage System]]); in routine triage the sickest are treated first, whereas in a mass casualty event those unlikely to survive may wait.\n- Hidden high-risk groups who need a lower threshold for escalation: infants and young children, older adults, pregnant women, immunosuppressed clients, and people with heart or lung disease.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Airway noise, work of breathing, skin colour, pulse quality, mental status, bleeding, severe pain, and red-flag complaints such as chest pain, stroke signs, severe injury, poisoning.\n- Mechanism of injury in trauma: a fall from height or a vehicle crash can hide serious injury even when the client looks well.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: a client with an airway, breathing or circulation problem will die in minutes, so that client is seen first; Maslow's physiological needs and the ABC order decide.\n- Within the same category: unstable before stable, acute before chronic, a change from baseline before a long-standing finding.\n- Do not be swayed by noise, tears or arrival time. A quiet, pale, sweaty client with a fast pulse may be sicker than a shouting client with a bleeding finger.\n- A client with a headache who is suddenly drowsy is worse than a client with a severe but unchanged headache: a declining level of consciousness is a red flag.\n- Least invasive first and assess before intervening still apply: triage is assessment, not treatment, unless a life threat must be fixed on the spot (open the airway, press on a bleeding wound).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Look for immediate life threats and move those clients straight to treatment.\n2. Assign the category, then place and monitor the client.\n3. Reassess waiting clients at intervals and when anything changes.\n4. Document the category and reason.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTypical: a list of arriving clients and the question of who is seen first. Pick the one with an airway, breathing or circulation threat, then unstable over stable, then acute over chronic. Traps: the stable but loud or worried client, the one who arrived first, the client with a dramatic but non-life-threatening injury (a deep but controlled cut) versus a quietly deteriorating one, and choosing a client who is probably dead or dying in a disaster question where the best answer is the savable one.\n\n## Related topics\n[[CTAS and ESI Triage Scales]], [[PENA Triage]], [[NATO Disaster Triage System]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey]], [[Issues in Emergency Room Nursing]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact category names, colours and target times used by your school or hospital system.\n- The example lists above are teaching examples, not fixed rules; no handout in your set covers routine triage, so this note rests on standard textbook knowledge.",

"id": "np5-ctas-esi",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "CTAS and ESI Triage Scales",
"body": "## What it is\nCTAS is the Canadian Triage and Acuity Scale and ESI is the Emergency Severity Index. Both are five-level triage tools for emergency departments in which one end means immediate life-saving attention and the other means least urgent.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- CTAS: five levels from most to least urgent: resuscitation (level 1), emergent (2), urgent (3), less urgent (4) and non-urgent (5). Each is linked to a suggested time within which care should start, running from immediate for level 1 to a few hours for level 5 (commonly taught as immediate, 15, 30, 60 and 120 minutes; verify).\n- ESI: five levels, where level 1 is the most urgent. It asks in order:\n  - Step 1: does this client need an immediate life-saving intervention (level 1)? Examples are cardiac arrest, no pulse, severe respiratory distress, unresponsiveness needing airway or drugs.\n  - Step 2: is this a high-risk situation, a confused, lethargic or disoriented client, or someone in severe pain or distress (level 2)?\n  - Step 3: if neither, how many different resources will the client likely need? Two or more (level 3), one (level 4), none (level 5).\n  - Step 4: check vital signs for level 3 clients; danger-zone vital signs (fast pulse, fast breathing, low oxygen saturation) can push the client up to level 2.\n- ESI therefore mixes acuity (how sick) with resource prediction (how much care).\n- Resources in ESI count things like labs, imaging, IV fluids, IV or IM medications, specialist consults and procedures; simple things such as an oral medicine, a prescription refill, a dressing, crutches, a splint, a tetanus shot or a simple wound repair generally do not.\n- Both scales are re-applied when the client changes, and neither replaces clinical judgement.\n- Compared with a three-level system, a five-level scale separates the middle group (urgent but stable) better, which matters in crowded departments.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Stability first, then risk, then expected workload, using vital signs and presenting complaint.\n- Danger signs: altered mental status, severe pain, chest pain with sweating, signs of stroke, uncontrolled bleeding.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: first ask \"will this client die without immediate action?\" (ABC, Maslow physiological); only then ask about risk, and last about workload.\n- The order of the questions is the point: threat to life outranks high risk, which outranks resource count. A client with one test needed (level 4) never beats a confused, lethargic client (level 2).\n- A client who looks well but has abnormal vital signs is not \"stable\"; trust the numbers and trend.\n- Count resources by type, not by number of tests: a blood count and a chemistry panel are one resource (labs); a chest film plus labs are two.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Rule out immediate life threat before using any scale.\n2. Apply the scale, then re-triage if the condition changes.\n3. Document the level.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nRecognise which scale counts levels where 1 is most urgent, and recall that ESI level 1 = needs immediate life-saving intervention. Look-alike traps: confusing a client who is sick but stable (ESI 3 by resources) with one needing immediate action; counting an oral medication or a splint as a resource; treating a lethargic client as level 3.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage]], [[PENA Triage]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact CTAS level names and time targets; which scale your Philippine references emphasise.\n- ESI resource list and the danger-zone vital sign cut-offs vary by age; confirm in your reviewer. No handout in your set covers these scales, so this note rests on standard textbook knowledge.",

"body": "## What it is\nThe outline lists PENA as a triage-related topic under routine emergency department triage. I am not certain what the letters stand for or its exact categories, and none of the review handouts mention it, so this note keeps to what is safe: it belongs with the other ED triage tools and the same principles apply.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- All ED triage tools share a goal: place clients in an urgency category using a quick focused assessment, from immediate life threat to minor problem.\n- Whatever the tool, the top category goes to threats to airway, breathing, circulation or consciousness, and categories are rechecked when the client changes.\n- Triage nurses use clinical judgement; a tool supports but does not replace it.\n- Local hospitals may adopt their own colour or numeric scheme, so always apply the scheme of the facility and not a remembered colour.\n- Most tools you will meet sort into three to five levels; the most urgent level is treated at once and the least urgent may wait for hours.\n- Common triage colours: red for immediate, yellow for urgent or delayed, green for minor. Some systems add blue or grey for expectant and black for the dead; this is used mainly in disasters ([[NATO Disaster Triage System]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- The same basics as any triage: ABCs, level of consciousness, vital signs, pain, mechanism of injury and risk factors.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: if you do not know the tool, fall back on principles. Airway, breathing and circulation threats are first (they kill fastest); unstable before stable; acute before chronic; a change from baseline before an old problem.\n- An exam item about an unfamiliar tool almost always still asks \"who is seen first\"; answer it with ABCs and Maslow rather than guessing a label.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat immediate life threats first, regardless of tool.\n2. Assign the category of the tool in use and document it.\n3. Reassess regularly.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLook at how your own review materials define PENA's categories and memorise those; I cannot confirm them. Expect the same type of question as any triage item: who is seen first. Apply ABCs, then unstable before stable. Trap: memorising a colour or number instead of the reasoning.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage]], [[CTAS and ESI Triage Scales]], [[NATO Disaster Triage System]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- UNKNOWN: what PENA stands for and its exact levels. "id": "np5-primary-secondary-survey",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Primary vs Secondary Survey",
"body": "## What it is\nThe primary survey is the rapid first check that finds and fixes immediate threats to life. The secondary survey is the more complete head-to-toe examination and history done after the client is stabilised.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Primary survey order is ABCDE: Airway (with cervical spine protection in trauma), Breathing, Circulation (with bleeding control), Disability (neurologic status), Exposure (undress and look, while preventing heat loss). Each letter is fixed before moving on.\n- Life threats are treated as they are found; you do not finish assessing and then treat.\n- Secondary survey: full head-to-toe inspection and palpation, complete set of vital signs, history (often remembered as SAMPLE: signs and symptoms, allergies, medications, past history, last meal, events), and diagnostic tests (blood tests, imaging, ECG as ordered).\n- The secondary survey starts only when the primary survey is complete and the client is stable enough. If the client worsens, return to ABC.\n- Scene first: before touching the client, check that the scene is safe, use standard precautions, and note the mechanism of injury (fall, crash, blast, stab) because it predicts hidden injury.\n- In an unresponsive adult without trauma, the sequence is: check response, call for help, check breathing and pulse, then start compressions (circulation first for cardiac arrest) ([[Cardiac Arrest and Basic Life Support Principles]]). In trauma and for the in-hospital primary survey, the sequence stays ABCDE.\n- Back and spine: examine the back by log roll with the neck held in line; this is part of the secondary survey unless bleeding or a wound requires it earlier.\n- Everyone with significant trauma gets oxygen, large-bore IV access, monitoring and warming as part of the primary survey, not as an afterthought.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Primary: responsiveness, patent airway, breathing effort and chest movement, pulse and perfusion, bleeding, consciousness level, hidden injuries.\n- Secondary: every body region, back (by log roll), and the story of the event.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: the body can survive minutes without air, a few minutes of poor circulation to the brain, hours with a broken bone. So the order follows how fast each problem kills: A, then B, then C, then D, then E (Maslow physiological needs and ABC).\n- Assess before intervening: but if the airway is blocked you open it while you look, since assessment of a blocked airway is the finding.\n- Least invasive first: positioning, suction and oxygen before a surgical airway; direct pressure before a tourniquet.\n- Reassess after every intervention: a fixed problem can reappear (swelling closes the airway again) and fixing one letter may expose another.\n- Do not let a dramatic but non-lethal injury (an amputated finger, an open fracture) pull you away from the airway.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Check responsiveness and call for help.\n2. Airway, then breathing, then circulation, because lack of oxygen delivery kills fastest (Maslow physiological needs and ABC).\n3. Disability and exposure.\n4. Reassess ABCs after every intervention.\n5. Only then the secondary survey.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTrap: the client with an impressive wound versus one with a noisy airway. The airway comes first. Another trap: starting a head-to-toe exam or taking a history before the airway is secure. A third: choosing a neurologic check (D) before oxygen and bleeding control. A fourth: taking a full history from a client who is gasping.\n\n## Memory aid\nABCDE is standard; SAMPLE is widely used.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Airway Management in the Primary Survey]], [[Breathing Assessment in the Primary Survey]], [[Circulation and Shock Overview]], [[Disability and Exposure in the Primary Survey]], [[Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some references put catastrophic bleeding first (so the order can read C-ABC); confirm which your exam source uses.\n- Where oxygen and IV access sit in the sequence differs slightly between books.",

"id": "np5-airway",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Airway Management in the Primary Survey",
"body": "## What it is\nAirway assessment checks whether air can move freely from the mouth and nose to the lungs. It is the first priority because a blocked airway causes death in minutes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Signs of obstruction: noisy breathing (stridor, snoring, gurgling), hoarse voice, inability to speak, drooling, use of neck muscles, blue colour, agitation then drowsiness. Silence in a struggling client is a bad sign, not a good one.\n- Causes: tongue falling back in an unconscious client, blood, vomit, foreign body, swelling (burns, allergic reaction, infection), trauma.\n- Sound tells the site: snoring suggests the tongue or soft tissue; gurgling suggests fluid (suction it); stridor suggests narrowing at or above the larynx (very urgent); wheeze suggests lower airway narrowing; hoarseness plus facial burns suggests swelling of the airway.\n- Basic manoeuvres: head-tilt chin-lift for non-trauma; jaw thrust (without head tilt) when a spinal injury is possible.\n- Adjuncts: oropharyngeal airway only in an unresponsive client without a gag reflex; nasopharyngeal airway can be used in clients with a gag reflex but is avoided when facial or skull base fracture is suspected.\n- Suction clears fluids; keep each attempt short and give oxygen around it. Suction only what you can see or hear, and do not suction a conscious client's throat blindly.\n- Definitive airway means a tube in the trachea (intubation) or, when that fails, a surgical airway done by trained providers. A tracheostomy is a planned surgical opening ([[Oxygen Therapy: Safety and Delivery Devices]] covers devices).\n- Burns of the face, singed nasal hairs, soot in the mouth, hoarse voice or carbon-streaked sputum suggest airway burn; swelling worsens over the first 24 to 48 hours, so early airway protection matters ([[Smoke Inhalation and Carbon Monoxide Poisoning]]).\n- Anaphylaxis, angioedema, epiglottitis and neck haematoma can close the airway quickly; do not lay these clients flat or agitate them, and prepare for a difficult airway.\n- A child with drooling, a tripod position and a muffled voice may have epiglottitis: do not examine the throat with a tongue depressor; prepare for expert airway care.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Look, listen and feel for air movement; check the mouth for obstruction.\n- Mental status as a clue: a drowsy client may not protect their own airway; the gag and cough reflexes matter.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: swelling, blood or the tongue narrows the airway -> noise or silence, drooling, cannot speak -> open and protect the airway before anything else, because oxygen cannot reach any organ through a blocked pipe (Maslow oxygenation, ABC).\n- Least invasive first: reposition, jaw thrust, suction, simple adjunct, then intubation, then a surgical airway.\n- Safety: in trauma, move the head and neck as little as possible; use jaw thrust, not head tilt, until the spine is cleared.\n- A conscious client who can speak has a patent airway for now; a client who cannot speak does not. Reassess because swelling progresses.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Open the airway while protecting the neck in trauma.\n2. Clear it (suction, remove visible objects).\n3. Insert an adjunct if needed and give oxygen.\n4. Prepare for advanced airway and call for help.\n5. Recheck after every intervention.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich action first for a snoring unresponsive client: open the airway. Which airway is wrong for a conscious client with a gag reflex: the oral type. Which client first: the hoarse client with facial burns versus one with a painful but stable fracture. Trap: giving oxygen mask first to a client whose airway is still blocked; opening the airway comes first.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Airway Obstruction and Choking]], [[Breathing Assessment in the Primary Survey]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey]], [[Burn Care Phases and Emergency Priorities]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Adjunct sizing and technique, and how current guidelines handle spinal precautions.\n- Whether your reviewer treats a child with epiglottitis differently from the above.",

"id": "np5-breathing",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Breathing Assessment in the Primary Survey",
"body": "## What it is\nOnce the airway is open, the nurse checks whether breathing is effective: enough air moving in and out, enough oxygen reaching the blood.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Assess rate, depth, effort, chest movement symmetry, skin colour, ability to speak full sentences, and oxygen saturation by pulse oximeter. Normal saturation is about 95 percent or higher; a lower value means hypoxaemia. The oximeter can mislead in poor perfusion, carbon monoxide poisoning (it reads falsely normal because carboxyhaemoglobin looks like oxygenated blood), nail polish or cold fingers ([[Chest X-ray and Pulse Oximetry]]).\n- Warning signs: fast or very slow breathing, accessory muscle use, nasal flaring, retractions, cyanosis, restlessness or confusion from low oxygen.\n- Life-threatening chest problems to recognise in the primary survey:\n  - Tension pneumothorax: severe distress, one-sided absent breath sounds, hyperresonance, tracheal deviation away from the injured side, distended neck veins, falling blood pressure.\n  - Open pneumothorax (sucking chest wound): air moves through the wound with each breath.\n  - Flail chest: part of the chest wall moves in the opposite direction on breathing (paradoxical movement), usually after several rib fractures.\n  - Massive haemothorax: blood in the chest causing shock and dullness to percussion.\n- Open chest wound: cover with a dressing taped on three sides (or an occlusive dressing that lets air escape on exhale); sealing all four sides can convert it to a tension pneumothorax. The handout style teaches petroleum gauze secured with tape.\n- Tension pneumothorax needs urgent decompression (needle or chest tube) by a trained provider; do not wait for an X-ray ([[Pneumothorax: Types and Findings]], [[Pneumothorax: Management and Chest Tube Care]]).\n- Give oxygen as ordered or per protocol; support ventilation with a bag-valve-mask if breathing is inadequate. Oxygen devices deliver different concentrations: nasal cannula about 24 to 45 percent at 2 to 6 litres a minute, simple mask 40 to 60 percent, non-rebreather 95 to 100 percent at 10 to 15 litres a minute, Venturi mask a precise set concentration ([[Oxygen Therapy: Safety and Delivery Devices]]).\n- Blood gases: pH 7.35 to 7.45, PaO2 80 to 100 mm Hg, PaCO2 35 to 45 mm Hg, HCO3 22 to 26 ([[Arterial Blood Gas Basics]]).\n- Safety around oxygen: no smoking or open flame, avoid oils and static, cotton blankets.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Look at the chest, listen to both sides, count the rate over a full minute, check oxygen saturation, and note position of comfort.\n- Trachea position, neck veins, chest wall bruising, crepitus under the skin, and whether the chest rises equally.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: air trapped in the pleural space -> one-sided absent breath sounds with falling pressure -> decompress at once, because the pressure squeezes the heart and kills within minutes.\n- Maslow: oxygenation is the first physiological need; ABC: breathing is checked right after the airway is open.\n- Trust the client, not only the number: a normal saturation does not cancel severe distress.\n- Least invasive first: sit up and give oxygen before intubation, but do not delay decompression for a tension pneumothorax.\n- Safety: the open chest wound must never be sealed on all sides.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Confirm the airway is open.\n2. Give oxygen and sit the client up if possible and not contraindicated by injury.\n3. Treat the specific chest injury.\n4. Support breathing mechanically if inadequate.\n5. Monitor saturation and respiratory rate continuously.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nThe question often gives a trauma client with distress and unequal breath sounds. Think tension pneumothorax and act at once. Another pattern: a normal saturation reading but clinical signs of failure; trust the client, not only the number. A third: choose the correct dressing for a sucking chest wound (three sides taped). A fourth: which sign separates tension pneumothorax (tracheal deviation, low pressure) from a simple one.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Respiratory Failure Recognition]], [[Airway Management in the Primary Survey]], [[Circulation and Shock Overview]], [[Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Ventilation rates and oxygen delivery devices; verify against current guidelines.\n- Three-sided versus valved dressing for an open chest wound, and the time to decompress, vary by source.",
