"id": "np5-circulation-shock-overview",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Circulation and Shock Overview",
"body": "## What it is\nIn the primary survey, circulation means checking whether the heart is pumping enough blood to the tissues and whether bleeding is under control. Shock is the state in which tissues do not get enough oxygen-carrying blood flow, and cells begin to fail.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Shock is a perfusion problem, not simply low blood pressure. Blood pressure can still be normal in early shock; hypoperfusion can exist without significant hypotension.\n- Perfusion depends on the pump (heart), the fluid (blood volume) and the container (vessel tone). Shock types match which of these fails ([[Types of Shock]]): hypovolemic and cardiogenic, then the distributive group (neurogenic, anaphylactic, septic).\n- Check pulse rate and quality, skin colour, temperature and moisture, capillary refill, blood pressure, mental status, and urine output (a sensitive sign of kidney perfusion; a common target is about 30 to 50 mL an hour in adults, as taught in the burn handout).\n- External bleeding: direct pressure first, with a pressure dressing over the original dressing; elevate the site above the heart if possible; tourniquet for limb bleeding not controlled by pressure when trained and ordered by protocol.\n- Obtain large-bore IV access and send blood for tests as ordered; type and crossmatch is sent early in major bleeding.\n- Hidden bleeding is common in trauma: chest, abdomen, pelvis and long bones.\n- Bleeding is described by vessel: arterial is bright red and spurts with each heartbeat, venous is dark and flows steadily, capillary oozes ([[Postoperative Hemorrhage and Hypovolemic Shock]]).\n- Perfusion check at the bedside: skin cool, pale and clammy, capillary refill slower than about 2 seconds, thready pulse, restlessness, thirst and scanty urine point to poor perfusion.\n- Shock position (flat, legs raised about 20 degrees and kept straight) is taught for hypovolemic shock after surgery; avoid it with head injury, breathing distress or heart failure ([[Management of Shock]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Rapid weak pulse, cool pale clammy skin, delayed refill, anxiety or restlessness, falling urine output, low or falling blood pressure.\n- Source of bleeding, neck veins, heart and lung sounds, and temperature.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: lost volume or failing pump -> fast weak pulse, cool skin, low urine -> stop the loss or support the pump, restore flow and monitor. The urine, mind and skin show perfusion before the blood pressure falls.\n- Why this order: bleeding control comes with circulation because every drop lost reduces oxygen delivery; Maslow places oxygenation and circulation before comfort, and ABC places C after A and B because blood is useless without oxygen.\n- Safety: wear gloves; never remove an impaled object in the field.\n- Least invasive first: pressure, elevation and fluids before surgery.\n- Acute before chronic, unstable before stable: choose the client with signs of shock over one with a stable abnormal value.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Control obvious bleeding.\n2. Give oxygen, establish IV access, begin monitoring.\n3. Identify and treat the cause ([[Management of Shock]]).\n4. Keep the client warm and reassess continuously.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTrap: waiting for low blood pressure to call it shock. Early signs (tachycardia, restlessness, cool skin) come first ([[Early vs Late Stages of Shock]]). Another: choosing a fluid bolus for a cardiogenic client whose lungs are already wet. Another: rank which postoperative or trauma client is most urgent when one has a rising pulse and falling urine. Another: choose pressure over a tourniquet as the first step.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Types of Shock]], [[Early vs Late Stages of Shock]], [[Management of Shock]], [[Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition]], [[Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic]], [[Fluid Volume Deficit (Hypovolemia)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Target blood pressure goals, tourniquet protocols and fluid choices in trauma.\n- Capillary refill threshold (about 2 seconds) and urine output goals differ by source and age.",

"id": "np5-types-of-shock",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Types of Shock",
"body": "## What it is\nShock is grouped by what fails: volume, pump, vessel tone or obstruction to flow. Knowing the type tells you the treatment. Many review handouts teach three main groups (hypovolemic, cardiogenic, distributive) and sometimes list obstructive separately.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Hypovolemic: too little blood or fluid. Causes: haemorrhage, severe burns (plasma leak), vomiting and diarrhoea, dehydration, fluid loss from fistulas, massive third spacing. Signs: fast pulse, low pressure, cool pale skin, low urine, flat neck veins. Treatment: stop the loss and replace volume (fluids, blood, plasma, electrolytes) ([[Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic]]).\n- Cardiogenic: the heart cannot pump (myocardial infarction, severe arrhythmia, heart failure, tamponade in some lists). Signs: low pressure with signs of congestion such as crackles and distended neck veins. Goals: limit further heart damage, improve contractility and lower the heart's workload (afterload).\n- Distributive (vessels dilate, so the container is too big; fluid has not been lost) ([[Distributive Shock: Neurogenic, Anaphylactic and Septic]]):\n  - Septic: infection-driven, usually gram-negative toxins leak plasma into tissue; often warm flushed skin early, then cool. Treat by finding and removing the infection.\n  - Anaphylactic: severe allergy with histamine release, swelling, wheeze, hives, collapse. Remove the allergen, give epinephrine first, then support airway and give antihistamine as ordered.\n  - Neurogenic: loss of sympathetic tone below a spinal cord lesion; low pressure with a slow pulse and warm dry skin. Treat by stabilising the spine, positioning and restoring tone ([[Spinal Cord Injury]]).\n- Obstructive: flow blocked outside the heart (tension pneumothorax, cardiac tamponade, massive pulmonary embolism). Signs depend on cause; neck veins are distended with muffled heart sounds in tamponade.\n- Distinguishing cues: flat neck veins and cool skin = volume lost; wet lungs and distended veins = pump; warm skin and normal-to-high heart rate with fever = septic; slow pulse after spinal injury = neurogenic; hives, wheeze and swelling after an exposure = anaphylactic.\n- Spinal anaesthesia can cause a neurogenic-type fall in pressure; raising the head of the bed to about 30 degrees is taught to stop the anaesthetic spreading upward.\n- Mechanism summary: hypovolemic loses blood or plasma, cardiogenic loses pumping power, distributive loses vessel tone.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Pulse, blood pressure, skin temperature and colour, neck veins, breath sounds, history of trauma, infection, allergen exposure, heart disease.\n- Urine output and mental status for every type.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: first decide which of the three things (fluid, pump, container) has failed, then fix that one.\n- Treat life threats in ABC order whatever the type; the treatment that follows depends on the type. Giving a big fluid bolus to a cardiogenic client worsens congestion, while withholding fluid from a bleeding client kills.\n- Anaphylaxis is the one type where a single drug (epinephrine) is the first action because the airway and vessels fail within minutes.\n- Safety: stop the antigen (stop the infusion of the suspected drug), keep the spine aligned, avoid moving a client with suspected neck injury.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Support ABCs for any type.\n2. Match treatment to type: volume for hypovolemia, heart support for cardiogenic, epinephrine for anaphylaxis, antibiotics and fluids for sepsis, relief of the obstruction for obstructive.\n3. Monitor response.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA short vignette gives a clue (slow pulse after spinal injury, hives after a bite, wet lungs after a heart attack) and asks for the type or the first action. The slow pulse in shock is the neurogenic clue. Another pattern: a postoperative client who becomes restless with a rising pulse (think bleeding and hypovolemia).\n\n## Memory aid\nPump, volume, pipes, plug (my own invention): cardiogenic, hypovolemic, distributive, obstructive.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Circulation and Shock Overview]], [[Management of Shock]], [[Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition]], [[Vasopressors for Hypotension and Shock]], [[Burn Pathophysiology and Whole-Body Response]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some books list obstructive shock separately and some fold it into cardiogenic (the handout lists tamponade under cardiogenic); follow your reviewer.\n- Septic shock is described in the handout as caused by gram-negative infection; other organisms can also cause it.",

"emergency",
"shock"

"1. Types of Shock"

"id": "np5-early-late-shock",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Early vs Late Stages of Shock",
"body": "## What it is\nShock is described as a progression: the body compensates at first, then fails. Recognising the early stage allows treatment before damage becomes irreversible.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Early (compensated) stage: body protects the brain and heart by releasing stress hormones and tightening vessels. Signs: anxiety or restlessness, fast pulse, fast breathing, cool pale skin, delayed capillary refill, thirst, reduced urine output, normal or near-normal blood pressure, narrowing pulse pressure (the diastolic number rises toward the systolic).\n- Middle (progressive or decompensated) stage: compensation fails. Signs: falling blood pressure, weak thready pulse, confusion, low urine output, mottled skin, metabolic acidosis (lactic acid builds up) ([[Metabolic Acidosis]]).\n- Late (irreversible or refractory) stage: organs are failing and may not recover even if blood pressure is restored. Signs: profound hypotension, unresponsiveness, anuria, organ failure ([[DIC and Multiple Organ Dysfunction Recognition]]).\n- Hypotension is a late sign in most adults. Children can maintain blood pressure until very late; use other signs.\n- Exception: neurogenic shock can show low pressure early, with a slow rather than fast pulse.\n- Earliest changes are often behavioural: restlessness, anxiety, a feeling of doom, new confusion in an older adult. These are the first sign of poor brain perfusion.\n- Urine output below about 30 mL an hour in an adult signals poor kidney perfusion; this is why a urinary catheter and hourly output are used ([[Fluid Volume Deficit (Hypovolemia)]]).\n- Postoperative bleeding example from the handout: hypotension, rapid thready pulse, disorientation, restlessness, low urine, cold pale skin, and apprehension that marks the early phase.\n- Burn shock follows the same course: increased capillary leak lowers volume, then cardiac output falls and blood pressure drops ([[Burn Pathophysiology and Whole-Body Response]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Trend of pulse, mental status, skin, urine output and blood pressure, not one isolated reading.\n- Heart rate rising before blood pressure falls; compare with the client's baseline.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: volume or flow falls -> the body clamps down on skin, gut and kidneys to save brain and heart -> cool skin, low urine and fast pulse appear before pressure drops. Act at this stage, because treatment works best before cells die.\n- Why act early: organ damage (kidney, gut, liver, brain) happens while the pressure still looks acceptable; waiting for hypotension means waiting for decompensation.\n- Assess before intervening, but in shock the \"assessment\" is a quick trend check, and the intervention (oxygen, access, stop the cause) starts at once.\n- Safety: older adults and clients on beta-blockers may not mount a fast pulse; do not rely on heart rate alone.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat on early signs; do not wait for hypotension.\n2. Report changes promptly and reassess often.\n3. Begin treating the cause ([[Management of Shock]]).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nVignettes describe restlessness and a fast pulse with normal pressure and ask what this means or what to do first. The answer is early shock: act now. Another trap is reading a normal pressure as reassuring. Another is picking the client with the lowest pressure over the one with a rising pulse and falling urine who is still deteriorating.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Circulation and Shock Overview]], [[Types of Shock]], [[Management of Shock]], [[Postoperative Hemorrhage and Hypovolemic Shock]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Stage names (compensated, progressive, irreversible versus initial, compensatory, progressive, refractory) vary by book.\n- Pulse pressure and urine output cut-offs are standard teaching but check your reviewer's numbers.",

"id": "np5-shock-management",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Management of Shock",
"body": "## What it is\nShock management restores tissue oxygen delivery by treating the cause, supporting ABCs and watching response.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- All shock: airway and breathing first, oxygen, IV access, cardiac monitoring, keep warm, frequent vital signs, urine output monitoring (an indwelling catheter may be ordered). Severe shock may need a central line.\n- Hypovolemic: stop the bleeding, replace volume with ordered IV fluids or blood products. Balanced crystalloids such as lactated Ringer's are commonly used first; whole blood, plasma and colloid are used when ordered ([[IV Solutions: Isotonic, Hypotonic and Hypertonic]]). Exact volumes and fluid choices come from orders and current guidelines; I do not give numbers.\n- Hypovolemic from burns: lactated Ringer's calculated by a formula (Parkland: 4 mL x kg x percent burned, half in the first 8 hours, half over the next 16), then adjusted to urine output of about 30 to 50 mL an hour ([[Burn Fluid Resuscitation: Parkland and Brooke Formulas]]).\n- Postoperative hypovolemic shock: replace volume, give oxygen, keep warm, apply pressure to a visible bleeding site, and expect return to the operating room if bleeding is internal ([[Postoperative Hemorrhage and Hypovolemic Shock]]).\n- Cardiogenic: treat the heart problem; fluids can worsen congestion, so the provider chooses carefully. Drugs that improve pump function or lower strain may be ordered.\n- Distributive: septic needs early antibiotics and fluids; anaphylactic needs epinephrine as the first drug (it narrows vessels and opens airways), then airway support and an antihistamine such as diphenhydramine as ordered; neurogenic needs fluids and sometimes vasopressors, and spine protection.\n- Obstructive: relieve the obstruction (decompression, drainage of tamponade, treatment of embolism).\n- Vasopressors raise vessel tone; they require close monitoring and a reliable central or large vein, and an extravasation can destroy tissue ([[Vasopressors for Hypotension and Shock]]).\n- Positioning: lying flat with legs raised about 20 degrees and kept straight (the shock position) is a common teaching for hypovolemic shock; this is not suitable for every client (for example head injury, breathing distress, cardiogenic congestion). Verify local teaching.\n- Signs that treatment is working: stable vital signs, palpable peripheral pulses, urine output in the target range, clear thinking, warmer skin.\n- Warn about overload: crackles, rising neck veins or dyspnea during fluids mean stop and report.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Blood pressure and mean arterial pressure trend, pulse, mental status, skin, urine output, lactate and gases if ordered, signs of fluid overload.\n- Intake and output, weight and haemoglobin trend in bleeding.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: find which type of shock you face, because the treatments pull in opposite directions (fluids help hypovolemia, harm cardiogenic failure).\n- Order: ABC and oxygen -> stop the cause (bleeding, allergen, infection, obstruction) -> restore perfusion -> monitor. Controlling the cause before pouring fluid in matters, since fluid poured into an uncontrolled bleed is lost again.\n- Maslow: oxygenation and circulation first. Safety: stop the offending drug or transfusion at once and keep the vein open with saline.\n- Least invasive first: oxygen, positioning and peripheral IV fluids come before invasive lines and vasopressors.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, oxygen.\n2. Control cause (bleeding, allergen, infection, obstruction).\n3. Establish access and start ordered therapy.\n4. Monitor response and complications.\n5. Prepare for transfer to higher care.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich first for anaphylaxis (epinephrine, after airway readiness). Which fluid caution for cardiogenic shock. Which finding shows improvement (rising urine output, clearer mentation). Which position for shock (legs raised) and when it is wrong. Trap: giving a drug for blood pressure before controlling bleeding or opening the airway.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Types of Shock]], [[Early vs Late Stages of Shock]], [[Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition]], [[ICU Monitoring Concepts]], [[Blood Transfusion Reactions]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Drug names, doses, fluid volumes and targets; verify against current guidelines and orders.\n- Whether your reviewer teaches the legs-raised position at 20 degrees or strict flat.",

"title": "Disability and Exposure in the Primary Survey",
"body": "## What it is\nDisability is a quick check of neurological status. Exposure means removing clothing to find hidden injuries, then covering the client again to prevent heat loss.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Disability tools: AVPU (Alert, responds to Voice, responds to Pain, Unresponsive) is the quickest. The Glasgow Coma Scale scores eye opening (1 to 4), verbal response (1 to 5) and motor response (1 to 6); the lowest total is 3 and the highest is 15, with lower scores meaning deeper impairment. Review handouts call 15 fully alert and oriented, below 7 comatose, and 3 deep coma ([[Level of Consciousness and Glasgow Coma Scale]]).\n- Motor scores: obeys commands 6, localises pain 5, withdraws from pain 4, abnormal flexion (decorticate) 3, abnormal extension (decerebrate) 2, none 1. Verbal: oriented 5, confused 4, words only 3, sounds only 2, none 1. Eye opening: spontaneous 4, to sound 3, to pressure 2, none 1.\n- Check pupils for size, equality and reaction; unequal or fixed dilated pupils suggest raised intracranial pressure or brain injury.\n- Check blood glucose in any client with altered consciousness, since low glucose is a fast, reversible cause.\n- A drop in consciousness can also come from low oxygen or poor perfusion, so recheck A, B and C before blaming the brain.\n- Suspected spinal injury: immobilise on a firm flat surface with the neck in line, move the client as a unit (log roll), do not try to realign body parts; respiratory function is the first priority in a high cervical injury ([[Spinal Cord Injury]]).\n- Exposure: remove clothing as needed, inspect front and back (log roll with spine care), then cover with warm blankets. Hypothermia worsens bleeding and acidosis; keep the room warm and use warmed fluids when available ([[Body Temperature: Heat Loss, Fever and Hypothermia]]).\n- Maintain dignity and privacy; cut clothing rather than pulling it over an injured limb, and keep belongings listed.\n- Nursing care for seizure, poisoning or overdose also starts with ABCs, then glucose, then cause.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Level of consciousness, orientation, pupils, movement of limbs, glucose, injuries, body temperature.\n- Signs of raised intracranial pressure: worsening headache, vomiting, falling level of consciousness, unequal pupils, slowing pulse with rising blood pressure.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: a drop in consciousness is a symptom, not a diagnosis. The brain fails first from lack of oxygen or blood, so exclude hypoxia, shock and low glucose, then look for the brain cause.\n- Why D comes after C: a brain cannot be fixed until it receives oxygenated blood.\n- Safety: protect the spine before any move; keep the airway protected in a drowsy client.\n- Least invasive first: glucose check and oxygen before sedatives or CT; avoid sedating a client whose neuro status you need to track.\n- A falling GCS by 2 or more points, or a new unequal pupil, is an emergency report.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Complete ABC first.\n2. Assess AVPU or GCS and pupils.\n3. Correct low glucose per order.\n4. Expose, examine, then warm and cover.\n5. Repeat neuro checks so changes are detected early.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTrap: attributing confusion to head injury when the client is hypoxic or hypotensive. Another: leaving a trauma client uncovered after the exam. Another: picking GCS component scores or the lowest possible total (3). Another: choose the first action for an unconscious client found down (airway, then breathing and circulation, then glucose).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary vs Secondary Survey]], [[Circulation and Shock Overview]], [[ICU Monitoring Concepts]], [[Level of Consciousness and Glasgow Coma Scale]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact GCS component scores and thresholds your exam source uses (the handout also mentions an updated GCS-P).\n- Use of glucose in an unconscious client: confirm the order and route your reviewer teaches.",

"id": "np5-burns-causes",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Burns: Causes and Overview",
"body": "## What it is\nA burn is tissue injury from heat, chemicals, electricity or radiation. The damage depends on the agent, how hot or strong it is, and how long it touched the skin. Review handouts define it as destruction of skin layers with loss of fluid and electrolytes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Thermal: flame, scald (hot liquid or steam, common in children), contact with hot objects, flash. This is the most common type.\n- Chemical: acids and alkalis (tissue contact, ingestion or inhalation); damage continues until the chemical is removed or diluted, and the body can absorb enough to cause systemic poisoning.\n- Electrical: heat generated as current passes through the body; a small skin wound can hide deep damage along the current path, with entry and exit wounds; risk of heart rhythm problems and muscle breakdown. Alternating current is considered more dangerous than direct current because it can cause cardiac arrest, ventricular fibrillation, strong muscle contractions and fractures of long bones or vertebrae ([[Burn Types: Thermal, Chemical, Electrical and Radiation]]).\n- Radiation: sunburn (UV), radiotherapy, x-rays and radioactive sources.\n- Inhalation injury can accompany any fire burn and may be the deadliest part: facial burns, singed nasal hair, soot in sputum, hoarseness, stridor, wheeze, swelling of the mouth and throat. Carbon monoxide binds haemoglobin about 200 times more strongly than oxygen and gives cherry-red colour, headache, weakness and confusion ([[Smoke Inhalation and Carbon Monoxide Poisoning]], [[Airway Management in the Primary Survey]]).\n- Risk groups: children, older adults, people with disability, alcohol use, smoking in bed, unsafe cooking and electrical wiring.\n- Burn severity depends on depth ([[Degrees of Burns]]), size ([[Rule of Nines and Burn Size Estimation]]), location (face, hands, feet, genitals, joints, circumferential burns are more serious), age, other injuries and health problems. Remember the SCALD aid: Size, Cause, Age, Location, Depth ([[Burn Size, Depth and Severity Categories]]).\n- Local response has three zones: coagulation (centre, dead tissue), stasis (poor blood flow, can be saved or can die) and hyperaemia (outer, tends to recover). Good early care aims to save the zone of stasis ([[Burn Pathophysiology and Whole-Body Response]]).\n- Whole-body effects of a large burn: capillary leak, fluid loss, rising haematocrit, shock, increased metabolic rate (up to about three times normal), suppressed immunity and slowed gut.\n- Suspected abuse should be considered when the burn pattern does not match the story (for example sharply outlined, stocking or glove scalds).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Cause and time of injury (fluid formulas count from the time of injury), enclosed space or smoke, clothing and jewellery, tetanus status, other injuries, breathing, burn depth and size.\n- Signs of airway burn and carbon monoxide poisoning, and the pattern of the injury.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: heat damages skin and airway -> fluid shifts out of vessels (first day) -> shock and airway swelling kill first, infection later. So early priorities are airway, breathing, circulation, then fluid, then wound.\n- Maslow: oxygenation before pain relief or wound care; safety before everything (stop the burning, protect yourself from current and chemicals).\n- Assess before intervening, but stopping the burning process is the first intervention.\n- Large or deep burns are the unstable ones; small superficial burns are stable and come later.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Stop the burning process and ensure scene safety.\n2. Airway, breathing, circulation first, as in any trauma ([[Primary vs Secondary Survey]]).\n3. Cool or irrigate as appropriate ([[Burn First Aid, RACE and PASS]]).\n4. Assess and estimate the burn, then follow burn centre or hospital protocol.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich burn type needs prolonged flushing (chemical), which hides damage (electrical), which first priority in a fire victim (airway), which type of current is more dangerous (alternating), which sign suggests carbon monoxide (cherry-red colour, headache). Trap: choosing wound care or pain relief before checking the airway in an enclosed-space fire.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Phases of Burn Care]], [[Burn First Aid, RACE and PASS]], [[Other Burn Management: Fluids, Airway and Complications]], [[The Burned Child and the Seven Phases of Burn Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Transfer criteria to a burn centre; electrical injury monitoring details.\n- Metabolic rate rise (about three times) and the 200 times carbon monoxide affinity are handout figures; confirm with your textbook.",

"id": "np5-burns-degrees",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Degrees of Burns",
"body": "## What it is\nBurn depth is described by how many layers of skin are damaged. Older books use degrees; newer ones use thickness terms. Depth decides pain, healing time and whether grafting is needed.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- First degree (superficial): only the outer layer, possibly part of the dermis. Red, dry, painful, tingling, blanches with pressure, minimal swelling, no or few blisters; heals within about a week without scarring (sunburn). Cooling soothes the pain. Some review handouts label this \"superficial partial thickness\".\n- Second degree (partial thickness):\n  - Superficial partial: red, moist, blisters, very painful, usually heals within a few weeks.\n  - Deep partial: epidermis, upper dermis and part of the deeper dermis; mottled red base, weeping surface, swelling, blisters, painful and sensitive to cold air; heals in about 2 to 4 weeks with some scarring and colour loss; slower healing means scarring risk, contractures and possible grafting. Infection can convert it to full thickness.\n- Third degree (full thickness): epidermis, entire dermis and sometimes fat, and may reach muscle and bone. Dry, pale white, leathery or charred, little or no pain in the burned area because nerve endings are destroyed (surrounding areas may hurt). Signs of shock, blood in urine and red cell breakdown can accompany large burns. Eschar sloughs; grafting is necessary and scarring, loss of contour and contractures follow.\n- Fourth degree (in some classifications): reaches muscle, tendon or bone.\n- Burns are often mixed; depth can deepen over the first days, especially if infected or poorly perfused.\n- Electrical burns may show only small entry and exit wounds while deep tissue is damaged.\n- Depth, size, location and age together give the severity ([[Burn Size, Depth and Severity Categories]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Colour, moisture, blisters, capillary refill, sensation, pain; circumferential burns that squeeze limbs or the chest (check circulation, and breathing for the chest).\n- Signs of compartment pressure: severe pain out of proportion, tense swelling, pale or cool extremity, weak pulses, numbness or tingling.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: deeper burn destroys nerves and dermis -> less pain, dry leathery surface, no regrowth -> needs surgery and has the highest fluid, infection and scarring risk.\n- Priority: airway and circulation outrank depth. Among burns, the deep, large or circumferential burn is most urgent: limb circulation may need escharotomy ([[Burn Wound Dressing, Escharotomy and Fasciotomy]]).\n- Safety: protect new tissue from friction and infection.\n- Least invasive first: cooling, cover and pain control before debridement and grafts.\n- The most painful burn is not the deepest; do not use pain to rank severity.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat ABCs and airway first.\n2. Describe the burn depth and size accurately and document with the time.\n3. Protect from contamination; manage pain.\n4. Monitor circulation to burned limbs; report loss of pulses promptly.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTrap: assuming the least painful burn is the mildest. A painless, leathery area is the deepest (full thickness). Another: assuming blisters mean first degree. Another: which depth heals without scarring (first degree) and which needs grafting (third degree, some deep partial). Another: which depth is described as weeping and blistered with a mottled red base (second).\n\n## Memory aid\nThink \"deeper means drier and less painful\" (my own shortcut).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Rule of Nines and Burn Size Estimation]], [[Skin Grafting]], [[Burns: Causes and Overview]], [[Burn Wound Closure: Autograft, Allograft and Xenograft]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Whether your textbook uses degree or thickness terms (the handout calls first degree superficial partial thickness and second degree deep partial thickness, which differs from many books); exact descriptions of deep partial burns.",

"id": "np5-burns-phases",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Phases of Burn Care",
"body": "## What it is\nBurn care is organised in three overlapping phases, each with different main problems. Names vary slightly between books (resuscitative or emergent, acute or intermediate or diuretic, rehabilitation or convalescent).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Emergent (resuscitative) phase: from injury through the first 24 to 48 hours, until fluid resuscitation is complete. Capillaries leak, so fluid moves out of the vessels into tissue; the main dangers are hypovolemic shock, airway swelling and inhalation injury, low urine output. Findings: third spacing and swelling, hypotension, fast pulse, oliguria, thirst. Labs: high potassium early (cell breakdown), low sodium, high haematocrit (haemoconcentration), metabolic acidosis (bicarbonate loss). Goal: prevent shock and protect vital organs.\n- Acute (intermediate, diuretic) phase: begins when the client is stable and fluid starts returning to the vessels (about 2 to 5 days after injury) and lasts until wound closure. Findings: diuresis, increased urine output, falling haematocrit (dilution). Labs: potassium falls as it shifts back into cells (hypokalaemia), sodium stays low. Main concerns: infection, wound closure and grafting, nutrition, pain, anaemia, and fluid overload as fluid returns to circulation. Some handouts also list hypertension at this stage.\n- Rehabilitation (convalescent) phase: begins with wound closure and continues for months or longer. Focus: scar and contracture prevention, mobility, pressure garments (as ordered), physical and occupational therapy, reconstruction, emotional and body-image recovery, return to work and school.\n- A seven-step view (rescue, resuscitate, retrieve, resurface, rehabilitate, reconstruct, review) is also taught ([[The Burned Child and the Seven Phases of Burn Care]]).\n- Fluid formulas count from the time of injury, not arrival ([[Burn Fluid Resuscitation: Parkland and Brooke Formulas]]).\n- The exact timing of each phase differs by book; verify.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Emergent: airway, breathing sounds, urine output, blood pressure, pulse, burn extent. Acute: wound appearance, temperature, signs of infection, weight, intake. Rehab: range of motion, scar, coping.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: leaky capillaries (emergent) -> low volume, shock, high potassium -> airway and fluids first. Later the fluid returns -> diuresis, dilution, low potassium -> watch for overload and infection.\n- Why this order: in the first day the client dies of shock and airway loss, not infection; Maslow and ABC place oxygenation and circulation first.\n- Potassium trap: high in the emergent phase (cells burst and kidneys make little urine), low in the acute phase (diuresis and shift into cells).\n- Assess before intervening; least invasive first: cool and cover, then fluids, then surgery.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Emergent: protect airway and restore fluid volume.\n2. Acute: prevent infection, support healing, nutrition and pain control.\n3. Rehab: positioning, exercise, splints, counselling.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich finding to expect and which complication to watch in each phase; expect hyperkalaemia early; expect fluid mobilisation, diuresis and low haematocrit later. Trap: choosing wound care first in the emergent phase.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Other Burn Management: Fluids, Airway and Complications]], [[Pain, Infection and Nutrition in Burns]], [[Skin Grafting]], [[Burn Care Phases and Emergency Priorities]], [[Burn Pathophysiology and Whole-Body Response]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Timing of each phase and whether acute-phase sodium is low or normal (the handout says low in both emergent and acute phases); acute-phase blood pressure statement.\n- Phase names vary by book.",

"id": "np5-burns-first-aid",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Burn First Aid, RACE and PASS",
"body": "## What it is\nBurn first aid stops the burning, protects the airway, cools the injury and gets help. Fire safety acronyms (RACE, PASS) are tested alongside it.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Burning clothing: stop, drop and roll (or smother with a blanket, rug or coat, starting at the head); do not run.\n- Remove the person from the source; remove smouldering clothing and jewellery before swelling begins, but do not pull off fabric stuck to the skin; soak adherent clothing with cool water.\n- Cool thermal burns with cool running water. Do not use ice directly, butter, ointments or home remedies; very cold treatment can worsen tissue damage and cause hypothermia in large burns.\n- Chemical burns: brush off dry powder first, then flush with plenty of running water (a shower is ideal); remove contaminated clothing; flush the eyes at once with cool clean water.\n- Electrical burns: make sure power is off before touching the person; note the victim's position and likely entry and exit sites; maintain airway and check heart rate and rhythm.\n- Smoke inhalation: get to fresh air, ensure a patent airway, give oxygen.\n- Cover with a clean dry sheet or non-stick dressing; this reduces contamination, keeps warmth and lowers pain. Keep a large-burn client warm; start an IV if possible and transport at once.\n- RACE: Rescue (those in danger), Alarm (activate), Confine (close doors and windows) and Extinguish or Evacuate (the handout uses Extinguish).\n- PASS: Pull the pin, Aim at the base of the fire, Squeeze the handle, Sweep side to side.\n- Fire extinguisher types: water for ordinary combustibles, carbon dioxide or dry chemical for electrical and flammable liquids; do not use water on an electrical or grease fire.\n- Oxygen areas: turn off oxygen and remove clients when a fire starts near it; keep no-smoking signs.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Scene safety, airway and breathing, extent of burn, other injuries, time of burn, cause.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: continuing heat keeps burning tissue, so stop the source; then airway, because swelling and smoke injury close it; then cool and cover.\n- Safety first: switch off the current before touching an electrical victim, or you become a second casualty.\n- In a hospital fire the order is protect people in immediate danger (rescue), then raise the alarm, then confine, then extinguish or evacuate.\n- Least invasive first: running water and a clean cover before any medication or cream.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Ensure safety, stop the burning.\n2. Airway, breathing, circulation.\n3. Cool and cover.\n4. Get emergency help; do not leave the person alone.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWrong first-aid choices (ice, ointments, breaking blisters, pulling off stuck clothing). In hospital fire scenarios, remove people in immediate danger first, then raise the alarm. Which first for an electrical burn (turn off the source). Trap: choosing to extinguish the fire before rescuing the client.\n\n## Memory aid\nRACE and PASS are standard acronyms.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Burns: Causes and Overview]], [[Airway Management in the Primary Survey]], [[Burn Care Phases and Emergency Priorities]], [[Oxygen Therapy: Safety and Delivery Devices]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- How long to cool, local fire-safety protocol and which E your reviewer uses in RACE (Extinguish in the handout, Evacuate in some books).",

"id": "np5-burns-rule-of-nines",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Rule of Nines and Burn Size Estimation",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Rule of Nines is a quick way to estimate how much of an adult's body surface is burned, using areas that are roughly 9 percent or multiples of 9. Burn size guides fluid needs and transfer decisions.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Adult: head and neck about 9; each arm about 9; front of trunk (chest and abdomen) about 18; back of trunk about 18; each leg about 18; genitals (groin) about 1. These add to about 100.\n- Children: the handout gives head 18, each arm 9, each leg 14, chest 18 and abdomen 18 as a child pattern (written loosely; a child has a larger head and smaller legs). Paediatric charts or the Lund and Browder chart, which changes percentages by age, are more accurate and should be re-checked after the first wound cleaning.\n- A small or scattered patch can be estimated by the palm method: the client's palm is about 1 percent of body surface.\n- Only second and third degree burns are usually counted; do not count simple redness.\n- Size is only part of severity; location and depth also matter ([[Degrees of Burns]]). Extensive burns are 25 percent TBSA or more, with a whole-body response ([[Burn Size, Depth and Severity Categories]]).\n- Minor burns in adults: second degree under 15 percent (under 10 percent in children) or third degree under 2 percent; major: second degree over 25 percent in adults (over 20 percent in children), any burn over 10 percent third degree, or special areas (eyes, ears, face, hands, feet, perineum, joints), inhalation, electrical or concurrent trauma.\n- Fluid resuscitation is typically indicated in adults with burns over about 15 to 20 percent and in children over about 10 to 15 percent ([[Burn Fluid Resuscitation: Parkland and Brooke Formulas]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Which regions are burned, depth, and the percentage; weight for fluid calculations.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: a larger area of leaking skin means more fluid lost, so size decides the amount of IV fluid and the level of care.\n- ABCs come before measuring: a small burn with an airway burn is more urgent than a large burn on the back with a clear airway.\n- Estimates start rough and are refined; it is better to estimate quickly than to delay fluids.\n- Example: front of trunk (18) plus one whole arm (9) = 27 percent, which is extensive.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Do ABCs before measuring.\n2. Estimate size quickly, record the time of burn.\n3. Report the estimate so the provider can order fluids ([[Other Burn Management: Fluids, Airway and Complications]]).\n4. Reassess because first estimates are often wrong.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nCalculate the percent for a client with burns on the front trunk and one arm. Trap: using the adult rule for a small child; counting a first degree area; counting a leg as 9.\n\n## Memory aid\n9, 9, 18, 18, 18 each pair; 1 for genitals. This is a standard pattern.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Burns: Causes and Overview]], [[Phases of Burn Care]], [[The Burned Child and the Seven Phases of Burn Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Paediatric percentages (the handout's child figures look garbled: child percentages should add to about 100 and the abdomen is usually part of an 18 trunk); confirm.\n- The fluid formula your exam source teaches.",

"id": "np5-burns-other-management",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Other Burn Management: Fluids, Airway and Complications",
"body": "## What it is\nBeyond first aid and wound care, burn management covers fluid resuscitation, breathing support, monitoring and prevention of complications. The first day is mostly about keeping the circulation and airway working.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Major burns cause large fluid shifts. The client needs IV fluids, usually lactated Ringer's (an isotonic crystalloid) initially, with colloid added on the second day, calculated from weight and burn size ([[Burn Fluid Resuscitation: Parkland and Brooke Formulas]]).\n- Parkland formula (handout): 4 mL x body weight in kg x percent TBSA burned in 24 hours, half in the first 8 hours (counted from the time of injury) and the rest over the next 16 hours. Example: 70 kg and 80 percent gives 22,400 mL on day 1. Second day: colloid plus dextrose in water, as ordered. The Brooke formula uses less crystalloid (1.5 mL per kg per percent) plus colloid and dextrose.\n- Urine output is the most sensitive guide: the target in the handout is about 30 to 50 mL an hour. Too little means more fluid may be needed; above about 50 mL an hour the rate may be reduced; too much may mean overload.\n- Successful resuscitation: stable vital signs, palpable peripheral pulses, adequate urine output, clear thinking.\n- Suspect inhalation injury: face burns, singed hair, soot, hoarseness, stridor. Oxygen is given; early intubation if swelling is progressing (swelling peaks in the first 24 to 48 hours). Carbon monoxide poisoning is treated with high-concentration oxygen until the carboxyhaemoglobin level is below 5 percent ([[Smoke Inhalation and Carbon Monoxide Poisoning]]).\n- Circumferential burns of a limb or chest may need escharotomy (a lengthwise cut through the eschar); fasciotomy follows if perfusion does not return ([[Burn Wound Dressing, Escharotomy and Fasciotomy]]).\n- Complications to watch: compartment syndrome, hypothermia, stress (Curling's) ulcers, paralytic ileus, renal failure, infection and sepsis, ARDS, heart rhythm problems after electrical burns ([[Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)]]).\n- GI protection: keep nothing by mouth until bowel sounds return, insert a gastric tube as ordered, give antacids, proton pump inhibitors or H2 blockers, and test stool for blood.\n- Tetanus prophylaxis is checked and given if needed ([[Tetanus Toxoid Immunization]]).\n- Keep a burned client warm; heat is lost through damaged skin.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Airway sounds, urine output, vital signs, distal pulses and capillary refill, neurovascular status, bowel sounds, temperature, haematocrit and electrolytes.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: leaking vessels and evaporation -> falling volume -> shock; fluids replace it. Because the leak is greatest in the first 8 hours, the first half of the fluid goes in during that time.\n- Order: airway and breathing, then large-bore IV and fluids, then catheter and hourly urine, then limb circulation, then wound and infection.\n- Safety: an unrecognised tight eschar can strangle a limb or the chest; report loss of pulses, pain or a drop in ventilation.\n- Never use pulse or blood pressure alone to judge fluid; urine output shows kidney perfusion.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway and breathing.\n2. Large-bore IV access and fluid replacement.\n3. Insert catheter, monitor hourly urine output.\n4. Elevate burned limbs, check circulation hourly, report changes.\n5. Prevent infection and heat loss.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich assessment finding means the fluid is adequate (steady urine output); which burn needs airway watching first; which sign suggests compartment syndrome; what a urine output of 20 mL an hour means in the first day (increase fluids, report). Trap: oral fluids or oral drugs in a client with an ileus.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Phases of Burn Care]], [[Rule of Nines and Burn Size Estimation]], [[Airway Management in the Primary Survey]], [[Fluid Volume Deficit (Hypovolemia)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Fluid formula, target urine output, and escharotomy indications; verify against current guidelines.\n- Carboxyhaemoglobin target and timing of colloid on day 2.",

"id": "np5-burns-pain-infection-nutrition",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Pain, Infection and Nutrition in Burns",
"body": "## What it is\nThree connected problems drive recovery after the first days: severe pain, high risk of infection, and a very high need for calories and protein.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Pain: major burns are very painful, and dressing changes cause sharp procedural pain. Morphine is the usual drug for acute burn pain; meperidine is also named in the handout. In the early phase opioids are given by the IV route because absorption from muscle or under the skin is unreliable when circulation is poor and fluid is shifting; oral drugs are avoided early because the gut works poorly. Oral route returns near discharge. Give pain medicine before dressing changes (the handout says about 30 minutes before painful care; other lines say 20), plan the timing, position burned areas in alignment, and use non-drug supports (distraction, relaxation).\n- Infection: burned skin no longer protects, and the immune system is depressed, so infection and sepsis are leading causes of illness. Signs: change in wound colour, odour, pus, increased drainage, fever or low temperature, rising heart rate, worsening wound. Prevent with strict hand hygiene, aseptic dressing, clean environment, protective isolation as policy dictates ([[Burn Wound Care Methods, Hydrotherapy and Debridement]], [[Antimicrobials for Burns]]).\n- Nutrition: burns cause a hypermetabolic state (metabolic rate up to about three times normal) with tissue breakdown. Plan a diet high in protein, carbohydrate, calories, vitamins and minerals; protein needs are high (the handout gives roughly 1.5 to 4 g per kg a day). Feeding starts early, often through a tube, once bowel sounds return; before that the client is kept nothing by mouth, then advanced from clear liquids. Parenteral nutrition is considered for weight loss over 10 percent, prolonged wound exposure or poor pre-burn nutrition. Schedule wound care at least an hour before meals. Weigh regularly and monitor intake ([[Burn Pain, Nutrition and GI Protection]]).\n- GI protection: stress ulcers (Curling's ulcer) and paralytic ileus are risks; antacids, PPIs or H2 blockers are given.\n- Emotional support: fear, body image changes, sleep loss and post-traumatic stress are common.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Pain score, sedation level, wound signs, temperature, weight, albumin and other labs as ordered, intake, bowel sounds, mood.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: damaged skin plus weak immunity -> infection risk; high metabolic demand -> weight and protein loss, poor healing. So infection prevention and nutrition are as important as dressings.\n- Pain before procedures: untreated pain raises metabolic demand and breathing effort. But airway and breathing come before pain relief.\n- IV route first because other routes are unreliable; safe dosing needs sedation and respiratory checks.\n- Least invasive nutrition first: oral or tube feeding before parenteral, because feeding the gut protects it.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat pain first when it prevents breathing or movement, and premedicate for procedures.\n2. Use strict aseptic technique.\n3. Get dietician input; give high-protein high-calorie nutrition.\n4. Offer emotional support and involve family.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhy IV route for early opioids; which diet is correct; which sign points to infection early (change in wound or temperature). Trap: restricting calories in a burn client, or giving IM injections in the first day.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Phases of Burn Care]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention|Crisis Intervention]], [[Antimicrobials for Burns]], [[Burn Pain, Nutrition and GI Protection]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Calorie and protein targets (the handout's very high calorie figure should be confirmed); local pain protocols; timing of premedication.",

"id": "np5-burns-wound-care",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Burn Wound Care Methods, Hydrotherapy and Debridement",
"body": "## What it is\nBurn wound care aims to clean the wound, remove dead tissue, prevent infection, and prepare for healing or grafting.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Open (exposure) method: wound left uncovered, usually with topical antimicrobial and a clean warm room. Easier observation, but heat and fluid loss and infection risk need control.\n- Closed (occlusive) method: topical agent plus dressings that are changed regularly; common for many wounds. Light dressings over joints allow motion; dress circumferential wounds from distal to proximal and wrap fingers and toes separately. Occlusive gauze with an antimicrobial over new grafts stays 3 to 5 days.\n- Biological and synthetic dressings (animal skin or manufactured materials) cover clean wounds, reduce pain and fluid loss, and help prepare for grafting.\n- Cleansing: pat dry, gentle cleaning at intervals; blister policy varies (large blisters are often removed or left depending on protocol; verify).\n- Hydrotherapy: cleansing by immersion, shower or spray for 30 minutes or less, to prevent sodium loss through the wound; water about 37.8 C. It loosens dressings and dead tissue and allows exercise. Risks: pain, chilling, infection spread, fluid and electrolyte loss. Not used for haemodynamically unstable clients or new skin grafts; premedicate.\n- Debridement removes dead tissue (eschar) to prevent bacteria growing under it and to promote healing:\n  - Natural: dead tissue separates by itself over the first week or two.\n  - Mechanical: scissors, scalpel, forceps, and wet-to-dry coarse-mesh dressings.\n  - Enzymatic: topical enzyme preparations.\n  - Surgical: excision to bleeding viable tissue, done early; blood loss can be large (the handout gives about 100 to 125 mL per percent excised).\n- Escharotomy relieves pressure from a tight eschar; fasciotomy cuts through fascia if perfusion does not return, done in the operating room ([[Burn Wound Dressing, Escharotomy and Fasciotomy]]).\n- Aseptic technique and personal protective equipment for every change.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Wound base colour, edges, drainage, odour, signs of healing, pain, temperature, circulation.\n- After escharotomy: pulses, colour, movement, sensation; bleeding controlled with pressure.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: dead tissue feeds bacteria -> infection -> sepsis; so removing eschar and keeping the wound clean prevents the leading cause of late death.\n- Safety: premedicate and keep the client warm, since chilling and pain increase the metabolic load.\n- Least invasive first: natural and dressing-based debridement before surgical; open method only where the environment is controlled.\n- Assess before acting: look at the wound and the circulation each time before applying agents.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Premedicate for pain and warm the room.\n2. Use aseptic technique and remove old dressings gently.\n3. Clean, debride as ordered, apply the topical agent and dressing.\n4. Assess and document each time.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich action prevents hypothermia during hydrotherapy; which method debrides surgically; what to do when dressing sticks (soak to loosen); why hydrotherapy is limited to about 30 minutes (sodium loss); which clients should not have it (unstable, new grafts).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Antimicrobials for Burns]], [[Skin Grafting]], [[Pain, Infection and Nutrition in Burns]], [[Burn Wound Dressing, Escharotomy and Fasciotomy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Blister management and local protocols for dressing type.\n- Hydrotherapy temperature and duration; amount of blood lost in excision.",

"id": "np5-burns-skin-grafting",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Skin Grafting",
"body": "## What it is\nA skin graft is healthy skin placed over a burn or wound that cannot heal on its own. It closes the wound, reduces infection, fluid loss, heat loss and scarring. Closure is usually done between about the 5th and 21st day depending on the burn.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Autograft: the client's own skin; permanent. Taken from a donor site; the graft is immobilised for about 3 to 7 days so it can attach.\n- Allograft (homograft): donor skin from another human, often cadaver skin taken within 24 hours of death; temporary cover; rejection can occur within about 24 hours.\n- Xenograft (heterograft): animal skin, often pig; temporary; replaced every 2 to 5 days until the wound heals or an autograft is done; rejection within about 24 to 72 hours.\n- Synthetic or biosynthetic skin substitutes and cultured skin exist; some are transparent so the wound can be seen.\n- Split-thickness graft: top layers; donor site heals itself in about 7 to 14 days; may be meshed to cover a larger area. Full-thickness graft: all layers; better look for face and hands, but the donor site must be closed.\n- Graft needs a clean, well-supplied bed to take; fluid, blood or infection under it can lift it off. Bleeding beneath an autograft stops it from sticking.\n- First dressing change usually 2 to 5 days after surgery, earlier if there is pus or a foul odour ([[Burn Wound Closure: Autograft, Allograft and Xenograft]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Graft colour, adherence, drainage, odour, signs of infection or bleeding, donor site pain and bleeding, circulation, nutrition.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: a graft lives on blood supply from the bed, so anything that lifts or squeezes it (fluid, blood, movement, pressure, infection) kills it. So protect, immobilise and watch the bed.\n- Safety: avoid shearing and pressure; keep the area elevated and still.\n- Temporary covers buy time until an autograft can be done; only an autograft is permanent.\n- Donor site: a partial-thickness wound that is painful; treat pain and keep dressing as ordered.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Immobilise and protect the graft site as ordered, avoiding pressure, shearing and moisture.\n2. Elevate the area and avoid movement until the surgeon allows.\n3. Keep dressing intact; observe for fluid collection; follow the order for first dressing change.\n4. Care for the donor site (keep dry or covered as ordered); it is often more painful than the graft.\n5. Prevent infection; encourage protein-rich nutrition.\n6. Teach about sun protection, moisturising, scar care.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich graft is temporary (allograft, xenograft, synthetic); which is permanent (autograft); what to do first when a graft looks blue or has blood collecting under it (report promptly); why a client with a new graft cannot have hydrotherapy. Trap: calling a xenograft permanent because it is a \"graft\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Burn Wound Care Methods, Hydrotherapy and Debridement]], [[Phases of Burn Care]], [[Degrees of Burns]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact timing of first dressing change, donor site dressing practice, and positioning rules.\n- Handout times for rejection and immobilisation vary by source.",

"id": "np5-burns-antimicrobials",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Antimicrobials for Burns",
"body": "## What it is\nTopical antimicrobials are applied directly to burn wounds to prevent or treat infection. Systemic antibiotics are used for confirmed infection and not routinely as prevention.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Silver sulfadiazine (Silvadene) cream: widely used, soothing, minimal penetration of eschar; used with open, light or occlusive dressings; applied one to three times daily after cleansing. Cautions: sulfa allergy (rash, itching, burning), temporary drop in white cells that clears in a few days, and a pseudo-eschar that is removed easily after about 72 hours and makes inspection harder. Store away from heat.\n- Mafenide acetate (Sulfamylon) 5 to 10 percent cream: penetrates eschar, so used on deep burns, ears and cartilage and is the agent of choice for electrical burns in the handout. Cautions: painful during and after application (give analgesic about 30 minutes before) and metabolic acidosis; not used over large areas (the handout says not over 20 percent TBSA). Daily baths remove old cream.\n- Silver nitrate 0.5 percent solution: bacteriostatic and fungicidal; does not penetrate eschar; dressings are kept wet, remoistened about every 2 hours; stains skin, linen and utensils grey or black; monitor sodium and potassium (can lower them).\n- Others: cerium nitrate, povidone iodine, gentamicin (watch hearing, balance and kidneys), polymyxin B, bacitracin ointment (face and superficial wounds), silver-impregnated dressings.\n- Nurses observe application technique: thin layer with sterile gloves, remove old product at each change, check sensitivities and report allergy.\n- Tetanus prophylaxis should be checked.\n- Culture the wound when infection is suspected before changing therapy.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Allergy history, wound signs, temperature, white cell and acid-base results as ordered, pain on application, sodium and potassium with silver nitrate.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: each agent has a signature adverse effect, and the exam picks the agent from it: sulfa allergy and low white cells (silver sulfadiazine); pain and acidosis (mafenide); stains and electrolyte loss (silver nitrate).\n- Safety: check allergy before the first application; sulfa allergy rules out both sulfa-based agents.\n- Assess before acting: look at the wound and labs; treat pain first for painful agents.\n- A dry crust-like layer after silver sulfadiazine is not always eschar; do not confuse it with wound worsening.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Check allergy and order before applying.\n2. Use sterile technique and appropriate coverage.\n3. Premedicate for painful agents.\n4. Monitor laboratory findings and report abnormal ones.\n5. Document and report infection signs.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich agent is contraindicated in sulfa allergy; which causes acidosis; which stains; which penetrates eschar (mafenide); which does not (silver nitrate, silver sulfadiazine minimal). Pick the agent by its signature adverse effect.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Burn Wound Care Methods, Hydrotherapy and Debridement]], [[Pain, Infection and Nutrition in Burns]], [[Topical Antimicrobial Agents for Burns]], [[Sulfonamides and Urinary Anti-Infectives]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Drug lists, concentrations, frequency and which agent your exam source prefers for which area; verify against pharmacology references.\n- The handout calls silver sulfadiazine \"most bactericidal\"; confirm. The 20 percent TBSA limit for mafenide is doubtful.",

"id": "np5-disaster-basic-concepts",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts",
"body": "## What it is\nA disaster is a sudden event that overwhelms the ability of a community to cope using its own resources and needs outside help. Disaster nursing applies nursing knowledge to protect health, give care and support recovery before, during and after such events. A common definition: a serious disruption of how a society functions, with widespread human, material or environmental losses beyond its own coping ability.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Types: natural (typhoon, flood, earthquake, volcanic eruption, landslide), human-made (fire, transport accident, conflict, industrial or chemical accident, terrorism), and mixed. Mass casualty incident means many casualties at once; it may or may not be a disaster depending on local capacity.\n- Hazard is the source of danger; vulnerability is how exposed and unprepared people are; capacity is what helps them cope; risk comes from hazard combined with vulnerability and low capacity. Disasters hurt most where vulnerability is high.\n- Major risk groups: natural, technological (chemical, radiological), epidemics, and societal risks (poverty, exclusion, violence).\n- Most vulnerable people: children, older adults, pregnant women, people with disability, chronic illness, poverty.\n- Emergency means something needing immediate response; the main objective of emergency management is to decrease death, illness and disability.\n- General principles (handout): first priority is protecting people at risk; second is protecting critical resources and systems; capabilities must be built before impact; management relies on understanding hazards and on teamwork across disciplines ([[Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Program]]).\n- Disaster care differs from daily care: goal shifts to the greatest good for the greatest number, with limited resources, which is why disaster triage is used ([[NATO Disaster Triage System]]).\n- Nurses' roles: preparedness teaching, triage, first aid, shelter and evacuation-centre care, infection and sanitation control, vaccination and surveillance, psychosocial support, record keeping, advocacy.\n- Common health issues afterwards: injuries, infection outbreaks from poor water and sanitation, malnutrition, mental stress, interrupted care for chronic disease.\n- Philippine legal roots: Presidential Decree 1566 (1978) on disaster control and community preparedness, the Local Government Code (RA 7160, which moved responsibilities to local units), and RA 10121 (2010) ([[NDRRMC and the Philippine Disaster Framework]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Scene safety, number and condition of casualties, available resources, water and sanitation, vulnerable people, psychological state.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: overwhelmed local resources -> many casualties of mixed severity -> sort first, treat the savable first.\n- Maslow and safety: your own safety, then life-saving care, then shelter, food, water and sanitation, then comfort and psychosocial care.\n- Assess before acting: a rapid scene and casualty assessment decides what help to call.\n- Prevent the second wave: outbreaks from contaminated water kill after the event, so sanitation and surveillance are priorities.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Personal and team safety first.\n2. Rapid assessment and triage.\n3. Life-saving care before comfort care.\n4. Preventing secondary problems such as outbreaks.\n5. Psychosocial support ([[Crisis and Crisis Intervention|Crisis Intervention]]).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich role fits which phase, who is seen first in a mass casualty event, which action first at arrival to the scene (safety). Trap: confusing hazard with vulnerability, or choosing comfort care before life-saving care.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Disaster Management Continuum]], [[NATO Disaster Triage System]], [[NDRRMC and the Philippine Disaster Framework]], [[Crisis Types, Phases and Intervention Styles]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Definitions differ between sources; use your reviewer's wording for exam definitions.\n- Decree and law numbers come from the handout and from memory; confirm.",

"id": "np5-disaster-continuum",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Disaster Management Continuum",
"body": "## What it is\nThe disaster management continuum (cycle) shows that disaster work is continuous, not just the response. Phases flow into one another: mitigation and prevention, preparedness, response, and recovery with rehabilitation. Many books group them as before, during and after.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Prevention and mitigation: reduce the damage before disaster by stopping hazards or lowering risk. Examples: land-use planning, building codes, flood control, hazard mapping, community education.\n- Preparedness: be ready to act. Examples: emergency plans, drills, early warning, stockpiling, trained volunteers, evacuation routes, family kits, community health assessment.\n- Response: actions during and immediately after impact. Examples: search and rescue, triage, emergency treatment, evacuation, shelter, emergency food and water, disease control.\n- Recovery and rehabilitation: return to normal or better. Examples: rebuilding, restoring services, counselling, follow-up of injured, lessons learned, updating plans.\n- Nursing roles per phase: educate and train (before), triage and treat (during), long-term care and mental health follow-up (after).\n- Philippine law frames the same ideas as four thematic areas ([[NDRRMC and the Philippine Disaster Framework]]).\n- Capabilities must be developed before a hazard strikes; this is why preparedness is called the nurse's most useful phase.\n- Health emergency programs follow the same cycle at the community level ([[Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Program]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Before: community risk and readiness. During: casualties, needs. After: unmet health needs, outbreaks, stress.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: ask \"has the hazard struck yet?\" If no, the activity is mitigation or preparedness; if it is happening or just happened, response; if the immediate danger is over, recovery.\n- Mitigation changes the hazard or the risk (a dike, a building code); preparedness prepares people to react (a drill, a kit). If the activity makes the event smaller, it is mitigation; if it makes people ready, it is preparedness.\n- Priority within response follows ABC and safety first; Maslow places food, water and shelter before education.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Match the action to the phase: teaching and drills before, triage and care during, follow-up after.\n2. Keep records and report.\n3. Evaluate and improve plans.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nGive an activity and ask which phase it belongs to (for example a drill is preparedness; setting up a first aid station is response; building a seawall is mitigation; counselling survivors weeks later is recovery). Trap: confusing mitigation with preparedness.\n\n## Memory aid\n\"Reduce, ready, respond, recover\" (my own wording).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts]], [[NDRRMC and the Philippine Disaster Framework]], [[NATO Disaster Triage System]], [[Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Program]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Number and names of phases differ between texts (three, four or five). No handout in your set covers this cycle in detail, so this note rests on standard textbook knowledge.",
"tags": [
"disaster",
"phases"
],
"priority": "high",
"outlineRefs": [
"Disaster Management Continuum"
],

"nurseReviewed": false

"id": "np5-nato-triage",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "NATO Disaster Triage System",
"body": "## What it is\nNATO triage is a disaster and military-style system that sorts many casualties into groups by urgency and survivability, so limited help goes where it saves the most lives. It uses priority categories, often shown by colour tags.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Immediate (T1, usually red): life-threatening but treatable and likely to survive with quick care, such as airway obstruction, severe bleeding, shock, tension pneumothorax.\n- Delayed (T2, usually yellow): serious injuries that can wait a short time without immediate death risk, such as stable fractures or burns not threatening airway.\n- Minimal (T3, usually green): minor injuries, walking wounded; they can wait or help themselves.\n- Expectant (T4, often blue or grey; some systems use black): injuries so severe that survival is unlikely given available resources, for example massive head injury or very large burns; they receive comfort care and are rechecked when resources allow.\n- Dead (often black) in many systems.\n- Colour and label details vary by country and by textbook. State what your reviewer teaches; I am confident of the immediate, delayed, minimal, expectant structure.\n- Triage in disaster is repeated and done where the casualty is found and again at the treatment area.\n- START (simple triage and rapid treatment), a widely taught method, goes in this order: can the person walk (green)? If not, is breathing present (open airway; none = black/expectant)? Breathing rate above 30 a minute = immediate; then perfusion (no radial pulse or capillary refill over 2 seconds = immediate); then mental status (cannot follow simple commands = immediate); otherwise delayed. Verify steps.\n- Do not stop to treat in the field during first-pass sorting, except to open an airway or control bleeding quickly.\n- Large burns: very extensive burns in a disaster may be placed expectant if resources are scarce, while smaller burns are delayed or immediate by airway involvement.\n- Children and older adults may need a different yardstick; use pediatric tools where available.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Ability to walk, breathing, circulation (pulse, capillary refill), ability to follow commands.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: resources are scarce, so the aim shifts from \"sickest first\" to \"greatest number saved\". The immediate group (savable with quick care) goes first.\n- ABC still decides within the group: airway and bleeding problems are fixed fast because they are quick to fix and deadly.\n- Walking wounded are green because walking proves airway, breathing and enough circulation.\n- Reassess: a delayed casualty who worsens is retagged immediate; an expectant casualty with new resources may be reconsidered.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Sort quickly; identify the immediate group first.\n2. Tag, move to the right zone, treat in category order.\n3. Reassess regularly and retag.\n4. Document.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich category for a client with a given description; which casualty to treat first (immediate); what to do for the expectant group (comfort, reassess). Trap: assuming the most seriously injured always goes first; in disaster those unlikely to survive wait. Another trap: stopping to treat one casualty during the first sweep.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage]], [[Disaster Management Continuum]], [[Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts]], [[PENA Triage]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Category numbers, colours (especially for expectant), and whether dead are a separate category.\n- START cut-offs (breathing rate 30, capillary refill 2 seconds); no handout in your set covers triage, so this rests on standard textbook knowledge.",

"title": "NDRRMC and the Philippine Disaster Framework",
"body": "## What it is\nNDRRMC is the National Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Council, the Philippine body that leads national disaster risk reduction and response coordination. It operates under the law on Philippine disaster risk reduction and management passed in 2010 (Republic Act 10121), which replaced the older disaster coordinating council system.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The law shifted the national approach from mainly responding after disasters to reducing risk beforehand, with community participation.\n- The framework is organised around four thematic areas: disaster prevention and mitigation, disaster preparedness, disaster response, and disaster rehabilitation and recovery.\n- The system works through councils at national, regional, provincial, city or municipal, and barangay levels. Local governments have a first-responder role (consistent with the Local Government Code, RA 7160, which gave local units more responsibility). The Office of Civil Defense supports the national council as its secretariat. Different government agencies lead the different areas (for example health, social welfare, public works, defence).\n- Earlier law: Presidential Decree 1566 (1978) strengthened disaster control capability and set up a national program on community disaster preparedness ([[Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Program]]).\n- Local disaster funds exist; details are for your textbook.\n- The Department of Health runs health emergency management and coordinates health responders; nurses may be part of rapid response teams and evacuation centre care.\n- All details here are general and may have been amended; verify local policy.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Community hazards and readiness, evacuation centre conditions, health needs of evacuees, reporting channels.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: disaster hits a barangay first, so the barangay level is the first line; higher levels step in when local capacity is exceeded.\n- Match the activity to the thematic area, using the same logic as the continuum: reduce hazard = prevention and mitigation; readiness = preparedness; act now = response; rebuild = rehabilitation and recovery ([[Disaster Management Continuum]]).\n- Safety and Maslow: evacuation centres must provide safe water, sanitation, food, shelter and protection for vulnerable people before programs.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Know the local plan and who to report to.\n2. Join drills and teach families about preparedness.\n3. Respond within your role and report health data to authorities.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nName the council, the law, the four thematic areas, or which phase an activity belongs to. Trap: mixing the levels (barangay is the lowest and first line), or mixing the 2010 law with the older decree.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Disaster Management Continuum]], [[Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts]], [[NATO Disaster Triage System]], [[Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Program]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Verify local policy: council membership and chair, agency roles, law amendments, exact wording of the four areas and DOH health emergency arrangements.\n- Law numbers are from memory and the community health handout; confirm.",

"id": "np5-cardiac-arrest-bls",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Cardiac Arrest and Basic Life Support Principles",
"body": "## What it is\nCardiac arrest is sudden loss of effective heart pumping, so the client is unresponsive, not breathing normally and has no pulse. Basic life support (BLS) keeps blood and oxygen flowing until the heart can be restarted.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Rhythms: shockable (ventricular fibrillation, pulseless ventricular tachycardia) need defibrillation. Non-shockable (asystole, pulseless electrical activity) need high-quality compressions, airway support, medicines and treatment of the cause ([[ECG Waves and Intervals]]).\n- Chain of survival: early recognition and call for help, early CPR, early defibrillation, advanced care, post-arrest care.\n- Principles of good CPR: start promptly, push hard and fast on the centre of the chest (lower half of the breastbone), allow full recoil, keep interruptions very short, avoid over-ventilation, rotate compressors when tired. Commonly taught adult figures: a rate of 100 to 120 a minute, depth about 5 to 6 cm, and 30 compressions to 2 breaths for one rescuer. Exact numbers change between guideline updates and age groups: verify against current guidelines.\n- Use an automated external defibrillator as soon as available; follow its prompts; stand clear before a shock; resume compressions right after the shock.\n- Reversible causes are searched for (often grouped as the Hs and Ts such as low oxygen, low volume, tension pneumothorax, tamponade, clots).\n- Check for a pulse for no longer than the guideline time (about 10 seconds); if unsure, begin compressions. Carotid pulse in adults, brachial in infants, femoral during shock or arrest.\n- Epinephrine is the emergency drug used in arrest and anaphylaxis ([[Adrenergic Agonists: Epinephrine, Dopamine and Related Drugs]]).\n- Do-not-resuscitate orders and living wills are honoured when valid ([[Advance Directives]]); without a valid order, resuscitate.\n- After return of circulation: airway, oxygen, monitoring, 12-lead ECG, temperature care and transfer to intensive care.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Responsiveness, breathing, pulse, rhythm on monitor, scene safety.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: no pulse means no oxygen reaching the brain, so brain injury starts within minutes; compressions restore some flow, defibrillation restores a rhythm. Early compressions and early shock are the two things that change survival.\n- Why compressions first in an adult: the blood still holds oxygen for the first minutes, and what is missing is the pump (ABC becomes CAB in cardiac arrest). For drowning, children and obvious breathing causes, ventilation matters more.\n- Safety: scene safe, standard precautions, dry the chest and remove oxygen sources before shocking.\n- Assess before intervening, but never delay compressions for tests or equipment.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Check safety and responsiveness, call for help and the defibrillator.\n2. Start compressions (circulation first in adults).\n3. Open the airway and ventilate as trained.\n4. Defibrillate when a shockable rhythm is present.\n5. Continue until relieved, the client recovers, or a valid order to stop; document the event.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich action first (check response and call for help, then compressions). Which rhythm gets a shock. Trap: delaying compressions to find a pulse for too long or to intubate. Another: giving a shock for asystole.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Airway Obstruction and Choking]], [[Respiratory Failure Recognition]], [[ICU Monitoring Concepts]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- All numbers (rate, depth, ratio, shock energy, drug doses) and the sequence for children and infants. No handout in your set covers resuscitation, so this rests on standard textbook knowledge.",
