"id": "np5-airway-obstruction-choking",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Airway Obstruction and Choking",
"body": "## What it is\nChoking happens when a foreign body blocks the airway, either partly or fully. A full blockage is a life-threatening emergency within minutes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Mild (partial) obstruction: the person can cough, speak or breathe with noise. Encourage coughing; do not slap the back or perform thrusts while air still moves.\n- Severe obstruction: cannot speak, cough or breathe; may clutch the throat (universal choking sign), have silent struggle, turn blue.\n- Conscious adult or child: abdominal thrusts (Heimlich manoeuvre) repeatedly until the object comes out or the person becomes unresponsive. Pregnant or very obese clients: chest thrusts instead.\n- Infant: alternating back blows and chest thrusts, not abdominal thrusts; never blind finger sweeps. Exact numbers per cycle (commonly five of each): verify against current guidelines.\n- If the person becomes unresponsive: lower to the ground, call for help, and begin CPR; look in the mouth before ventilating and remove an object only if you can see it ([[Cardiac Arrest and Basic Life Support Principles]]).\n- Hospital: trained providers may use suction, forceps or advanced airway or surgical airway.\n- Choking in an unconscious client who is breathing noisily with snoring is usually the tongue, not a foreign body: open the airway first ([[Airway Management in the Primary Survey]]).\n- After thrusts, the person should be checked by a provider because abdominal thrusts can injure organs.\n- Prevention: cut food small, supervise children, avoid small objects and some foods for young children (nuts, hard sweets, whole grapes), careful eating for people with swallowing difficulty; the chewing and swallowing risk rises with dementia, stroke and sedation.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Ability to cough and speak, colour, breathing effort, level of consciousness.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: a solid object blocks air -> cannot speak or cough -> if air still moves, let the body clear it (least invasive first); if no air moves, force pressure to expel it (thrusts) at once.\n- Why not thrust a coughing client: a thrust may move a partial blockage into a full one.\n- Why back blows plus chest thrusts in infants: abdominal thrusts can damage a baby's liver; their small size lets back blows work.\n- Maslow and ABC: this is an airway emergency; nothing outranks it.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Ask \"Are you choking?\" and check if the person can speak or cough.\n2. If severe, call for help and begin thrusts right away.\n3. If the person collapses, start CPR.\n4. After recovery, assess and have the person medically checked.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich action first when a client can cough (encourage coughing, observe). Which technique for infants (back blows plus chest thrusts). Trap: doing abdominal thrusts on an infant, or sweeping the mouth blindly.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Airway Management in the Primary Survey]], [[Cardiac Arrest and Basic Life Support Principles]], [[Respiratory Failure Recognition]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Number of thrusts and blows and the sequence by age; verify against current guidelines. No handout in your set covers choking, so this rests on standard textbook knowledge.",

"id": "np5-respiratory-failure",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Respiratory Failure Recognition",
"body": "## What it is\nRespiratory failure means the lungs cannot keep blood oxygen or carbon dioxide at safe levels. It may develop fast (acute) or on top of chronic lung disease.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Hypoxaemic (type 1) failure: low oxygen with normal or low carbon dioxide, for example pneumonia, pulmonary oedema, acute respiratory distress syndrome, pulmonary embolism ([[Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)]], [[Pulmonary Embolism]]). In ARDS the low oxygen does not improve with extra oxygen; it develops over about 4 to 48 hours with severe breathlessness and bilateral lung shadows on X-ray, and is treated with ventilation and positive end-expiratory pressure.\n- Hypercapnic (type 2) failure: high carbon dioxide from poor ventilation, for example advanced COPD, drug overdose, neuromuscular weakness, chest wall injury, exhaustion.\n- Early signs: restlessness, anxiety, fast breathing, fast pulse, use of accessory muscles. Later signs: confusion, drowsiness, blue colour, slow tiring breathing, falling saturation. Drowsiness with high carbon dioxide is dangerous.\n- Arterial blood gases confirm the picture. Normal: pH 7.35 to 7.45, PaO2 80 to 100 mm Hg, PaCO2 35 to 45 mm Hg, HCO3 22 to 26. Failure shows low PaO2, high PaCO2 and a falling pH (respiratory acidosis) in the late stage ([[Arterial Blood Gas Basics]], [[Respiratory Acidosis]]). Oxygen saturation normally 95 to 100 percent.\n- Clients with long-term CO2 retention need cautious controlled oxygen, but oxygen must never be withheld from someone who is hypoxic.\n- Treatment: treat the cause, oxygen (device chosen by need: cannula for low flow, non-rebreather for the highest concentration), positioning (upright), airway clearance, bronchodilators or other drugs as ordered, non-invasive ventilation or mechanical ventilation when needed ([[Oxygen Therapy: Safety and Delivery Devices]]).\n- Common precipitants: pneumothorax, pulmonary embolism, heart failure, sedatives, opioids, spinal cord injury or neuromuscular disease (the diaphragm weakens), and severe asthma.\n- Shock and respiratory failure often travel together; sepsis and burns can start both.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Respiratory rate and pattern, effort, saturation, colour, mental status, breath sounds, blood gases, fatigue.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: poor gas exchange or poor ventilation -> low oxygen or high CO2 -> restlessness first, then drowsiness. The restless client is still compensating; the drowsy client with slow breathing is tiring.\n- Maslow and ABC: oxygenation is the most urgent need; sitting upright and oxygen are the least invasive first steps; escalate to ventilation when tiring.\n- Why not sedate an anxious breathless client: sedatives suppress breathing and mask the sign (restlessness) that tells you oxygen is falling.\n- Unstable before stable: a slow, drowsy breather outranks a fast, anxious one who is still talking.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway and breathing; call for help early.\n2. Sit the client upright, give oxygen as ordered.\n3. Monitor continuously and report deterioration quickly.\n4. Prepare for ventilation support and transfer to a higher level of care.\n5. Reassure the client and reduce oxygen demand.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSpot the early sign (restlessness), not only the late ones (cyanosis). Which client first when comparing a calm one with a slow, drowsy breathing one. Which gas result shows ventilatory failure (high CO2, low pH). Trap: giving a sedative for anxiety.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Breathing Assessment in the Primary Survey]], [[ICU Monitoring Concepts]], [[Cardiac Arrest and Basic Life Support Principles]], [[Chest X-ray and Pulse Oximetry]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Gas values, oxygen delivery targets for chronic lung disease, and ventilation settings.\n- Normal values are from the fundamentals handout; type 1 and 2 labels are standard textbook usage.",

"id": "np5-sepsis-septic-shock",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition",
"body": "## What it is\nSepsis is a life-threatening response to infection that injures the body's own organs. Septic shock is sepsis with dangerously low blood pressure and poor tissue perfusion that persists despite fluids. Both are time-critical.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Sources: pneumonia, urinary tract, abdomen, wounds and burns, lines and catheters. Higher risk: older adults, infants, diabetes, immune suppression, recent surgery, invasive devices. In burns, sepsis is a leading cause of illness because skin protection is lost and immunity is depressed ([[Burn Pathophysiology and Whole-Body Response]]).\n- Septic shock is a distributive shock: bacterial toxins (often gram-negative) make vessels leak plasma into tissue and dilate ([[Distributive Shock: Neurogenic, Anaphylactic and Septic]]).\n- Early clues: fever or low temperature, fast pulse, fast breathing, confusion, warm flushed skin early, reduced urine output. Later: low blood pressure, cool mottled skin, high lactate.\n- Quick bedside screens exist (such as qSOFA: fast breathing, altered mentation, low systolic pressure); verify criteria in your textbook.\n- Early care principles (often called a bundle): recognise early, get blood cultures before antibiotics without delaying them, give broad-spectrum antibiotics early, give IV fluids, measure lactate, start vasopressors if pressure stays low, find and control the source (remove infected lines, drain abscesses). Exact timing, volumes and drug choices follow current guidelines ([[Vasopressors for Hypotension and Shock]]).\n- Complications: shock, acute respiratory distress, kidney injury, DIC and multiple organ failure ([[DIC and Multiple Organ Dysfunction Recognition]]).\n- Older adults and neonates may show confusion, poor feeding or low temperature rather than fever.\n- Prevention is nursing work: hand hygiene, aseptic technique for lines and wounds, early removal of catheters, mouth care and early mobilisation.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Temperature, pulse, breathing, blood pressure trend, mental status, skin, urine output, wound and line sites, laboratory results (white count, lactate, cultures).\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: infection triggers body-wide inflammation -> vessels dilate and leak -> pressure falls and organs starve. Because organ damage progresses by the hour, the first action is early recognition and escalation, then cultures and antibiotics, then fluids.\n- Why cultures before antibiotics: antibiotics sterilise the sample, but never wait long for cultures if it delays treatment.\n- Safety and infection control: strict hand hygiene protects the next client.\n- Assess before intervening; least invasive first: fluids and antibiotics before vasopressors; ABC and oxygen throughout.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Recognise and escalate at once.\n2. Airway, breathing, oxygen, IV access.\n3. Collect cultures, then give antibiotics promptly as ordered.\n4. Give fluids as ordered; monitor response and urine output.\n5. Prevent spread: hand hygiene, remove unneeded lines as ordered.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nEarly subtle changes (new confusion in an older adult) as the first sign. Which order: cultures before antibiotics but do not delay antibiotics. Trap: waiting for fever. Another: identifying warm flushed skin as an early septic shock sign.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Types of Shock]], [[Management of Shock]], [[ICU Monitoring Concepts]], [[Burns: Causes and Overview]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Definitions (these have changed over time), bundle timing, screening tools, fluid and drug details.",

"id": "np5-dic-mods",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "DIC and Multiple Organ Dysfunction Recognition",
"body": "## What it is\nDisseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) is widespread, abnormal clotting that uses up clotting factors and platelets, so the client both clots and bleeds. Multiple organ dysfunction syndrome (MODS) is failure of two or more organ systems, often after shock, sepsis, trauma or burns.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- DIC is always secondary to something: sepsis, major trauma, burns, obstetric emergencies (abruptio placentae, amniotic fluid embolism, retained dead fetus; see [[Postpartum Hemorrhage]]), cancer, transfusion reactions ([[Blood Transfusion Reactions]]), snakebite.\n- Signs of bleeding: oozing from IV sites, nosebleeds, gum bleeding, blood in urine or stool, bruises and tiny red spots, bleeding from wounds. Signs of clotting: cold blue fingers or toes, organ ischaemia, confusion, low urine output.\n- Typical laboratory pattern: low platelets, prolonged clotting times, raised fibrin breakdown products (such as D-dimer) and low fibrinogen; verify values.\n- Management focuses on treating the cause, supporting circulation, and replacing blood products as ordered. Avoid injections and rough handling where possible.\n- MODS: kidney, lung, liver, heart, gut, brain and blood systems may fail one after another. Prevention is the main strategy: treat shock early, control infection, support nutrition, and monitor trends. Burn care teaches the same principle: \"resuscitate\", meaning immediate support for any failing organ system.\n- Late irreversible shock leads to MODS: anuria, ARDS, liver and gut failure ([[Early vs Late Stages of Shock]], [[Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)]]).\n- Blood is typed and cross-matched early; fresh frozen plasma, platelets and cryoprecipitate are the usual products; check each product with a second nurse ([[Blood Products and Preparing a Transfusion]]).\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- Bleeding sites, skin and extremity colour, vital signs, urine output, mental status, laboratory trend, oxygen saturation.\n- Measure blood loss: weigh pads, record drainage, measure abdominal girth or limb size if concealed bleeding is suspected.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: a trigger switches on clotting everywhere -> clotting factors and platelets are used up -> bleeding appears in unrelated places while small vessels block. So treat the trigger first, and protect the client from any extra bleeding.\n- Why avoid injections and invasive procedures: every puncture is a new bleeding site; use pressure for longer than usual.\n- Priority order: airway, breathing, circulation (stop and replace blood), then cause, then comfort.\n- Early recognition: bleeding at several unrelated sites in a very sick client is DIC until proved otherwise; report at once.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Report early signs of bleeding or unexpected bruising in a sick client.\n2. Protect from injury: gentle handling, soft toothbrush, pressure on puncture sites, avoid intramuscular injections.\n3. Give blood products and drugs as ordered; monitor reactions.\n4. Support each failing organ and monitor trends.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich clients are at risk (sepsis, obstetric emergency, trauma). Which finding means DIC is developing (bleeding from several unrelated sites). Trap: treating the bleeding alone without the cause; choosing an intramuscular injection.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition]], [[Early vs Late Stages of Shock]], [[ICU Monitoring Concepts]], [[Complete Blood Count and Reference Values]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Lab reference values, product choice and heparin use in DIC (debated), and the MODS scoring systems. No handout in your set covers DIC, so this rests on standard textbook knowledge.",

"id": "np5-icu-monitoring",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "ICU Monitoring Concepts",
"body": "## What it is\nIntensive care uses continuous monitoring and rapid action to catch deterioration early in acutely ill clients. The nurse interprets trends, responds to alarms and acts before the client collapses.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Continuous ECG, pulse oximetry, and frequent blood pressure; arterial lines give beat-to-beat pressure and sample blood for gases. Zero and level the transducer as taught; damped waveforms need troubleshooting. Before an arterial puncture or line at the wrist, the Allen test checks that the ulnar artery supplies the hand (colour should return within about 6 seconds) ([[Arterial Blood Gases and Allen's Test]]).\n- Pulse oximetry: normal is 95 percent or higher; sensor sites include fingertip, earlobe, forehead and toe ([[Chest X-ray and Pulse Oximetry]]). ECG monitoring reads rhythm ([[ECG Waves and Intervals]]).\n- Arterial blood gas normals: pH 7.35 to 7.45, PaO2 80 to 100, PaCO2 35 to 45, HCO3 22 to 26 ([[Arterial Blood Gas Basics]]). Press the puncture site at least 5 minutes (15 minutes if on anticoagulants), and send the sample on ice.\n- Central venous catheters give access for drugs and pressure measurement; risks include infection, air embolism and bleeding.\n- Mean arterial pressure reflects organ perfusion; urine output (an hourly indwelling catheter measure; about 30 mL an hour or more in adults) is an easy bedside measure of kidney perfusion; mental status reflects brain perfusion.\n- Mechanical ventilation: watch the alarms, secretions, tube position, sedation level, and comfort. Prevent ventilator-associated pneumonia with mouth care, head-of-bed elevation, hand hygiene and daily review of readiness to wean. Turn the client frequently, give nutrition (the ARDS handout suggests 35 to 45 kcal/kg/day) and rest to cut oxygen use.\n- Neurological monitoring: GCS, pupils, intracranial pressure when a device is placed ([[Level of Consciousness and Glasgow Coma Scale]]).\n- Sedation and delirium are assessed with scales; pain is assessed even in clients who cannot speak.\n- Early warning scores combine vital signs into a trigger for escalation.\n- Never silence or ignore an alarm without checking the client first.\n- Emergency equipment (suction, oxygen, bag-valve-mask, defibrillator) is checked at the start of each shift.\n\n## What the nurse assesses\n- The client first, then the monitor. Trends over hours, not single numbers. Lines, tubes and drains each shift.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause -> finding -> priority: a worrying number may mean the patient or the equipment; look at the client first (airway, breathing, circulation), then the probe, lead or line. Treat the client, not the monitor.\n- Why trends matter: single readings vary with position, movement and probes; a steady drift in pulse, urine or mental state shows true deterioration.\n- Safety: infection prevention for every device; secure tubes; keep alarms on and audible.\n- Assess before intervening; escalate early with clear data (what changed, since when, what you did).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Look at the client before the monitor.\n2. Check ABCs when an alarm sounds.\n3. Escalate trends early.\n4. Prevent device infections and pressure injuries; document.\n5. Keep family informed and support them.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA monitor shows a worrying number but the client looks fine (check equipment, e.g. probe, then recheck the client). Or a client looks poor but numbers look acceptable (trust assessment). Another: the first action when a ventilator alarm sounds (assess the client, disconnect and bag if needed).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Respiratory Failure Recognition]], [[Management of Shock]], [[Disability and Exposure in the Primary Survey]], [[Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Normal pressure ranges, ventilator settings and local ICU protocols.",

"id": "np5-pc-mental-health-history",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Mental Health Concepts and History of Psychiatric Care",
"body": "## What it is\nMental health is a state of emotional, psychological and social wellness shown by satisfying relationships, effective behavior and coping, a positive self-concept and emotional stability. A mental disorder is a clinically significant behavioral or psychological pattern that comes with distress or disability, or with a raised risk of suffering, pain, death or loss of freedom.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Components of mental health: autonomy and independence, reaching one's potential, tolerating life's uncertainties, self-esteem, mastery of the environment, reality orientation and stress management.\n- Ancient times: illness was seen as the gods' displeasure. People with mental illness were thought divine (worshipped) or demonic (punished or burned). Aristotle's humoral idea tied mood to balance of body fluids; treatment was bloodletting, starving and purging.\n- Early Christian era: illness blamed on demons and possession; treatment was exorcism, then dungeons, flogging and starvation if it failed.\n- Renaissance: the mentally ill began to be separated from criminals. Harmless people wandered; dangerous ones were jailed and chained. The Hospital of St. Mary of Bethlehem was the first hospital for the insane, but inmates were treated like animals. Witch hunts also happened.\n- Enlightenment: Philippe Pinel and William Tuke created the idea of the asylum as a safe refuge. Dorothea Dix pushed for adequate shelter, food and warm clothing.\n- Emil Kraepelin classified mental disorders by symptoms; Eugene Bleuler coined the term schizophrenia.\n\n## Why the trend matters\nThe story moves from blame and punishment to shelter, then to classification and humane treatment. Exam items usually ask who is credited with what.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch a name to a contribution (Pinel and Tuke: asylum; Dix: humane conditions; Kraepelin: classification; Bleuler: schizophrenia). Another common item asks for a sign of good mental health.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented: Pinel and Tuke give a \"place of safety\"; Dix gives the \"daily needs\"; Kraepelin \"categorizes\"; Bleuler \"names schizophrenia\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Psychiatric Nursing Overview]], [[Therapeutic Use of Self and Johari's Window]], [[Nurse-Patient Relationship Phases]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout gave the Aristotle humor idea; many books credit Hippocrates. Check who your textbook names.",

"id": "np5-pc-therapeutic-use-of-self",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Therapeutic Use of Self and Johari's Window",
"body": "## What it is\nTherapeutic use of self means the nurse uses his or her own personality and presence as the main tool for building a helping relationship. Hildegard Peplau (1952) stressed that a nurse who understands herself can help the client grow.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- It depends on self-awareness: knowing your own feelings, beliefs and attitudes.\n- Goal of self-awareness: keep your values and biases from being projected onto the client and interfering with care.\n- Johari's Window has four quadrants:\n  - Quadrant I, open (arena or public self): known to you and to others.\n  - Quadrant II, blind self: known to others but not to you.\n  - Quadrant III, hidden (facade or private self): known only to you.\n  - Quadrant IV, unknown: not yet discovered by you or anyone.\n- Ways to grow self-awareness: role play, introspection, discussion with others (feedback), and enlarging your experience.\n- In the pre-orientation phase the main task is developing self-awareness; see [[Nurse-Patient Relationship Phases]].\n- Characteristics of the relationship that uses the self: goal-directed, client-focused, planned, time-limited, professional. Basic elements: TRUST (trust, rapport, unconditional positive regard, setting limits, therapeutic communication).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Reflect on your own reaction before reacting to a difficult client.\n2. Ask for feedback to shrink the blind quadrant.\n3. Notice countertransference and discuss it in supervision.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Strong feelings about a client are information about you. Name them, discuss them in supervision, and keep the plan centered on the client.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nIdentify the quadrant from a description (a trait that coworkers see but the nurse does not is the blind self), or name the nurse's main tool.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Mental Health Concepts and History of Psychiatric Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout credits Peplau for the term; confirm in your own text.",

"area": "NP5",
"title": "Group, Milieu and Family Therapy",
"body": "## What it is\nThese are therapies that use the people and surroundings around the client. Each treats the client in a social setting rather than one-to-one.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Group therapy: three or more clients with a therapist to relieve emotional difficulty, build self-esteem, gain insight and improve relationships. Minimum three members; ideal group is 8 to 10.\n- Types of group:\n  - Therapeutic group: insight into problems (Alcoholics Anonymous is the handout's example).\n  - Socialization group: improves interaction among clients.\n  - Life review or reminiscing group: lessens isolation, often for older adults.\n- Milieu therapy: planned control of the environment so that the setting itself promotes positive change. The nurse looks at what each client needs while remembering the needs of the whole group.\n- Family therapy: treats the family as an interacting system; the handout lists it as best suited when there is domestic violence.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Keep the unit safe, predictable and structured (milieu).\n2. In a group, set rules, keep confidentiality, and make sure everyone has a chance to take part.\n3. In family work, involve all members and avoid taking sides.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nChoose the therapy that matches the goal (isolation in older adults: reminiscing group; changing the unit environment: milieu) or state group size.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]], [[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Expressive and Insight Therapies]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Many books describe family therapy as useful for many family problems, not only domestic violence; check your text.",

"area": "NP5",
"title": "Expressive and Insight Therapies",
"body": "## What it is\nA set of talk, art, play and relaxation approaches used on the psychiatric unit, from deep exploration of the unconscious to simple activities that ease tension.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Psychoanalysis: explores the unconscious and the client's defenses. Behavior problems are linked to unresolved, anxiety-provoking childhood experiences that were repressed. Goal: bring them into awareness and learn healthier coping. Tools: dream analysis and free association (saying thoughts aloud without censoring).\n- Hypnotherapy: induces a trance in which the client is highly open to instruction.\n- Remotivation therapy: discussion of neutral topics to promote expression of feelings. Reality orientation suits rehabilitation clients, not clients who are actively psychotic.\n- Music therapy: relaxation, expression of feelings and release of tension.\n- Play therapy: lets a child feel intense emotion safely through play; dolls are used with abused children.\n- Humor therapy: laughter eases tension that comes with serious matters and encourages interaction.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Match the therapy to the client's level of function and safety.\n2. Do not push reality orientation on a client who is acutely psychotic; that may increase agitation.\n3. Observe and record the client's responses.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPick the therapy for a described goal (child abuse victim: play therapy with dolls; free association: psychoanalysis).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms]], [[Group, Milieu and Family Therapy]], [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]]",

"id": "np5-pc-behavior-modification",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Behavior Modification and Reinforcement",
"body": "## What it is\nBehavior modification applies learning principles to change maladaptive behavior. It strengthens a wanted behavior by reinforcement.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Positive reinforcement: giving something pleasant after the behavior, such as attention and praise. Example: a group leader praises the client each time he uses assertive communication.\n- Negative reinforcement: removing something unpleasant right after the behavior so the behavior is more likely to be repeated. Example: an anxious client volunteers to speak first so the anxious waiting ends.\n- Both increase the behavior. Negative reinforcement is NOT punishment.\n- Behavior modification targets a clear, observable behavior. Reinforcers must follow the behavior at once to work.\n- Typical psychiatric uses: assertiveness practice in groups, eating-disorder contracts, limit setting in personality disorders, rewards for self-care in schizophrenia.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Define the target behavior clearly.\n2. Reinforce the desired behavior immediately and consistently.\n3. Keep all staff using the same plan.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Ask two questions: was a stimulus added or taken away, and did the behavior increase. Added and increased: positive reinforcement. Removed and increased: negative reinforcement. Unwanted behavior that is ignored so it fades is extinction (general knowledge).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nDifferentiate positive from negative reinforcement: ask whether something was added or removed, and whether the behavior increased.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Developmental Disorders]], [[Expressive and Insight Therapies]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]], [[Personality Disorders]], [[Anorexia Nervosa and Bulimia Nervosa: Findings and Treatment]]",

"id": "np5-pc-electroconvulsive-therapy",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Electroconvulsive Therapy",
"body": "## What it is\nElectroconvulsive therapy (ECT) delivers a brief electric current to the brain under anesthesia to produce a generalized tonic-clonic seizure. A seizure is the sign it worked.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Used for severe depression that does not respond to drugs, and for clients actively suicidal who cannot wait weeks for an antidepressant. Also listed: mania, catatonia and schizophrenia.\n- Typical course: 6 to 12 treatments (up to 15), given with about 48 hours between sessions (commonly three per week). Current lasts about 0.5 to 2 seconds.\n- Needs informed consent.\n- Medications: atropine to reduce secretions, methohexital as the short-acting anesthetic, succinylcholine as the muscle relaxant to soften the seizure. The brain is monitored with EEG.\n- High-risk conditions: raised intracranial pressure, recent fracture, cardiac disease, retinal detachment, pregnancy.\n- Complications: memory loss, headache, apnea, respiratory depression, fracture.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nBefore:\n1. Confirm signed consent, NPO after midnight, have the client void, start an IV, remove dentures and jewelry.\nAfter (airway first, ABCs):\n1. Ventilate with 100% oxygen until the client breathes unassisted, and watch breathing.\n2. Reorient to time, place and person; expect brief confusion and short-term memory loss.\n3. Observe until oriented and steady, especially when first standing; protect from falls.\n4. Give a benzodiazepine if ordered for agitation; document the treatment.\n\n## Client and family teaching\n- Temporary confusion and memory gaps are common and usually improve; treatments are repeated over weeks.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPriority after ECT is airway and breathing, then reorientation and safety. Another item asks for a contraindication or the purpose of each drug.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Schizophrenia]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout lists the voltage as \"70-50 volts\", which looks garbled; verify. Whether schizophrenia and mania are indications varies by source.",

"title": "Crisis Types, Phases and Intervention Styles",
"body": "## What it is\nFills out the handout's view of crisis: how crises are grouped, how they progress, and the two ways a nurse can intervene. See [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]] for the core concept.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Features of a crisis: highly individual, usually lasts about 4 to 6 weeks, the person becomes passive and dependent, and the support system is affected.\n- Maturational (developmental): expected and predictable life events, such as leaving home, marriage, having a baby, starting a career.\n- Situational (accidental): sudden and unexpected external events, such as death of a loved one, job loss, illness or an accident.\n- Social (adventitious): disasters and violence, such as floods, earthquakes, war, terrorism, riots, rape or murder.\n- Four phases: denial; increased tension (still doing daily activities); disorganization (cannot do daily activities); attempts to reorganize (previous coping mechanisms mobilized).\n- Goal of crisis intervention: reach the best possible level of functioning.\n- Authoritative style: assesses, gives information, raises self-awareness through feedback, and directs behavior with suggestions.\n- Facilitative style: meets the need for empathy by encouraging the client to name feelings, acting as a sounding board and affirming self-worth.\n- The nurse's role is active and directive.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Assess safety first.\n2. Take a directive approach during disorganization.\n3. Encourage use of past successful coping and support people.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nClassify the crisis from a short story, or identify the phase from what the client can still do.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Trauma and Stressor-Related Disorders]], [[Rape, Rape Trauma Syndrome and Survivor Care]], [[Disaster Management Continuum]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Other books describe different phase counts and durations; use the model your course teaches.",

"title": "Rape, Rape Trauma Syndrome and Survivor Care",
"body": "## What it is\nRape is a crime of violence and humiliation expressed through sexual means. It is generally an act of hostility, anger or violence, not of sexual desire. Under Philippine law (RA 8353) the act includes insertion of the penis into the mouth, vagina or anus without consent. Consent is not valid if the victim was forced, threatened, drugged or intoxicated, mentally incapable of judging, or below the age of consent.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Power rape: the aim is to command and master the victim; the rapist feels inadequate and uses rape to prove potency.\n- Sadistic rape: brutality, bondage and torture; the assault itself is eroticized.\n- Rape trauma syndrome phases: acute (shock, numbness, disbelief); denial (refuses to talk about it); heightened anxiety (fear, tension, nightmares); reorganization (life normalizes).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Give immediate emotional support in a private, safe place; stay with the client.\n2. Let her proceed at her own pace; do not rush interviews or examination.\n3. Return control: let her decide whom to call and what happens next.\n4. Preserve evidence (a priority in care); consent must be signed before photographs and before hair and nail samples are taken. Avoid bathing or changing clothes before collection.\n5. Offer prophylaxis for sexually transmitted infection and pregnancy; HIV testing may be encouraged; refer to a rape crisis center.\n6. The decision to file charges and testify belongs to the victim.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nBest response: nonjudgmental support and client control. Wrong answers push the victim, blame her, or reassure falsely.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Crisis Types, Phases and Intervention Styles]], [[Trauma and Stressor-Related Disorders]], [[VAWC and Violence Against Women and Children]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout describes rape only for female victims; RA 8353 also covers sexual assault of any person, and male survivors need the same care. Check the law and local protocols (Women and Children Protection Unit).",

"id": "np5-pc-eps-nms",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Extrapyramidal Symptoms and Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome",
"body": "## What it is\nMovement side effects of antipsychotics, caused by dopamine (D2) blockade in the brain's motor pathways, plus the dangerous neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS). See [[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]] for the drug classes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Acute dystonia (early, an emergency): torticollis, opisthotonus, oculogyric crisis, muscle rigidity and cramping, thick tongue, difficulty swallowing, laryngospasm, respiratory difficulty. Treat with IM benztropine or IM or IV diphenhydramine.\n- Pseudoparkinsonism: stooped posture, mask-like face, reduced arm swing, shuffling festinating gait, cogwheel rigidity, drooling, pill-rolling at rest. Treat by changing to a drug with fewer EPS, or adding an anticholinergic or amantadine.\n- Akathisia: cannot sit still, restless, anxious. Treat by changing the drug or adding a beta-blocker, anticholinergic or benzodiazepine.\n- Tardive dyskinesia: worm-like tongue movements, lip smacking, involuntary body movements; appears after prolonged therapy (handout says at least 8 months), may be permanent. Valbenazine and deutetrabenazine are used; lowering the antipsychotic may stop progression.\n- NMS: potentially fatal reaction with rigidity, high fever, unstable blood pressure, sweating, pallor, delirium, and swings from agitation to stupor. Stop all antipsychotics at once and treat dehydration and hyperthermia.\n- Drugs used for EPS include amantadine, benztropine, biperiden, procyclidine, trihexyphenidyl, diphenhydramine, diazepam and propranolol.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway first: laryngospasm or trouble swallowing in dystonia is an emergency.\n2. Fever plus rigidity: hold the drug, call the provider, cool and hydrate.\n3. Screen regularly for tardive dyskinesia.\n4. Teach the client to report stiffness, restlessness or fever.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nName the EPS from a description, choose the first drug for dystonia, or pick NMS signs over EPS signs (fever and autonomic instability).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Schizophrenia]], [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Lithium Therapy and Toxicity]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Time frame for tardive dyskinesia varies; verify. Dosing is not given here.",

"id": "np5-pc-lithium-toxicity",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Lithium Therapy and Toxicity",
"body": "## What it is\nLithium is the best-established mood stabilizer for bipolar disorder. It steadies mood, prevents highs and lows, and treats acute mania. Other mood stabilizers include carbamazepine, valproic acid, gabapentin and lamotrigine. See [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]].\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Forms: tablets, capsules and sustained-release liquid; no injectable form.\n- Handout levels: normal 0.5 to 1.5 mEq/L; therapeutic 0.6 to 1.2 mEq/L. Above 3.0 mEq/L, dialysis is considered.\n- Common side effects: mild nausea and diarrhea, loss of appetite, fine hand tremor, increased thirst and urination, metallic taste, fatigue.\n- Toxic signs: severe vomiting and diarrhea, muscle weakness, poor coordination; untreated can lead to kidney failure, coma and death.\n- Valproic acid: can cause liver failure. Carbamazepine: aplastic anemia and agranulocytosis. Lamotrigine: Stevens-Johnson syndrome (severe rash).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Check serum levels regularly; hold the dose and report toxic signs.\n2. Give with food to reduce nausea.\n3. Keep sodium and fluid intake normal; avoid heavy sweating and dehydration because lithium levels rise quickly.\n4. For tremor, a beta-blocker such as propranolol may be requested.\n5. Expect some weight gain; teach a balanced diet and exercise.\n6. Anticonvulsants: sedation can be eased with the larger dose at bedtime.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nRecognize toxicity (coarse tremor, vomiting, ataxia), choose the safest advice (steady salt and fluids), or match adverse effects to each mood stabilizer.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Extrapyramidal Symptoms and Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Verify the target level (many books give 0.8 to 1.2 mEq/L) and when to draw the level (about 12 hours after the last dose).",

"area": "NP5",
"title": "Antianxiety Drugs: Benzodiazepines and Buspirone",
"body": "## What it is\nAnxiolytics treat anxiety, insomnia, alcohol withdrawal, and sometimes OCD, depression and post-traumatic stress. Benzodiazepines are the most effective for quick relief.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Benzodiazepines in the handout: alprazolam, chlordiazepoxide, clonazepam, diazepam, flurazepam, lorazepam, oxazepam, temazepam, triazolam. Buspirone is a non-benzodiazepine option (non-sedating, slow onset).\n- They relieve symptoms; they do not treat the cause of anxiety.\n- They strongly add to the effect of alcohol: one drink may act like three.\n- Drowsiness: avoid driving and dangerous tasks.\n- Withdrawal from benzodiazepines can be fatal; never stop abruptly without medical supervision.\n- Take only as prescribed.\n- Handout adds sleep-related and other benzodiazepines: flurazepam and temazepam and triazolam are mainly for insomnia. Alcohol withdrawal and PTSD are also listed uses.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety: fall precautions, sedation and respiratory checks.\n2. Teach no alcohol, no abrupt stopping, no driving until effect is known.\n3. Encourage non-drug coping skills with the medication.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Danger ranking: abrupt stopping (withdrawal can be fatal) and alcohol or other sedatives together (breathing depression) outrank drowsiness and memory problems. Teach those first.\n- Buspirone does not give instant relief, so a client who wants fast effect needs explanation, not a dose increase.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPick the teaching point (no alcohol, taper off) or the drug with low abuse risk and slow action (buspirone).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Downers: Barbiturates and Opioids]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout calls SSRIs the safest drug in panic and advises giving anxiolytics before meals; both are doubtful. Verify drug timing and alternatives. See [[Anxiolytic Drugs and Cognitive-Behavioral Techniques for Anxiety]].",

"area": "NP5",
"title": "Autism Spectrum Disorder",
"body": "## What it is\nA neurodevelopmental disorder with impaired communication and social interaction plus stereotyped behaviors, interests and activities. It is more common in boys, shows before about 3 years of age, has a genetic link, and is treatable but not curable. The main problem is impaired interpersonal functioning.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Signs: little eye contact, few facial expressions or gestures, does not relate to peers and parents, little spontaneous enjoyment, flat affect, little intelligible speech, stereotyped movements (hand flapping, body twisting, head banging), may seem deaf, no fear of danger.\n- Tantrums may include head banging.\n- Low-dose antipsychotic may be used for severe behaviors.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first: risk for injury is the priority diagnosis. Protect from head banging with a helmet and padded surroundings; observe closely, even 1:1.\n2. Keep a consistent routine and introduce change slowly.\n3. Use short, simple sentences when speaking.\n4. Offer small, frequent, balanced meals for nutrition below body needs.\n5. Support the family (love and belonging): family therapy and parent teaching.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Risk for injury is the priority because the child has no fear of danger and may head-bang. Protect first, then communication and routine.\n- A change in routine can trigger a tantrum. Introduce changes slowly and keep staff consistent.\n- Short sentences work because the child understands little speech; do not overload.\n- Family therapy and teaching meet the love and belonging need of the parents too.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPriority diagnosis is risk for injury; best approach is routine, short sentences and consistent staff.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Developmental Disorders]], [[Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder]], [[Intellectual Disability]], [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout line \"all vowels\" under communication is unclear and was left out. Current criteria are usually described as one spectrum, not separate diagnoses.",

"title": "Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder",
"body": "## What it is\nA childhood disorder of inattention, overactivity and impulsiveness, more common in boys, diagnosed around preschool or school entry (symptoms before age 7 in the handout).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Causes: unknown; linked to neurologic impairment, early malnutrition, reduced frontal lobe blood flow, and drugs, alcohol or tobacco in pregnancy.\n- Risks: family history, relatives with antisocial personality disorder, lower socioeconomic status, male sex, family discord.\n- Inattentive: misses details, careless mistakes, cannot sustain attention, seems not to listen, does not finish chores, disorganized, avoids mental effort, loses things.\n- Hyperactive-impulsive: fidgets, leaves seat, runs and climbs, cannot play quietly, always on the go, talks too much, blurts, interrupts, cannot wait turn.\n- Stimulants: methylphenidate, dextroamphetamine, pemoline. Side effects: loss of appetite, weight loss, nausea, irritability, dizziness, dry mouth, blurred vision, palpitations. Pemoline can cause life-threatening liver failure.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety: stop unsafe behavior, supervise closely, give clear limits.\n2. Give methylphenidate after meals to limit appetite loss; give dextroamphetamine's last dose early afternoon to prevent insomnia. Monitor weight and height (growth).\n3. Simplify directions: get full attention, break tasks into small steps, allow breaks.\n4. Keep a structured daily routine with few changes; quiet place for tasks.\n5. Offer finger foods; avoid caffeine and excess sugar.\n6. Praise success; listen to parents' frustration; store drugs out of the child's reach; give medicine without drawing attention to the child.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStimulant side effect/monitoring, or choosing structure and positive feedback.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Developmental Disorders]], [[Autism Spectrum Disorder]], [[Behavior Modification and Reinforcement]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Pemoline has been withdrawn in many countries. Time to full effect in the handout (2 days) is unusual; verify. Current criteria allow onset before age 12.",

"area": "NP5",
"title": "Intellectual Disability",
"body": "## What it is\nBelow-average intellectual functioning (IQ below about 70) with significant limits in adaptive functioning. The handout uses the older term mental retardation.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Causes: hereditary (Tay-Sachs disease, trisomy 21), pregnancy and birth problems (fetal malnutrition), medical conditions in infancy.\n- Mild: IQ 55 to 69, educable (can learn to read and write).\n- Moderate: IQ 40 to 54, trainable (vocational skills such as cooking or sewing).\n- Severe: IQ 25 to 39, needs close supervision (basic daily activities such as brushing and dressing).\n- Profound: IQ below 25, custodial care, totally dependent.\n- Nursing care: repetition, role modelling, restructuring the environment. Education focus for the educable: reading, writing, basic arithmetic.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Meet safety and basic needs first.\n2. Teach in small steps with repetition; use concrete language.\n3. Encourage self-care matched to level.\n4. Support the family and arrange community services.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Match goals to level: a profound client needs total care, not academic teaching. Safety and basic needs come first at the severe and profound levels.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch level to IQ range or to teaching capacity.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Developmental Disorders]], [[Autism Spectrum Disorder]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- IQ bands and labels vary by manual; newer manuals grade by adaptive function. Same content appears in [[Intellectual Disability: Levels and Care]].",

"id": "np5-x-therapeutic-techniques",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Therapeutic Communication Techniques with Examples",
"body": "## What it is\nA working list of helpful nurse responses, each with the example sentence and the reason it helps. Questions often give a sentence and ask which technique it is.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Accepting: signals you heard and followed; \"Yes\", \"I follow what you said\", nodding. It does not mean agreement.\n- Broad opening: lets the client choose the topic; \"Where would you like to begin?\"\n- Consensual validation: checks that both people mean the same thing by a word; \"Are you using this word to convey that?\"\n- Encouraging comparison: \"Have you had similar experiences?\" It may recall past coping.\n- Encouraging description of perceptions: \"What does the voice seem to be saying?\" Helps the nurse see the client's view.\n- Encouraging expression: \"What are your feelings about...?\" The client makes his own appraisal.\n- Exploring: \"Tell me more about that.\" Respect refusal.\n- Focusing: concentrate on one point; useful when the client jumps from topic to topic.\n- Formulating a plan of action: \"Next time this comes up, what might you do?\" Planning ahead helps coping.\n- General leads: \"Go on\", \"And then?\" The client keeps the initiative.\n- Giving information: name, purpose, visiting hours. Builds trust.\n- Giving recognition: noticing efforts or changes; \"I noticed you combed your hair.\"\n- Making observations: \"You appear tense.\" Used when the client cannot put things in words.\n- Offering self: \"I will sit with you a while.\" Unconditional presence.\n- Placing events in time sequence: \"What led up to it? Before or after?\" Shows cause and effect.\n- Presenting reality: \"I see no one else in the room.\" Offers what is real without arguing.\n- Reflecting: sends the question or feeling back; \"This makes you feel angry?\" Restating: repeats the main idea.\n- Seeking information: \"I am not sure I follow.\" Avoids assuming.\n- Silence: gives time to organize thoughts.\n- Suggesting collaboration: \"Perhaps you and I can discover what triggers your anxiety.\"\n- Summarizing: pulls the discussion together.\n- Translating into feelings: \"I am way out in the ocean\" becomes \"You seem to feel lonely.\"\n- Verbalizing the implied: \"Do you feel that no one understands?\"\n- Voicing doubt: \"Isn't that unusual?\" Lets the client see others do not perceive things the same way.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Match the technique to the need. Client rambles: focusing. Client silent but trying: silence or general lead. Client symbolic: translating into feelings. Client misperceiving: presenting reality.\n- Feeling comes before fact, and the client leads.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Non-Therapeutic Communication Blocks with Examples]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Names and examples differ between books; learn your own list.",

"id": "np5-x-nontherapeutic-blocks",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Non-Therapeutic Communication Blocks with Examples",
"body": "## What it is\nReplies that close the conversation, hurt trust or take control away from the client. Each has an example and the harm it does.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Advising: \"I think you should...\" Implies only the nurse knows best.\n- Agreeing and giving approval: \"That is right\", \"That is good.\" Limits the client's freedom and may push him to please you.\n- Belittling feelings: \"Everybody gets down in the dumps.\" Implies the pain is mild and temporary.\n- Challenging: \"If you are dead, why is your heart beating?\" Makes the client defend delusions.\n- Defending: \"The hospital has a fine reputation.\" Says the client has no right to an opinion.\n- Disagreeing: \"That is wrong.\" Makes the client defensive.\n- Giving literal responses: answering a figurative remark as fact (\"Try not to watch television\" to the camera-in-my-head statement).\n- Indicating an external source: \"Who told you that you were a prophet?\" Implies he was made to think.\n- Interpreting: \"What you really mean is...\" Thoughts belong to the client.\n- Introducing an unrelated topic: \"Did you have visitors last night?\" after \"I would like to die.\" Takes the lead away.\n- Stereotyped comments: clichés, \"Tell me your psychiatric history.\"\n- False reassurance: \"Everything will be all right.\" Devalues the feeling.\n- Rejecting: \"Let us not discuss...\" Client feels personally rejected.\n- Requesting an explanation: \"Why do you feel that way?\" Intimidates.\n- Testing: \"Do you know what kind of hospital this is?\" Forces insight.\n- Using denial: \"Do not be silly\" to \"I am dead.\" Dismisses the feeling.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- A blocking reply sounds like advice, reassurance, judgment, a \"why\" or a topic switch. Eliminate those first, then choose the reply that explores the feeling.\n- A statement about suicide must be explored directly; changing the subject is the worst choice.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Therapeutic Communication Techniques with Examples]], [[Therapeutic Communication]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some books allow a gentle \"why\" in a research interview; avoid it in clinical stems.",

"title": "Psychotropic Medication Classes: Which Drug for Which Problem",
"body": "## What it is\nA one-page map of the main psychiatric drug groups with the drugs and the uses the review handout lists.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Antipsychotics (neuroleptics): schizophrenia first, also psychotic depression, acute mania, drug-induced psychosis. Typical: haloperidol, chlorpromazine, perphenazine, fluphenazine, thioridazine, mesoridazine, trifluoperazine. Atypical: clozapine, risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, ziprasidone, aripiprazole.\n- Antidepressants: major depression, anxiety disorders, depressed phase of bipolar disorder, psychotic depression. Classes: SSRI, TCA, MAOI. See [[Tricyclic and SSRI Antidepressants: Drug Lists and Nursing Care]].\n- Mood stabilizers: bipolar disorder. Lithium plus carbamazepine, valproic acid, gabapentin, lamotrigine.\n- Anxiolytics: anxiety, insomnia, OCD, depression, PTSD, alcohol withdrawal. Benzodiazepines and buspirone.\n- Stimulants: now mainly ADHD in children and teens: methylphenidate, dextroamphetamine, pemoline.\n- Substance-use drugs: disulfiram, naltrexone, methadone, clonidine.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Match drug family to the problem first, then the most dangerous effect of that family (EPS and NMS, hypertensive crisis, toxicity, withdrawal).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]], [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Antianxiety Drugs: Benzodiazepines and Buspirone]], [[Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some listed drugs are rarely used today.",

"area": "NP5",
"title": "Antipsychotic Anticholinergic Effects, Clozapine Alert and Client Teaching",
"body": "## What it is\nThe comfort and safety effects of antipsychotics that are not EPS, and the teaching that goes with them.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Anticholinergic effects: orthostatic hypotension, dry mouth, constipation, urinary hesitancy or retention, blurred vision, dry eyes, photophobia, nasal congestion, decreased memory. They lessen in 3 to 4 weeks but do not fully go away.\n- Care: stool softeners, calorie-free fluids, adequate fluid, grains and fruit; sugar-free hard candy for dry mouth; exercise; sunscreen and protective covering against photosensitivity; rise slowly; avoid driving until drowsiness and dizziness settle.\n- Missed dose (handout rule): take it if only 3 to 4 hours late; skip it if later or if the next dose is due. Use a chart or labelled pill box.\n- Clozapine: risk of agranulocytosis; baseline white count and differential, then weekly counts and for 4 weeks after stopping.\n- QT alert: droperidol, thioridazine and mesoridazine.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Report sore throat or fever on clozapine at once.\n2. Prevent falls from orthostatic hypotension.\n3. Relieve constipation and dry mouth.\n4. Teach photosensitivity precautions.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Rank by danger: blood count and rhythm risks above comfort effects.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Typical and Atypical Antipsychotics]], [[Extrapyramidal Symptoms and Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Missed-dose and white-cell schedules vary by drug and policy.",

"title": "Tricyclic and SSRI Antidepressants: Drug Lists and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nThe two big older and newer groups of antidepressants, with the drugs and side effects the handout lists.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Tricyclics (available since the 1950s): imipramine, desipramine, amitriptyline, nortriptyline, doxepin, trimipramine, protriptyline, clomipramine (the handout also lists maprotiline, mirtazapine, amoxapine). Effects: dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, dry nasal passages, blurred vision, orthostatic hypotension, sedation, weight gain, tachycardia, sexual dysfunction, agitation, delirium.\n- TCAs are potentially lethal in overdose. Depressed or impulsive clients get limited prescription amounts.\n- SSRIs: fluoxetine, fluvoxamine, paroxetine, sertraline, citalopram, escitalopram. They replaced TCAs as first choice (equal effect, fewer side effects) and help OCD. Effects: agitation, akathisia, nausea, insomnia, sexual dysfunction, less weight gain, sedation, sweating, headaches.\n- Care: nausea, take with food; insomnia, take doses in the morning and avoid alcohol; restlessness or tremor, ask for propranolol or a benzodiazepine; dry mouth, sugar-free options; weight gain, balanced diet; constipation, fluids and stool softener.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Overdose risk makes limited supplies the answer for a suicidal client. Insomnia from SSRI is a timing problem (morning dose).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Antidepressant Classes: TCA, SSRI and MAOI Drug Review]], [[MAOIs and Other Antidepressants: Tyramine Diet and Special Risks]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout says TCAs are the \"first choice\" and then says SSRIs replaced them; use SSRIs as first line.",

"body": "## What it is\nDisulfiram (Antabuse) is a sensitizing drug: alcohol in the body then causes an unpleasant reaction. It suits motivated, non-impulsive clients.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Reaction starts about 5 to 10 minutes after alcohol and may last 30 minutes to 2 hours: flushing, throbbing headache, sweating, dry mouth, nausea, vomiting, dizziness, weakness; severe cases bring chest pain, breathlessness, severe hypotension, confusion and death.\n- Drug side effects: fatigue, drowsiness, halitosis, tremor, impotence.\n- Hidden alcohol: shaving cream, aftershave, cologne, deodorant, cough preparations.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Teach to read labels and pick alcohol-free products.\n2. Treat severe hypotension as an emergency.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Safe use depends on the client avoiding all alcohol, including skin products.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Alcohol Use Disorder]], [[Recovery Programs and Drugs Used in Substance Use]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Verify timing against your text.",

"id": "np5-x-asd-ptsd",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Acute Stress Disorder versus Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder",
"body": "## What it is\nBoth follow extreme trauma; time course separates them.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Acute stress disorder: within 1 month, lasts 2 days to 4 weeks, with numbing, dazed feeling, derealization, depersonalization or amnesia.\n- PTSD: flashbacks and nightmares, avoidance, numbing, hyperarousal. Treatment: antianxiety drugs, antidepressants, group therapy.\n- Acute stress disorder needs exposure to a traumatic event with intense fear, helplessness or horror, marked anxiety or arousal, persistent re-experiencing, and at least three of: emotional numbing, feeling dazed, derealization, depersonalization, dissociative amnesia for part of the event.\n- PTSD adds avoidance of memory-provoking stimuli and numbing of general responsiveness, plus arousal: sleep disturbance, irritability or angry outbursts, poor concentration, hypervigilance, exaggerated startle.\n- Both cause significant distress or impaired functioning.\n- Management of PTSD in the handout: benzodiazepines (diazepam, oxazepam, chlordiazepoxide, clorazepate, alprazolam), antidepressants, group therapy.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety and suicide screening.\n2. Calm, predictable setting; let the client set the pace of talking.\n3. Ground the client during flashbacks (present place, date, safe).\n4. Teach relaxation; refer for trauma-focused therapy.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Under a month: acute stress; over: PTSD.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Trauma and Stressor-Related Disorders]], [[Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Cutoffs vary.",

"title": "Panic Disorder, Agoraphobia, Social Phobia and Generalized Anxiety Disorder",
"body": "## What it is\nHandout criteria for the main anxiety disorders.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Panic attack: sudden intense fear with impending doom; 15 to 30 minutes in the handout; four or more of palpitations, sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, choking, chest pain, nausea, derealization, fear of dying, paresthesias, chills or hot flashes. Panic disorder: recurrent unexpected attacks.\n- Agoraphobia: avoiding places from which escape is hard.\n- Social phobia: fear of embarrassment; medicine and social skills training.\n- GAD: six months or more of excessive worry plus restlessness, fatigue, poor concentration, irritability, muscle tension, sleep trouble. Buspirone and SSRIs.\n- Social phobia criteria: fear of embarrassment or inability to perform, avoidance or dreaded endurance, belief that others judge negatively, significant distress in relationships, work or social life; anxiety can reach panic level.\n- Agoraphobia signs: avoids being outside or at home alone, avoids travelling in vehicles, trouble with work and errands such as shopping or appointments.\n- GAD: apprehensive expectation more days than not about several events, worry that cannot be controlled, distress or impaired functioning, and at least three listed physical or mental symptoms.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Stay with the client during a panic attack; low stimulation, short directions.\n2. Give anxiolytics as ordered; teach breathing and relaxation afterward.\n3. Social phobia: social skills training; GAD: buspirone or SSRI teaching that benefit takes time.\n\n## Priority diagnosis\n- Ineffective individual coping.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Differentiate by trigger: unexpected attacks, panic; places, agoraphobia; performance, social; everything, GAD.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Verify attack duration.",

"body": "## What it is\nCare for anxious or fearful personality disorders.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Avoidant: support and reassurance; explore positive self-aspects.\n- Dependent: foster autonomy; problem solving and decisions; restructuring.\n- Obsessive-compulsive: negotiation, timely decisions, restructuring.\n- Avoidant: pervasive social discomfort and silence, low self-esteem, hypersensitivity to negative evaluation, fear of rejection, criticism, shame; aloof and feeling inferior.\n- Dependent: excessive need to be cared for, submissive and clinging, fear of separation.\n- Obsessive-compulsive: preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and control; handout examples of obsessions (fear of dirt, fear of burglary) and compulsions (hand washing, checking locks).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. "id": "np5-x-delusions",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Delusion Types and Thought Disturbances in Schizophrenia",
"body": "## What it is\nDelusions and thought changes in schizophrenia.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Persecutory, grandiose, religious, somatic, referential delusions with handout examples.\n- Thought broadcasting, insertion, blocking, alogia, clang associations.\n- Persecutory: others plan to harm, spy on or ridicule the client (poisoned food, bugged room).\n- Grandiose: association with famous people or belief of being famous or capable of great feats.\n- Religious: second coming, being the Messiah or a prophet, special mission.\n- Somatic: vague unrealistic beliefs about the body (pregnant male, worms in the brain).\n- Referential (ideas of reference): television, music or news carry special messages to him.\n- Thought broadcasting: others can hear or know his thoughts. Thought insertion (handout wording: others take thoughts away): client feels powerless to stop it.\n- Disorganized thought: inappropriate responses. Thought blocking: abrupt stop mid-sentence. Alogia: poverty of content. Clang associations: ideas linked by sound. Neologisms, echolalia, word salad also occur.\n- Hallucinations may be visual, olfactory, gustatory, tactile or auditory; perception may be heightened or blunted.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Assess safety, especially with persecutory or command content.\n2. Do not argue or agree; acknowledge the fear and present reality.\n3. Keep communication simple and give reality-based activity.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Do not argue; address the feeling.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Schizophrenia]], [[Disturbances in Perception and Thought]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Thought withdrawal is the textbook term for insertion's opposite; the handout describes it loosely.",

"title": "Stimulants: Amphetamine, Cocaine and Methamphetamine",
"body": "## What it is\nCNS stimulant misuse.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Intoxication: euphoria, hyperactivity, grandiosity, tachycardia, high BP, dilated pupils, seizures. Chlorpromazine controls hallucinations.\n- Withdrawal \"crash\": dysphoria, fatigue, hypersomnia, suicidal ideation; no drug.\n- Amphetamines (\"uppers\") were used for weight loss and staying awake. Cocaine has virtually no clinical use, is highly addictive and gives intense immediate euphoria. Methamphetamine is highly addictive, causes psychotic behavior and brain damage.\n- Intoxication also: hypervigilance, talkativeness, anxiety, stereotyped behavior, anger, fighting, poor judgment, anorexia, perspiration or chills, nausea, chest pain, confusion, dysrhythmias; overdose can cause seizures, coma and death.\n- Withdrawal begins hours to days after stopping and is not life-threatening: dysphoria, fatigue, vivid unpleasant dreams, insomnia or hypersomnia, increased appetite, psychomotor change.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Monitor cardiac rhythm, blood pressure, temperature; seizure precautions.\n2. Suicide precautions during the crash.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cardiac and seizure risk first; watch suicide.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Uppers and Hallucinogens]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Verify.",

"area": "NP5",
"title": "Intellectual Disability: Levels and Care",
"body": "## What it is\nIntellectual disability (older term: mental retardation) is below-average thinking ability together with limits in everyday adaptive skills, starting in childhood. Severity is graded by IQ and, more usefully, by how much support the person needs.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Moderate: IQ about 40-54, called trainable. Can learn vocational skills such as cooking or sewing.\n- Severe: IQ about 25-39, needs close supervision. Can be taught basic activities of daily living such as brushing teeth and dressing.\n- Profound: IQ below 25, needs custodial care. Totally dependent on others.\n- Mild (not in the source slice): usually called educable, able to learn reading and writing. The IQ band is commonly about 55-70; confirm in your textbook.\n- Education focus for the educable client: reading, writing and basic arithmetic.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Keep the person safe and meet basic needs first (feeding, hygiene, protection), especially at the severe and profound levels.\n2. Match teaching to the level of function, not the age. Break tasks into small steps.\n3. Use repetition: skills are learned by doing the same step many times.\n4. Use role modelling: show the skill, then let the client copy it.\n5. Restructure the environment (simple, consistent, labelled, free of hazards) rather than expecting the client to adapt.\n6. Praise every success; support the family.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the IQ band to the label (trainable, close supervision, custodial) and pick the teaching goal that fits the level. Trap: planning academic teaching for a severe or profound client.\n\n## Memory aid\nMade up: \"educable reads, trainable works, severe dresses, profound is carried.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Developmental Disorders]], [[General Psychiatric Disorders]], [[Therapeutic Communication]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The mild range and the exact IQ cut-offs differ by manual; newer manuals grade by adaptive function rather than IQ alone.",

"title": "Anxiolytic Drugs and Cognitive-Behavioral Techniques for Anxiety",
"body": "## What it is\nTwo treatment arms for anxiety disorders: antianxiety drugs (mainly benzodiazepines) and cognitive-behavioral techniques that change how the client thinks and acts.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Benzodiazepine examples in the handout: diazepam, oxazepam, chlordiazepoxide, clorazepate and alprazolam. Buspirone and SSRIs are used for generalized anxiety; antidepressants and group therapy also help.\n- Side effects: drowsiness, sedation, poor coordination, impaired memory and a clouded sensorium. This is why falls and driving matter.\n- Alcohol and caffeine interfere; benzodiazepines add to alcohol's depressant effect.\n- Cognitive-behavioral techniques:\n  - Positive reframing: turn a catastrophic thought (\"my heart is pounding, I will die\") into a coping thought (\"this is anxiety, it will pass\").\n  - Decatastrophizing: the therapist asks \"what is the worst that could happen, is it likely, could you survive it?\" to judge the situation realistically.\n  - Thought stopping: break the cycle of negative thoughts with a jolt, such as splashing the face with water or snapping a rubber band on the wrist.\n  - Assertiveness training: use \"I\" statements (\"I feel angry when you turn your back while I am talking\") to state needs and gain control.\n  - Systematic desensitization: gradual exposure to the feared object while relaxed, used for phobias.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first: sedation and unsteadiness mean fall precautions and no driving.\n2. Teach: avoid alcohol, caffeine and other sedating drugs; do not stop suddenly after long use.\n3. Teach the client to name a thought, challenge it and replace it.\n4. Priority diagnosis: ineffective individual coping.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nName the technique from a one-line example, and pick the teaching point about alcohol or sedation.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anxiety and Anxiety Disorders]], [[Phobic and Somatic Symptom Disorders]], [[Therapeutic Communication]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout says to give anxiolytics before meals because food delays absorption and calls them \"incompatible with any drugs\": both are over-simplified; confirm timing and interactions for each drug.",

"title": "Anorexia Nervosa and Bulimia Nervosa: Findings and Treatment",
"body": "## What it is\nA side-by-side look at the two classic eating disorders, with the signs, lab clues and treatment aims the exam likes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Anorexia nervosa: refusal or inability to keep a minimally normal weight (about 85 percent of expected), intense fear of fatness, distorted body image and denial of seriousness.\n- Anorexia findings: amenorrhea, cold intolerance, lethargy, emaciation, low blood pressure, temperature and pulse, constipation, abdominal pain, enlarged salivary glands, raised BUN, leukopenia, mild anemia and raised liver tests. Mood is low and the client withdraws socially.\n- Subtypes: restricting, and binge-purge (self-induced vomiting or misuse of laxatives, enemas and diuretics).\n- Bulimia nervosa: binge eating (large amount in a short period, about two hours or less) followed by compensation, at least twice a week for three months. Weight is usually normal.\n- Bulimia findings: tooth enamel loss and a ragged \"moth-eaten\" look, dental caries, esophageal tears, laxative dependence, menstrual irregularity, mildly raised amylase, metabolic alkalosis from vomiting and metabolic acidosis from diarrhea or laxatives. Alcohol and stimulant use are common.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Stabilize physical status: fluids, electrolytes, heart rate and rhythm (Maslow: physiologic first).\n2. Restore weight and nutrition with a set plan; rehydrate.\n3. Use a calm, matter-of-fact manner with positive expectations; behavior modification with clear rules.\n4. Support coping with anxiety and build self-esteem.\n5. Common diagnoses: body image disturbance, self-esteem disturbance, ineffective individual coping.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- The handout lists amitriptyline and cyproheptadine for weight gain and olanzapine for weight gain with an antipsychotic effect; antidepressants (desipramine, imipramine, nortriptyline, phenelzine) for bulimia; individual therapy.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTell anorexia from bulimia by weight and behavior, and choose the physiologic priority over body-image talk.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Eating Disorders]], [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Major Depressive Disorder]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Drug choices are old-fashioned: current texts favor SSRIs such as fluoxetine for bulimia, and no drug is first line for anorexia. "body": "## What it is\nParaphilias are patterns of sexual arousal to unusual objects, situations or people. They become a disorder when they cause distress or harm, or involve someone who cannot or does not consent.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Non-coercive (involve only the person or consenting activity):\n  - Fetishism: arousal from objects (shoes, leather, rubber) or body parts (feet, hair).\n  - Autoerotic asphyxia: neck constriction to heighten arousal; often ends in accidental death.\n  - Sexual masochism: arousal from receiving real or imagined pain or humiliation.\n  - Transvestic behavior: arousal from wearing clothing of the other sex.\n- Coercive (a victim is involved):\n  - Exhibitionism: exposing genitals to a stranger.\n  - Voyeurism: secretly watching an unaware person in a private act.\n  - Frotteurism: rubbing against a non-consenting person.\n  - Pedophilia: sexual interest in children.\n  - Sadism: arousal from inflicting pain on another.\n  - Urophilia and coprophilia: involving urine and feces with a partner.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Protect safety, including that of potential victims; know the legal reporting duties, especially for children.\n2. Keep a calm, nonjudgmental, professional manner and set clear limits.\n3. Use diversional activities and behavior modification.\n4. Refer for specialized therapy.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the term to the description (voyeurism versus exhibitionism versus frotteurism) and pick the nonjudgmental, limit-setting response.\n\n## Memory aid\nMade up: \"-ism of looking (voyeur), showing (exhibitionist), rubbing (frotteur).\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[General Psychiatric Disorders]], [[Personality Disorders by Cluster]], [[Behavioral Management, Restraints and De-escalation]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Current manuals separate unusual interests from paraphilic disorders; the handout labels (for example \"transvestitism\") are dated.",

"title": "Mood Disorder Theories and Variants",
"body": "## What it is\nMood disorders are lasting changes in emotion that show up as depression, mania or both. This note covers the theories of cause and the milder or special forms that sit around major depression and bipolar disorder.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Genetic: one parent with bipolar disorder gives about a 25 percent chance to the child (figure from the handout).\n- Aggression turned inward: harsh superego, anger directed at self. Object loss: early loss of a parent (before about age 11). Personality organization: obsessive, oral-dependent or hysterical styles carry more risk.\n- Cognitive: negative views of self, future and experience. Learned helplessness: belief that nothing one does matters.\n- Biologic: mania linked to high norepinephrine, depression to low.\n- Triggers: major life events, loss, fewer coping resources, drastic physical change.\n- Mania: elevated, expansive or irritable mood for about a week. Hypomania: at least 4 days, less severe, no psychosis and no major impairment. Mixed episode (handout also calls it rapid cycling; usually these are different, see check section): both poles nearly every day for a week.\n- Bipolar I: at least one manic or mixed episode. Bipolar II: major depression plus hypomania, never full mania.\n- Dysthymia: milder depression for at least 2 years. Cyclothymia: 2 years of hypomanic and depressive symptoms not meeting full criteria.\n- Substance-induced mood disorder: mood change caused directly by a drug or toxin.\n- Seasonal affective disorder: fall-winter type brings oversleeping, carbohydrate craving, weight gain; spring-summer type is rarer, with insomnia, poor appetite and weight loss.\n- Postpartum: blues (peak days 3-7, fade without treatment), postpartum depression (full depressive episode within weeks), postpartum psychosis (within about 3 weeks, confusion then delusions and hallucinations; an emergency).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety for suicide, harm to others and the newborn.\n2. Differentiate the episode from the description, then match the response.\n3. Treat sleep, nutrition and hydration.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPick Bipolar I versus II, hypomania versus mania, and blues versus psychosis from timing and severity.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Rapid cycling usually means 4 or more episodes in a year, not a mixed episode. Time criteria differ by manual.",

"title": "Lithium Therapy: Nursing Responsibilities",
"body": "## What it is\nLithium carbonate is the classic mood stabilizer. It lowers the frequency and depth of manic cycling. Its exact action is unknown; it crosses the blood-brain barrier and the placenta, so it is avoided in pregnancy.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Give with or after meals to reduce stomach upset.\n- Benefit takes 10 to 14 days to appear, so acute mania is often covered by another drug at first.\n- The handout lists a normal level of 0.5 to 1.5 mEq/L; toxicity signs include severe nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, muscle weakness and tremor.\n- Too little water or sodium (sweating, vomiting, diarrhea, low-salt diet) raises the lithium level and can cause toxicity. Too much water dilutes it.\n- Fluids: about 2 to 3 liters daily. Keep salt intake normal and steady.\n- Track daily weight, intake and output, and look for dependent edema.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Hold the dose and report if toxicity signs appear; this is urgent.\n2. Check blood levels as ordered.\n3. Teach steady fluid and salt intake, and to call the provider during vomiting, diarrhea or fever.\n4. Manic clients: protect from injury, limit stimulation, give high-calorie finger foods, set limits kindly, and assess for suicidal or violent thoughts.\n5. Promote rest with a bedtime routine and a quiet environment.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich statement shows the client understands lithium (steady salt and fluids, no stopping suddenly) and which sign needs a call.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Antidepressants and Mood Stabilizers]], [[Bipolar Disorder and Mania]], [[Mood Disorder Theories and Variants]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout writes \"salt intake 2-3 L/day\", which is a clear typo (liters apply to fluids).
