"id": "np5-pd-somatoform-gains-management",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Somatoform Disorders: Gains, Theories and Management",
"body": "## What it is\nPhysical symptoms with no organic cause that fully explains them, arising from psychological conflict and not under the client's conscious control. The distress is real.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Three central features: symptoms suggest a medical illness without a demonstrable cause; psychological factors start, worsen or maintain them; they are not consciously produced.\n- Somatization disorder: many symptoms beginning before age 30, lasting years: pain, gastrointestinal, sexual and pseudo-neurologic symptoms.\n- Conversion disorder: sudden loss of sensory or motor function (blindness, paralysis); la belle indifference (strangely unconcerned) may appear.\n- Pain disorder: pain, often not relieved by analgesics, strongly shaped by emotion.\n- Hypochondriasis: fear of having (or catching) a serious disease, from misreading body sensations. Body dysmorphic disorder: preoccupation with an imagined or exaggerated defect.\n- Mechanisms: internalization (keeping stress inside) and somatization (expressing it physically), both unconscious.\n- Primary gain: relief of anxiety or conflict by having symptoms. Secondary gain: benefits from others, such as attention.\n- Biologic view: amplified awareness of normal sensations.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Show empathy, accept complaints and build trust; this keeps the client with one provider rather than \"doctor shopping.\"\n2. Do not confront the origin of symptoms until the client has other coping tools.\n3. Limit time spent discussing symptoms and avoid reinforcing gains.\n4. Teach routine, nutrition, sleep, journaling and expression of feelings.\n5. Coping: emotion-focused (relaxation, deep breathing, guided imagery, distraction) and problem-focused (problem solving, role play).\n6. Pain: relaxation, imagery, physical therapy, non-steroidal pain relievers; avoid narcotics because of dependence risk.\n\n## Medications\n- SSRIs (fluoxetine, sertraline, paroxetine) mainly for accompanying depression.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nChoose primary versus secondary gain, and the non-confrontational, trust-building response.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Phobic and Somatic Symptom Disorders]], [[Freud's Theory and Defense Mechanisms]], [[Malingering, Factitious Disorder and Munchausen Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Newer terms: somatic symptom disorder and illness anxiety disorder replace several of these labels. Always rule out physical illness first.",

"title": "Malingering, Factitious Disorder and Munchausen Syndrome",
"body": "## What it is\nConditions where symptoms are deliberately produced or exaggerated. Unlike somatic disorders, the client is aware of the faking, but the reason differs.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Malingering: intentional false or exaggerated symptoms for an outside reward such as avoiding work, escaping prosecution, getting money or obtaining drugs. Symptoms stop when the goal is reached. Not a mental illness.\n- Factitious disorder: symptoms feigned or produced just to receive attention and the sick role.\n- Munchausen syndrome: a severe form where the person may injure himself or herself to gain care.\n- Munchausen by proxy: harming another person (often a child) to gain attention from medical staff or to be a \"hero\" for saving the victim. This is abuse.\n- Somatic disorders differ: symptoms are not consciously produced.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Always assess and treat real physical problems first.\n2. For suspected proxy abuse, protect the victim and report according to law and policy.\n3. Stay objective and nonjudgmental; document facts, not opinions.\n4. Refer to psychiatric care.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTell malingering (clear external gain) from factitious (attention) from somatic (unconscious).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Somatoform Disorders: Gains, Theories and Management]], [[Phobic and Somatic Symptom Disorders]], [[General Psychiatric Disorders]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Reporting duties for suspected abuse follow Philippine law and facility policy; verify locally.",

"body": "## What it is\nInhalants are chemicals breathed in for a brief high: gasoline, glue, paint thinner, spray paint, cleaners, correction fluid, spray propellants, solvents (esters, ketones, glycols), anesthetics and nitrates. They can badly damage the brain, nerves and liver.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Intoxication: dizziness, nystagmus, poor coordination, slurred speech, unsteady gait, tremor, weakness, blurred vision, aggression, apathy, poor judgment; stupor and coma may follow.\n- Acute toxicity: low oxygen, respiratory depression, vagal stimulation and rhythm problems; death can come from bronchospasm, cardiac arrest, suffocation or aspiration of vomit.\n- No antidote. Treatment is support of breathing and the heart until the substance clears.\n- No true withdrawal syndrome or detoxification. Long-term effects include persistent dementia and psychosis, anxiety or mood disorders.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing and circulation first; monitor cardiac rhythm.\n2. Later: counseling, family education, referral.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nKnow that there is no antidote or withdrawal treatment, and that support of breathing is the priority.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Substance Use Disorders: Basic Concepts]], [[Uppers and Hallucinogens]], [[Downers: Barbiturates and Opioids]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some texts describe mild withdrawal symptoms with heavy chronic use; confirm.",

"title": "Recovery Programs and Drugs Used in Substance Use",
"body": "## What it is\nLong-term help for alcohol and drug problems: peer groups, medications that deter use or ease withdrawal, and nursing education.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Alcoholics Anonymous: peer self-help from the 1930s; the 12-step model holds that total abstinence is essential and that support from others keeps sobriety. Closed meetings are for people seeking recovery; open meetings welcome anyone. Related: Narcotics Anonymous, Al-Anon (spouses, partners, friends) and Alateen (children of parents with substance problems).\n- Alcohol: thiamine (vitamin B1) to prevent or treat Wernicke and Korsakoff syndromes; vitamin B12 and folic acid for deficiency; disulfiram deters drinking: alcohol then causes flushing, throbbing headache, sweating, nausea, vomiting, severe hypotension, confusion, coma and possible death.\n- Opioids: methadone, a long-acting opioid substitute that meets physical need without cravings for more; levomethadyl (analgesic only for opioid dependence); naltrexone blocks opioid effects; clonidine eases autonomic withdrawal signs (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea); ondansetron treats nausea.\n- Opioid overdose is treated with naloxone (see [[Downers: Barbiturates and Opioids]]).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. "id": "np5-pd-dissociative-disorders",
"area": "NP5",
"title": "Dissociative Disorders and Grounding Care",
"body": "## What it is\nDissociation is an unconscious defense that protects a person from the full pain of a horrific event by splitting off memory, identity or awareness. Dissociative disorders disrupt the normal link between consciousness, memory, identity and perception of surroundings.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Dissociative amnesia: cannot recall important personal information, usually stressful or traumatic.\n- Dissociative fugue: sudden leaving of home or work, travel, inability to recall past or identity, perhaps a new identity.\n- Dissociative identity disorder (formerly multiple personality): two or more distinct identities that take control, with memory gaps.\n- Depersonalization disorder: persistent detachment from one's mind or body, but reality testing stays intact; the client is not psychotic.\n\n## Assessment findings\n- Hyperalert, startles at small noises, uncomfortable when someone stands close, pacing or curled up with arms around the knees.\n- Frightened, may cry, scream or hide. Self-destructive thoughts, possible suicidal ideation.\n- Memory gaps, low self-esteem (feels bad or deserving of abuse), trouble trusting.\n- Nightmares, sleep trouble, overeating or poor appetite, alcohol or drug use to blot out memories.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Assess suicide and self-harm risk first.\n2. Teach the client to go to a safe place during destructive urges and wait for them to pass.\n3. Use grounding: remind the client they are in the present, an adult and safe; have them stand and walk, focus on sensory information, breathe deeply and relax.\n4. Encourage written feelings at set times and positive distraction (exercise, music, talking, hobbies).\n5. See the client as a survivor, not a victim.\n\n## Treatment\n- SSRIs such as paroxetine or sertraline; group, individual and cognitive-behavioral therapy aimed at re-association.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the type to the description and choose grounding and safety actions.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Trauma and Stressor-Related Disorders]], [[General Psychiatric Disorders]], [[Disturbances in Affect and Memory]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The assessment list in the source blends trauma and dissociation signs; fugue is now a specifier of amnesia in current manuals.",
